. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One.

Aṅguttaranikāyo

The Numerical Discourses

Navakanipātapāḷi

The Book of the Nines

1. Paṭhamapaṇṇāsakaṃ

1. The First Fifty

1. Sambodhivaggo

1. The Chapter on Enlightenment

1. Sambodhisuttaṃ

1. The Discourse on Enlightenment

1. Evaṃ [T 4.0363][M 3.0163][V 3.0171][P 4.0351] me sutaṃ – ekaṃ samayaṃ bhagavā sāvatthiyaṃ viharati jetavane anāthapiṇḍikassa ārāme. Tatra kho bhagavā bhikkhū āmantesi –

1. Thus have I heard. At one time the Blessed One was dwelling in Sāvatthī at Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍika's monastery. There, the Blessed One addressed the bhikkhus:

“Sace, bhikkhave, aññatitthiyā paribbājakā evaṃ puccheyyuṃ – ‘sambodhipakkhikānaṃ sambodhapakkhikānaṃ (sī. syā. pī.), āvuso, dhammānaṃ kā upanisā bhāvanāyā’ti, evaṃ puṭṭhā tumhe, bhikkhave, tesaṃ aññatitthiyānaṃ [V 3.0172] paribbājakānaṃ kinti byākareyyāthā”ti? “Bhagavaṃmūlakā no, bhante, dhammā…pe… bhagavato sutvā bhikkhū dhāressantī”ti.

“Bhikkhus, if wanderers of other sects should ask you: ‘Friends, what is the supporting condition for the development of the qualities that partake of enlightenment?’—being asked thus, bhikkhus, how would you answer those wanderers of other sects?” “Venerable sir, our teachings are rooted in the Blessed One… having heard it from the Blessed One, the bhikkhus will bear it in mind.”

“Tena hi, bhikkhave, suṇātha, sādhukaṃ manasi karotha; bhāsissāmī”ti. “Evaṃ, bhante”ti kho te bhikkhū bhagavato paccassosuṃ. Bhagavā etadavoca –

“In that case, bhikkhus, listen and pay close attention. I will speak.” “Yes, venerable sir,” those bhikkhus replied to the Blessed One. The Blessed One said this:

“Sace, bhikkhave, aññatitthiyā paribbājakā evaṃ puccheyyuṃ – ‘sambodhipakkhikānaṃ, āvuso, dhammānaṃ kā upanisā bhāvanāyā’ti, evaṃ puṭṭhā [M 3.0164] tumhe, bhikkhave, tesaṃ aññatitthiyānaṃ paribbājakānaṃ evaṃ byākareyyātha [T 4.0364] –

“Bhikkhus, if wanderers of other sects should ask you: ‘Friends, what is the supporting condition for the development of the qualities that partake of enlightenment?’—being asked thus, bhikkhus, you should answer those wanderers of other sects in this way:

“Idhāvuso, bhikkhu kalyāṇamitto hoti kalyāṇasahāyo kalyāṇasampavaṅko[P 4.0352]. Sambodhipakkhikānaṃ, āvuso, dhammānaṃ ayaṃ paṭhamā upanisā bhāvanāya.

“‘Here, friends, a bhikkhu has admirable friends, admirable companions, and is inclined towards the admirable. This, friends, is the first supporting condition for the development of the qualities that partake of enlightenment.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sīlavā hoti, pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuto viharati ācāragocarasampanno aṇumattesu vajjesu bhayadassāvī, samādāya sikkhati sikkhāpadesu. Sambodhipakkhikānaṃ, āvuso, dhammānaṃ ayaṃ dutiyā upanisā bhāvanāya.

“‘Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu is virtuous. He dwells restrained by the Pātimokkha restraint, accomplished in conduct and resort, seeing danger in the slightest faults. He undertakes and trains in the training rules. This, friends, is the second supporting condition for the development of the qualities that partake of enlightenment.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu yāyaṃ kathā abhisallekhikā cetovivaraṇasappāyā, seyyathidaṃ – appicchakathā santuṭṭhikathā pavivekakathā asaṃsaggakathā vīriyārambhakathā sīlakathā samādhikathā paññākathā vimuttikathā vimuttiñāṇadassanakathā, evarūpiyā kathāya nikāmalābhī hoti akicchalābhī akasiralābhī. Sambodhipakkhikānaṃ, āvuso, dhammānaṃ ayaṃ tatiyā upanisā bhāvanāya.

“‘Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu obtains at will, without difficulty and without trouble, talk that is conducive to effacement and suitable for opening up the mind, that is: talk on fewness of wishes, talk on contentment, talk on seclusion, talk on non-association, talk on arousing energy, talk on virtue, talk on concentration, talk on wisdom, talk on liberation, talk on the knowledge and vision of liberation. This, friends, is the third supporting condition for the development of the qualities that partake of enlightenment.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu āraddhavīriyo viharati akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya, kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ upasampadāya, thāmavā daḷhaparakkamo anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu. Sambodhipakkhikānaṃ, āvuso, dhammānaṃ ayaṃ catutthī upanisā bhāvanāya.

“‘Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu dwells with energy aroused for the abandoning of unwholesome qualities and for the attainment of wholesome qualities; he is strong, firm in his effort, not shirking the burden in regard to wholesome qualities. This, friends, is the fourth supporting condition for the development of the qualities that partake of enlightenment.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu paññavā hoti udayatthagāminiyā paññāya samannāgato ariyāya [V 3.0173] nibbedhikāya sammā dukkhakkhayagāminiyā. Sambodhipakkhikānaṃ, āvuso, dhammānaṃ ayaṃ pañcamī upanisā bhāvanāya”.

“‘Furthermore, friends, a bhikkhu is wise, endowed with wisdom that discerns arising and passing away, which is noble, penetrative, and leads to the right destruction of suffering. This, friends, is the fifth supporting condition for the development of the qualities that partake of enlightenment.’”

“Kalyāṇamittassetaṃ[T 4.0365], bhikkhave, bhikkhuno pāṭikaṅkhaṃ kalyāṇasahāyassa kalyāṇasampavaṅkassa – sīlavā bhavissati, pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuto viharissati ācāragocarasampanno aṇumattesu vajjesu bhayadassāvī, samādāya sikkhissati sikkhāpadesu.

“Bhikkhus, this is to be expected of a bhikkhu who has admirable friends, admirable companions, and is inclined towards the admirable: he will be virtuous, will dwell restrained by the Pātimokkha restraint, accomplished in conduct and resort, seeing danger in the slightest faults. He will undertake and train in the training rules.

“Kalyāṇamittassetaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno pāṭikaṅkhaṃ kalyāṇasahāyassa kalyāṇasampavaṅkassa – yāyaṃ kathā abhisallekhikā cetovivaraṇasappāyā, seyyathidaṃ – appicchakathā santuṭṭhikathā pavivekakathā [M 3.0165] asaṃsaggakathā vīriyārambhakathā sīlakathā samādhikathā paññākathā vimuttikathā vimuttiñāṇadassanakathā, evarūpiyā kathāya nikāmalābhī bhavissati akicchalābhī akasiralābhī.

“Bhikkhus, this is to be expected of a bhikkhu who has admirable friends, admirable companions, and is inclined towards the admirable: he will obtain at will, without difficulty and without trouble, talk that is conducive to effacement and suitable for opening up the mind, that is: talk on fewness of wishes, talk on contentment, talk on seclusion, talk on non-association, talk on arousing energy, talk on virtue, talk on concentration, talk on wisdom, talk on liberation, talk on the knowledge and vision of liberation.

“Kalyāṇamittassetaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno pāṭikaṅkhaṃ kalyāṇasahāyassa kalyāṇasampavaṅkassa – āraddhavīriyo viharissati [P 4.0353] akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya, kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ upasampadāya, thāmavā daḷhaparakkamo anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu.

“Bhikkhus, this is to be expected of a bhikkhu who has admirable friends, admirable companions, and is inclined towards the admirable: he will dwell with energy aroused for the abandoning of unwholesome qualities and for the attainment of wholesome qualities; he will be strong, firm in his effort, not shirking the burden in regard to wholesome qualities.

“Kalyāṇamittassetaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno pāṭikaṅkhaṃ kalyāṇasahāyassa kalyāṇasampavaṅkassa – paññavā bhavissati udayatthagāminiyā paññāya samannāgato ariyāya nibbedhikāya sammā dukkhakkhayagāminiyā.

“Bhikkhus, this is to be expected of a bhikkhu who has admirable friends, admirable companions, and is inclined towards the admirable: he will be wise, endowed with wisdom that discerns arising and passing away, which is noble, penetrative, and leads to the right destruction of suffering.

“Tena ca pana, bhikkhave, bhikkhunā imesu pañcasu dhammesu patiṭṭhāya cattāro dhammā uttari uttariṃ (sī. syā. pī.) bhāvetabbā – asubhā bhāvetabbā rāgassa pahānāya, mettā bhāvetabbā byāpādassa pahānāya, ānāpānassati ānāpānasati (sī. pī.) bhāvetabbā vitakkupacchedāya, aniccasaññā bhāvetabbā asmimānasamugghātāya. Aniccasaññino, bhikkhave, anattasaññā saṇṭhāti. Anattasaññī asmimānasamugghātaṃ pāpuṇāti diṭṭheva dhamme nibbāna”nti[T 4.0366]. Paṭhamaṃ.

“Moreover, bhikkhus, that bhikkhu, being established in these five qualities, should further develop four qualities. He should develop the perception of foulness for abandoning lust. He should develop loving-kindness for abandoning ill will. He should develop mindfulness of breathing for cutting off thoughts. He should develop the perception of impermanence for uprooting the conceit ‘I am.’ For one who perceives impermanence, bhikkhus, the perception of not-self is established. One who perceives not-self attains the uprooting of the conceit ‘I am’—Nibbāna in this very life.” The First.

2. Nissayasuttaṃ

2. The Discourse on Support

2. Atha [V 3.0174] kho aññataro bhikkhu yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ…pe… ekamantaṃ nisinno kho so bhikkhu bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – “‘nissayasampanno nissayasampanno’ti, bhante, vuccati. Kittāvatā nu kho, bhante, bhikkhu nissayasampanno hotī”ti? “Saddhaṃ ce, bhikkhu, bhikkhu nissāya akusalaṃ pajahati kusalaṃ bhāveti, pahīnamevassa taṃ akusalaṃ hoti. Hiriṃ ce, bhikkhu, bhikkhu nissāya…pe… ottappaṃ ce, bhikkhu, bhikkhu nissāya…pe… vīriyaṃ ce, bhikkhu, bhikkhu nissāya…pe… paññaṃ ce, bhikkhu, bhikkhu nissāya akusalaṃ pajahati kusalaṃ bhāveti, pahīnamevassa taṃ akusalaṃ hoti[P 4.0354]. Taṃ hissa bhikkhuno akusalaṃ pahīnaṃ hoti suppahīnaṃ, yaṃsa ariyāya paññāya disvā pahīnaṃ”.

2. Then a certain monk went to the Blessed One, approached, paid homage, and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, that monk said to the Blessed One: “Venerable sir, it is said, ‘one endowed with support, one endowed with support.’ To what extent, venerable sir, is a monk endowed with support?” “If, monk, a monk, relying on faith, abandons the unwholesome and develops the wholesome, that unwholesome quality is indeed abandoned by him. If, monk, a monk, relying on conscience… if, monk, a monk, relying on moral dread… if, monk, a monk, relying on energy… if, monk, a monk, relying on wisdom, abandons the unwholesome and develops the wholesome, that unwholesome quality is indeed abandoned by him. For that unwholesome quality of that monk is abandoned, well abandoned, which is abandoned after having been seen with noble wisdom.”

“Tena [M 3.0166] ca pana, bhikkhu, bhikkhunā imesu pañcasu dhammesu patiṭṭhāya cattāro upanissāya vihātabbā. Katame cattāro? Idha, bhikkhu, bhikkhu saṅkhāyekaṃ paṭisevati, saṅkhāyekaṃ adhivāseti, saṅkhāyekaṃ parivajjeti, saṅkhāyekaṃ vinodeti. Evaṃ kho, bhikkhu, bhikkhu nissayasampanno hotī”ti. Dutiyaṃ.

“Furthermore, monk, that monk, being established in these five things, should dwell relying on four things. What four? Here, monk, a monk makes use of one thing after reflection, endures one thing after reflection, avoids one thing after reflection, and dispels one thing after reflection. In this way, monk, a monk is endowed with support.” Second.

3. Meghiyasuttaṃ

3. The Discourse concerning Meghiya

3. Ekaṃ samayaṃ bhagavā cālikāyaṃ viharati cālikāpabbate. Tena kho pana samayena āyasmā meghiyo bhagavato upaṭṭhāko hoti. Atha kho āyasmā meghiyo yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ aṭṭhāsi. Ekamantaṃ ṭhito kho āyasmā meghiyo bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – “icchāmahaṃ, bhante, jantugāmaṃ jatugāmaṃ (sī. aṭṭha., syā. aṭṭha.), jattugāmaṃ (ka. aṭṭhakathāyampi pāṭhantaraṃ) piṇḍāya [T 4.0367] pavisitu”nti. “Yassa dāni tvaṃ, meghiya, kālaṃ maññasī”ti.

3. At one time the Blessed One was dwelling at Cālikā on Mount Cālikā. Now at that time, the Venerable Meghiya was the Blessed One’s attendant. Then the Venerable Meghiya went to the Blessed One; having gone, he paid homage to the Blessed One and stood to one side. Standing to one side, the Venerable Meghiya said to the Blessed One: “Venerable sir, I wish to enter Jantugāma for alms.” “Now, Meghiya, is the time for that of which you think it is the time.”

Atha kho āyasmā meghiyo pubbaṇhasamayaṃ nivāsetvā pattacīvaramādāya jantugāmaṃ piṇḍāya pāvisi. Jantugāme piṇḍāya caritvā pacchābhattaṃ piṇḍapātapaṭikkanto yena kimikāḷāya nadiyā tīraṃ tenupasaṅkami. Addasā kho āyasmā meghiyo kimikāḷāya nadiyā [V 3.0175] tīre jaṅghāvihāraṃ jaṅghavihāraṃ (syā. ka.) anucaṅkamamāno anuvicaramāno ambavanaṃ [P 4.0355] pāsādikaṃ ramaṇīyaṃ. Disvānassa etadahosi – “pāsādikaṃ vatidaṃ ambavanaṃ ramaṇīyaṃ, alaṃ vatidaṃ kulaputtassa padhānatthikassa padhānāya. Sace maṃ bhagavā anujāneyya, āgaccheyyāhaṃ imaṃ ambavanaṃ padhānāyā”ti.

Then the Venerable Meghiya, in the morning, having dressed, took his bowl and robe and entered Jantugāma for alms. Having wandered for alms in Jantugāma and, after his meal, having returned from his alms round, he went to the bank of the Kimikālā River. While walking back and forth and wandering for exercise on the bank of the Kimikālā River, the Venerable Meghiya saw an inspiring and delightful mango grove. Seeing it, this thought occurred to him: “This mango grove is indeed inspiring and delightful, suitable for a clansman who wishes to strive, for the purpose of striving. If the Blessed One were to permit me, I would come to this mango grove for striving.”

Atha kho āyasmā meghiyo yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinno kho āyasmā meghiyo bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – “idhāhaṃ, bhante, pubbaṇhasamayaṃ nivāsetvā pattacīvaramādāya jantugāmaṃ piṇḍāya pāvisiṃ. Jantugāme piṇḍāya caritvā pacchābhattaṃ piṇḍapātapaṭikkanto yena kimikāḷāya nadiyā tīraṃ tenupasaṅkamiṃ. Addasaṃ kho ahaṃ, bhante, kimikāḷāya nadiyā tīre jaṅghāvihāraṃ anucaṅkamamāno anuvicaramāno ambavanaṃ pāsādikaṃ ramaṇīyaṃ. Disvāna me etadahosi – ‘pāsādikaṃ vatidaṃ ambavanaṃ ramaṇīyaṃ. Alaṃ vatidaṃ kulaputtassa padhānatthikassa padhānāya. Sace [M 3.0167] maṃ bhagavā anujāneyya, āgaccheyyāhaṃ imaṃ ambavanaṃ padhānāyā’ti. Sace maṃ bhagavā anujāneyya, gaccheyyāhaṃ taṃ ambavanaṃ padhānāyā”ti. “Āgamehi tāva, meghiya[T 4.0368]! Ekakamhi ekakamhā (sī. pī.) tāva vata (ka.) yāva aññopi koci bhikkhu āgacchatī”ti dissatūti (sabbattha, ṭīkāyampi pāṭhantaraṃ), āgacchatūti, dissatīti (ṭīkāyaṃ pāṭhantarāni).

Then the Venerable Meghiya went to the Blessed One; having gone, he paid homage to the Blessed One and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the Venerable Meghiya said to the Blessed One: “Here, venerable sir, this morning I dressed, took my bowl and robe, and entered Jantugāma for alms. Having wandered for alms in Jantugāma and returned from the alms round after the meal, I went to the bank of the Kimikālā River. I saw, venerable sir, while walking back and forth and wandering for exercise on the bank of the Kimikālā River, an inspiring and delightful mango grove. Seeing it, this thought occurred to me: ‘This mango grove is indeed inspiring and delightful. It is suitable for a clansman who wishes to strive, for the purpose of striving. If the Blessed One were to permit me, I would come to this mango grove for striving.’ If the Blessed One would permit me, I would go to that mango grove for striving.” “Wait for a while, Meghiya! I am alone for now. Wait until some other monk comes.”

Dutiyampi kho āyasmā meghiyo bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – “bhagavato, bhante, natthi kiñci uttari karaṇīyaṃ, natthi katassa paṭicayo. Mayhaṃ kho pana, bhante, atthi uttari karaṇīyaṃ, atthi katassa paṭicayo. Sace maṃ bhagavā anujāneyya, gaccheyyāhaṃ taṃ ambavanaṃ padhānāyā”ti. “Āgamehi tāva, meghiya, ekakamhi tāva yāva aññopi koci bhikkhu āgacchatī”ti.

For a second time, the Venerable Meghiya said to the Blessed One: “Venerable sir, for the Blessed One there is nothing further to be done, nor is there any augmenting of what has been done. But for me, venerable sir, there is something further to be done, and there is augmenting of what has been done. If the Blessed One were to permit me, I would go to that mango grove for striving.” “Wait for a while, Meghiya! I am alone for now. Wait until some other monk comes.”

Tatiyampi [P 4.0356] kho āyasmā meghiyo bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – “bhagavato, bhante, natthi kiñci uttari karaṇīyaṃ, natthi katassa paṭicayo. Mayhaṃ kho pana, bhante, atthi uttari karaṇīyaṃ, atthi katassa paṭicayo. Sace maṃ bhagavā anujāneyya, gaccheyyāhaṃ taṃ ambavanaṃ padhānāyā”ti. “Padhānanti kho, meghiya, vadamānaṃ kinti vadeyyāma! Yassa dāni tvaṃ, meghiya, kālaṃ maññasī”ti.

For a third time, the Venerable Meghiya said to the Blessed One: “Venerable sir, for the Blessed One there is nothing further to be done, nor is there any augmenting of what has been done. But for me, venerable sir, there is something further to be done, and there is augmenting of what has been done. If the Blessed One were to permit me, I would go to that mango grove for striving.” “When you speak of striving, Meghiya, what can we say? Now, Meghiya, is the time for that of which you think it is the time.”

Atha [V 3.0176] kho āyasmā meghiyo uṭṭhāyāsanā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā padakkhiṇaṃ katvā yena taṃ ambavanaṃ tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā taṃ ambavanaṃ ajjhogāhetvā aññatarasmiṃ rukkhamūle divāvihāraṃ nisīdi. Atha kho āyasmato meghiyassa tasmiṃ ambavane viharantassa yebhuyyena tayo pāpakā akusalā vitakkā samudācaranti, seyyathidaṃ – kāmavitakko, byāpādavitakko, vihiṃsāvitakko. Atha kho āyasmato meghiyassa etadahosi – “acchariyaṃ vata bho, abbhutaṃ vata bho! Saddhāya ca vatamhā agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajitā; atha ca panimehi tīhi pāpakehi akusalehi vitakkehi anvāsattā – kāmavitakkena, byāpādavitakkena, vihiṃsāvitakkenā”ti.

Then the Venerable Meghiya rose from his seat, paid homage to the Blessed One, circumambulated him keeping him to the right, and went to that mango grove. Having approached the mango grove and entered it, he sat down at the foot of a certain tree for the day's abiding. While the Venerable Meghiya was dwelling in that mango grove, for the most part three kinds of evil, unwholesome thoughts arose in him—namely, sensual thought, thought of ill will, and thought of harming. Then it occurred to the Venerable Meghiya: “It is astonishing, sirs, it is amazing, sirs! Indeed, out of faith we have gone forth from the household life into homelessness, yet we are beset by these three evil, unwholesome thoughts—sensual thought, thought of ill will, and thought of harming.”

Atha [T 4.0369] kho āyasmā meghiyo yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinno kho āyasmā meghiyo bhagavantaṃ etadavoca –

Then the Venerable Meghiya approached the Blessed One; having approached, he paid homage to the Blessed One and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the Venerable Meghiya said this to the Blessed One:

“Idha [M 3.0168] mayhaṃ, bhante, tasmiṃ ambavane viharantassa yebhuyyena tayo pāpakā akusalā vitakkā samudācaranti, seyyathidaṃ – kāmavitakko, byāpādavitakko, vihiṃsāvitakko. Tassa mayhaṃ, bhante, etadahosi – ‘acchariyaṃ vata bho, abbhutaṃ vata bho! Saddhāya ca vatamhā agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajitā; atha ca panimehi [P 4.0357] tīhi pāpakehi akusalehi vitakkehi anvāsattā – kāmavitakkena, byāpādavitakkena, vihiṃsāvitakkenāti’”.

“Venerable sir, while I was dwelling in that mango grove, for the most part these three evil, unwholesome thoughts arose in me: sensual thought, thought of ill will, and thought of harming. Then, venerable sir, this occurred to me: ‘It is astonishing, sirs, it is amazing, sirs! Indeed, out of faith we have gone forth from the household life into homelessness, yet we are beset by these three evil, unwholesome thoughts: sensual thought, thought of ill will, and thought of harming.’”

“Aparipakkāya, meghiya, cetovimuttiyā pañca dhammā paripakkāya saṃvattanti. Katame pañca? Idha, meghiya, bhikkhu kalyāṇamitto hoti kalyāṇasahāyo kalyāṇasampavaṅko. Aparipakkāya, meghiya, cetovimuttiyā ayaṃ paṭhamo dhammo paripakkāya saṃvattati.

“Meghiya, for the liberation of mind that is still immature, five qualities conduce to its ripening. What five? Here, Meghiya, a bhikkhu has good friends, good companions, and good associates. For the liberation of mind that is still immature, Meghiya, this is the first quality that conduces to its ripening.

“Puna caparaṃ, meghiya, bhikkhu sīlavā hoti, pātimokkhasaṃvarasaṃvuto viharati ācāragocarasampanno aṇumattesu vajjesu bhayadassāvī, samādāya sikkhati sikkhāpadesu. Aparipakkāya, meghiya, cetovimuttiyā ayaṃ dutiyo dhammo paripakkāya saṃvattati.

“Furthermore, Meghiya, a bhikkhu is virtuous. He dwells restrained by the Pātimokkha restraint, is endowed with good conduct and resort, and sees danger in the slightest faults. He undertakes and trains in the training rules. For the liberation of mind that is still immature, Meghiya, this is the second quality that conduces to its ripening.

“Puna caparaṃ, meghiya, yāyaṃ kathā abhisallekhikā cetovivaraṇasappāyā, seyyathidaṃ – appicchakathā [V 3.0177] santuṭṭhikathā pavivekakathā asaṃsaggakathā [T 4.0370] vīriyārambhakathā sīlakathā samādhikathā paññākathā vimuttikathā vimuttiñāṇadassanakathā, evarūpiyā kathāya nikāmalābhī hoti akicchalābhī akasiralābhī. Aparipakkāya, meghiya, cetovimuttiyā ayaṃ tatiyo dhammo paripakkāya saṃvattati.

“Furthermore, Meghiya, a bhikkhu obtains at will, without difficulty, and without trouble, talk that is effacing and conducive to the opening of the mind, that is to say: talk on fewness of wishes, talk on contentment, talk on seclusion, talk on non-association, talk on arousing energy, talk on virtue, talk on concentration, talk on wisdom, talk on liberation, and talk on the knowledge and vision of liberation. For the liberation of mind that is still immature, Meghiya, this is the third quality that conduces to its ripening.

“Puna caparaṃ, meghiya, bhikkhu āraddhavīriyo viharati akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya, kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ upasampadāya, thāmavā daḷhaparakkamo anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu. Aparipakkāya, meghiya, cetovimuttiyā ayaṃ catuttho dhammo paripakkāya saṃvattati.

“Furthermore, Meghiya, a bhikkhu dwells with aroused energy for abandoning unwholesome qualities and for acquiring wholesome qualities; he is strong, firm in his striving, not shirking the burden in regard to wholesome qualities. For the liberation of mind that is still immature, Meghiya, this is the fourth quality that conduces to its ripening.

“Puna caparaṃ, meghiya, bhikkhu paññavā hoti udayatthagāminiyā paññāya samannāgato ariyāya nibbedhikāya sammā dukkhakkhayagāminiyā. Aparipakkāya, meghiya, cetovimuttiyā ayaṃ pañcamo dhammo paripakkāya saṃvattati.

“Furthermore, Meghiya, a bhikkhu is wise, endowed with wisdom that discerns rise and fall, which is noble, penetrative, and leads rightly to the complete destruction of suffering. For the liberation of mind that is still immature, Meghiya, this is the fifth quality that conduces to its ripening.

“Kalyāṇamittassetaṃ[M 3.0169], meghiya, bhikkhuno pāṭikaṅkhaṃ kalyāṇasahāyassa kalyāṇasampavaṅkassa – ‘sīlavā bhavissati…pe. [P 4.0358]… samādāya sikkhissati sikkhāpadesu’”.

“It is to be expected, Meghiya, of a bhikkhu who has good friends, good companions, and good associates, that he will be virtuous... and will undertake and train in the training rules.

“Kalyāṇamittassetaṃ, meghiya, bhikkhuno pāṭikaṅkhaṃ kalyāṇasahāyassa kalyāṇasampavaṅkassa – ‘yāyaṃ kathā abhisallekhikā cetovivaraṇasappāyā, seyyathidaṃ – appicchakathā…pe… vimuttiñāṇadassanakathā, evarūpiyā kathāya nikāmalābhī bhavissati akicchalābhī akasiralābhī’”.

“It is to be expected, Meghiya, of a bhikkhu who has good friends, good companions, and good associates, that he will obtain at will, without difficulty, and without trouble, talk that is effacing and conducive to the opening of the mind, that is to say: talk on fewness of wishes… on the knowledge and vision of liberation.

“Kalyāṇamittassetaṃ, meghiya, bhikkhuno pāṭikaṅkhaṃ kalyāṇasahāyassa kalyāṇasampavaṅkassa – ‘āraddhavīriyo viharissati…pe… anikkhittadhuro kusalesu dhammesu’”.

“It is to be expected, Meghiya, of a bhikkhu who has good friends, good companions, and good associates, that he will dwell with aroused energy... not shirking the burden in regard to wholesome qualities.

“Kalyāṇamittassetaṃ[T 4.0371], meghiya, bhikkhuno pāṭikaṅkhaṃ kalyāṇasahāyassa kalyāṇasampavaṅkassa – ‘paññavā bhavissati…pe… sammādukkhakkhayagāminiyā’”.

“It is to be expected, Meghiya, of a bhikkhu who has good friends, good companions, and good associates, that he will be wise... endowed with wisdom that leads rightly to the complete destruction of suffering.

“Tena [V 3.0178] ca pana, meghiya, bhikkhunā imesu pañcasu dhammesu patiṭṭhāya cattāro dhammā uttari bhāvetabbā – asubhā bhāvetabbā rāgassa pahānāya, mettā bhāvetabbā byāpādassa pahānāya, ānāpānassati bhāvetabbā vitakkupacchedāya, aniccasaññā bhāvetabbā asmimānasamugghātāya. Aniccasaññino, meghiya, anattasaññā saṇṭhāti. Anattasaññī asmimānasamugghātaṃ pāpuṇāti diṭṭheva dhamme nibbāna”nti. Tatiyaṃ.

“Then, Meghiya, that bhikkhu, having established himself in these five qualities, should further develop four qualities: the perception of unattractiveness should be developed to abandon lust, loving-kindness should be developed to abandon ill will, mindfulness of breathing should be developed to cut off thoughts, and the perception of impermanence should be developed to uproot the conceit ‘I am.’ For one who perceives impermanence, Meghiya, the perception of not-self is established. One who perceives not-self attains the uprooting of the conceit ‘I am’ and Nibbāna in this very life.” The Third.

4. Nandakasuttaṃ

4. 4. The Discourse to Nandaka

4. Ekaṃ samayaṃ bhagavā sāvatthiyaṃ viharati jetavane anāthapiṇḍikassa ārāme. Tena kho pana samayena āyasmā nandako upaṭṭhānasālāyaṃ bhikkhū dhammiyā kathāya sandasseti samādapeti samuttejeti sampahaṃseti. Atha kho bhagavā sāyanhasamayaṃ paṭisallānā vuṭṭhito yenupaṭṭhānasālā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bahidvārakoṭṭhake aṭṭhāsi kathāpariyosānaṃ āgamayamāno. Atha kho bhagavā kathāpariyosānaṃ viditvā ukkāsetvā aggaḷaṃ [P 4.0359] ākoṭesi. Vivariṃsu kho te bhikkhū bhagavato dvāraṃ.

4. At one time the Blessed One was dwelling at Sāvatthī in Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍika's Park. At that time the Venerable Nandaka was instructing, urging, rousing, and encouraging the monks in the attendance hall with a talk concerning the Dhamma. Then, in the evening, the Blessed One, having arisen from seclusion, went to the attendance hall; having gone there, he stood outside the gateway, waiting for the talk to conclude. Then, the Blessed One, knowing the talk had concluded, cleared his throat and knocked on the door. Those monks opened the door for the Blessed One.

Atha [M 3.0170] kho bhagavā upaṭṭhānasālaṃ pavisitvā paññattāsane nisīdi. Nisajja kho bhagavā āyasmantaṃ nandakaṃ etadavoca – “dīgho kho tyāyaṃ, nandaka, dhammapariyāyo bhikkhūnaṃ paṭibhāsi. Api me piṭṭhi āgilāyati bahidvārakoṭṭhake [T 4.0372] ṭhitassa kathāpariyosānaṃ āgamayamānassā”ti.

Then the Blessed One entered the attendance hall and sat down on the prepared seat. Having sat down, the Blessed One said this to the Venerable Nandaka: 'Long indeed, Nandaka, was this discourse on the Dhamma that you delivered to the monks. Moreover, my back is aching from standing outside the gateway, waiting for the talk to conclude.'

Evaṃ vutte āyasmā nandako sārajjamānarūpo bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – “na kho pana mayaṃ, bhante, jānāma ‘bhagavā bahidvārakoṭṭhake ṭhito’ti. Sace hi mayaṃ, bhante, jāneyyāma ‘bhagavā bahidvārakoṭṭhake ṭhito’ti, ettakampi ( ) (dhammaṃ) katthaci no nappaṭibhāseyyā”ti.

When this was said, the Venerable Nandaka, appearing abashed, said this to the Blessed One: 'Indeed, Venerable Sir, we did not know that the Blessed One was standing outside the gateway. For if we had known, Venerable Sir, that the Blessed One was standing outside the gateway, not even this much would have occurred to us.'

Atha kho bhagavā āyasmantaṃ nandakaṃ sārajjamānarūpaṃ viditvā āyasmantaṃ nandakaṃ etadavoca – “sādhu, sādhu, nandaka! Etaṃ kho, nandaka, tumhākaṃ patirūpaṃ kulaputtānaṃ saddhāya agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajitānaṃ, yaṃ tumhe dhammiyā kathāya sannisīdeyyātha. Sannipatitānaṃ vo, nandaka, dvayaṃ karaṇīyaṃ – dhammī vā kathā ariyo vā tuṇhībhāvo. a. ni. 8.71; 9.1 Saddho ca, nandaka, bhikkhu [V 3.0179] hoti, no ca sīlavā. Evaṃ so tenaṅgena aparipūro hoti. Tena taṃ aṅgaṃ paripūretabbaṃ – ‘kintāhaṃ saddho ca assaṃ [P 4.0360] sīlavā cā’ti. Yato ca kho, nandaka, bhikkhu saddho ca hoti sīlavā ca, evaṃ so tenaṅgena paripūro hoti.

Then the Blessed One, knowing that the Venerable Nandaka was abashed, said this to the Venerable Nandaka: 'Good, good, Nandaka! It is fitting for you clansmen who have gone forth from the household life into homelessness out of faith that you should sit together for a talk concerning the Dhamma. When you have assembled, Nandaka, two things are to be done: either a talk on the Dhamma or noble silence. Nandaka, a monk may have faith but not be virtuous. In that case, he is incomplete in that factor. That factor should be fulfilled by him, reflecting: "How might I be one who has faith and is also virtuous?" But when, Nandaka, a monk has faith and is also virtuous, in that case, he is complete in that factor.'

“Saddho ca, nandaka, bhikkhu hoti sīlavā ca, no ca lābhī ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa. Evaṃ so tenaṅgena aparipūro hoti. Tena taṃ aṅgaṃ paripūretabbaṃ – ‘kintāhaṃ saddho ca assaṃ sīlavā ca lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissā’ti. Yato ca kho, nandaka, bhikkhu saddho ca hoti sīlavā ca lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa, evaṃ so tenaṅgena paripūro hoti.

Nandaka, a monk may have faith and be virtuous, but not be an obtainer of internal serenity of mind. In that case, he is incomplete in that factor. That factor should be fulfilled by him, reflecting: "How might I be one who has faith, is virtuous, and is also an obtainer of internal serenity of mind?" But when, Nandaka, a monk has faith, is virtuous, and is an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, in that case, he is complete in that factor.

“Saddho ca, nandaka, bhikkhu hoti sīlavā ca lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa, na lābhī adhipaññādhammavipassanāya. Evaṃ so tenaṅgena [T 4.0373] aparipūro hoti. Seyyathāpi, nandaka, pāṇako catuppādako assa. Tassa eko pādo omako lāmako. Evaṃ so tenaṅgena aparipūro assa. Evamevaṃ kho, nandaka, bhikkhu saddho ca hoti sīlavā ca lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa, na lābhī adhipaññādhammavipassanāya. Evaṃ [M 3.0171] so tenaṅgena aparipūro hoti. Tena taṃ aṅgaṃ paripūretabbaṃ – ‘kintāhaṃ saddho ca assaṃ sīlavā ca lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa lābhī ca adhipaññādhammavipassanāyā’”ti.

Nandaka, a monk may have faith, be virtuous, and be an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, but not be an obtainer of the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena. In that case, he is incomplete in that factor. Just as, Nandaka, a four-footed creature might have one foot that is lame and inferior, and in that case would be incomplete in that factor; in the very same way, Nandaka, a monk may have faith, be virtuous, and be an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, but not be an obtainer of the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena. In that case, he is incomplete in that factor. That factor should be fulfilled by him, reflecting: "How might I be one who has faith, is virtuous, an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, and also an obtainer of the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena?"

“Yato ca kho, nandaka, bhikkhu saddho ca hoti sīlavā ca lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa lābhī ca adhipaññādhammavipassanāya, evaṃ so tenaṅgena paripūro hotī”ti. Idamavoca bhagavā. Idaṃ vatvāna sugato uṭṭhāyāsanā vihāraṃ pāvisi.

But when, Nandaka, a monk has faith, is virtuous, is an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, and is an obtainer of the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena, in that case, he is complete in that factor.' The Blessed One said this. Having said this, the Sugata rose from his seat and entered his dwelling.

Atha kho āyasmā nandako acirapakkantassa bhagavato bhikkhū āmantesi – “idāni, āvuso, bhagavā catūhi padehi kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ pakāsetvā [P 4.0361] uṭṭhāyāsanā vihāraṃ paviṭṭho – ‘saddho ca, nandaka, bhikkhu hoti, no ca sīlavā. Evaṃ so tenaṅgena aparipūro hoti. Tena taṃ aṅgaṃ paripūretabbaṃ – kintāhaṃ saddho ca assaṃ sīlavā cā’ti. Yato ca kho nandaka bhikkhu saddho ca hoti sīlavā ca, evaṃ so tenaṅgena paripūro hoti. Saddho ca nandaka bhikkhu hoti sīlavā ca, no ca lābhī ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa…pe… lābhī [V 3.0180] ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa, na lābhī adhipaññādhammavipassanāya, evaṃ so tenaṅgena aparipūro [T 4.0374] hoti. Seyyathāpi nandaka pāṇako catuppādako assa, tassa eko pādo omako lāmako, evaṃ so tenaṅgena aparipūro assa. Evamevaṃ kho, nandaka, bhikkhu saddho ca hoti sīlavā ca, lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa, na lābhī adhipaññādhammavipassanāya, evaṃ so tenaṅgena aparipūro hoti, tena taṃ aṅgaṃ paripūretabbaṃ ‘kintāhaṃ saddho ca assaṃ sīlavā ca, lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa, lābhī ca adhipaññādhammavipassanāyā’ti. Yato ca kho, nandaka, bhikkhu saddho ca hoti sīlavā ca lābhī ca ajjhattaṃ cetosamādhissa lābhī ca adhipaññādhammavipassanāya, evaṃ so tenaṅgena paripūro hotī”ti.

Then, not long after the Blessed One had departed, the Venerable Nandaka addressed the monks: 'Friends, just now the Blessed One, having declared in four statements the holy life that is utterly complete and purified, rose from his seat and entered his dwelling, saying: "Nandaka, a monk may have faith but not be virtuous. In that case, he is incomplete in that factor. That factor should be fulfilled by him, reflecting: ‘How might I be one who has faith and is also virtuous?’ But when, Nandaka, a monk has faith and is also virtuous, in that case, he is complete in that factor. A monk may have faith and be virtuous, but not be an obtainer of internal serenity of mind... and he may be an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, but not be an obtainer of the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena. In that case, he is incomplete in that factor. Just as, Nandaka, a four-footed creature might have one foot that is lame and inferior, and in that case would be incomplete in that factor; in the very same way, Nandaka, a monk may have faith, be virtuous, and be an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, but not be an obtainer of the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena. In that case, he is incomplete in that factor. That factor should be fulfilled by him, reflecting: ‘How might I be one who has faith, is virtuous, an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, and also an obtainer of the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena?’ But when, Nandaka, a monk has faith, is virtuous, is an obtainer of internal serenity of mind, and is an obtainer of the higher wisdom of insight into phenomena, in that case, he is complete in that factor."

“Pañcime, āvuso, ānisaṃsā kālena dhammassavane kālena dhammasākacchāya. Katame pañca? Idhāvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ majjhekalyāṇaṃ pariyosānakalyāṇaṃ sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ, kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti. Yathā yathā, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ majjhekalyāṇaṃ pariyosānakalyāṇaṃ sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ, kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti[M 3.0172], tathā tathā so satthu piyo ca hoti manāpo ca garu ca bhāvanīyo ca. Ayaṃ, āvuso, paṭhamo ānisaṃso kālena dhammassavane kālena dhammasākacchāya.

Friends, there are these five benefits of listening to the Dhamma at the right time and discussing the Dhamma at the right time. What five? Here, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning, good in the middle, good in the end, with meaning and phrasing; he reveals the holy life that is entirely complete and perfectly pure. In whatever way, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning, good in the middle, good in the end, with meaning and phrasing, and reveals the holy life that is entirely complete and perfectly pure—in that same way he becomes dear and agreeable to the Teacher, respected and esteemed. This, friends, is the first benefit of listening to the Dhamma at the right time and discussing the Dhamma at the right time.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ majjhekalyāṇaṃ pariyosānakalyāṇaṃ sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ, kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ [T 4.0375] brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti. Yathā yathā, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ…pe… brahmacariyaṃ [P 4.0362] pakāseti, tathā tathā so tasmiṃ dhamme atthappaṭisaṃvedī ca hoti dhammappaṭisaṃvedī ca. Ayaṃ, āvuso, dutiyo ānisaṃso kālena dhammassavane kālena dhammasākacchāya.

Furthermore, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning, good in the middle, good in the end, with meaning and phrasing; he reveals the holy life that is entirely complete and perfectly pure. In whatever way, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning… and so on… and reveals the holy life, in that same way he becomes one who understands the meaning and one who understands the Dhamma in that Dhamma. This, friends, is the second benefit of listening to the Dhamma at the right time and discussing the Dhamma at the right time.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ majjhekalyāṇaṃ pariyosānakalyāṇaṃ sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ, kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti. Yathā yathā, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ…pe… brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti, tathā tathā so tasmiṃ dhamme gambhīraṃ atthapadaṃ paññāya ativijjha passati. Ayaṃ, āvuso, tatiyo ānisaṃso kālena dhammassavane kālena dhammasākacchāya.

Furthermore, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning, good in the middle, good in the end, with meaning and phrasing; he reveals the holy life that is entirely complete and perfectly pure. In whatever way, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning… and so on… and reveals the holy life, in that same way he penetrates and sees with wisdom a profound point of meaning in that Dhamma. This, friends, is the third benefit of listening to the Dhamma at the right time and discussing the Dhamma at the right time.

“Puna [V 3.0181] caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ…pe… brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti. Yathā yathā, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ…pe… brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti, tathā tathā naṃ sabrahmacārī uttari sambhāventi – ‘addhā ayamāyasmā patto vā pajjati vā’. Ayaṃ, āvuso, catuttho ānisaṃso kālena dhammassavane kālena dhammasākacchāya.

Furthermore, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning… and so on… and reveals the holy life. In whatever way, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning… and so on… and reveals the holy life, in that same way his companions in the holy life esteem him highly, thinking: ‘Surely this venerable one has attained or is on the way to attaining.’ This, friends, is the fourth benefit of listening to the Dhamma at the right time and discussing the Dhamma at the right time.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ majjhekalyāṇaṃ pariyosānakalyāṇaṃ sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ, kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti. Yathā yathā, āvuso, bhikkhu bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti ādikalyāṇaṃ majjhekalyāṇaṃ pariyosānakalyāṇaṃ sātthaṃ sabyañjanaṃ[T 4.0376], kevalaparipuṇṇaṃ parisuddhaṃ brahmacariyaṃ pakāseti, tattha ye kho bhikkhū sekhā appattamānasā anuttaraṃ yogakkhemaṃ patthayamānā viharanti, te taṃ dhammaṃ sutvā vīriyaṃ ārabhanti appattassa pattiyā anadhigatassa [M 3.0173] adhigamāya asacchikatassa sacchikiriyāya. Ye pana tattha bhikkhū arahanto khīṇāsavā vusitavanto katakaraṇīyā ohitabhārā anuppattasadatthā parikkhīṇabhavasaṃyojanā sammadaññāvimuttā, te taṃ dhammaṃ sutvā diṭṭhadhammasukhavihāraṃyeva [P 4.0363] anuyuttā viharanti. Ayaṃ, āvuso, pañcamo ānisaṃso kālena dhammassavane kālena dhammasākacchāya. Ime kho, āvuso, pañca ānisaṃsā kālena dhammassavane kālena dhammasākacchāyā”ti. Catutthaṃ.

Furthermore, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning, good in the middle, good in the end, with meaning and phrasing; he reveals the holy life that is entirely complete and perfectly pure. In whatever way, friends, a monk teaches the Dhamma to the monks—good in the beginning, good in the middle, good in the end, with meaning and phrasing, and reveals the holy life that is entirely complete and perfectly pure—there, those monks who are trainees, who have not attained the goal, who dwell aspiring for the unsurpassed security from bondage, upon hearing that Dhamma, rouse their energy for the attainment of the unattained, for the achievement of the unachieved, for the realization of the unrealized. As for those monks who are arahants, whose taints are destroyed, who have lived the holy life, done what was to be done, laid down the burden, attained their own goal, whose fetters of existence are utterly destroyed, and who are liberated through right knowledge—upon hearing that Dhamma, they dwell devoted only to pleasant abiding in the here and now. This, friends, is the fifth benefit of listening to the Dhamma at the right time and discussing the Dhamma at the right time. These, friends, are the five benefits of listening to the Dhamma at the right time and discussing the Dhamma at the right time.

5. Balasuttaṃ

5. The Discourse on Powers

5. “Cattārimāni, bhikkhave, balāni. Katamāni cattāri? Paññābalaṃ, vīriyabalaṃ, anavajjabalaṃ, saṅgāhabalaṃ. Katamañca, bhikkhave, paññābalaṃ? Ye dhammā kusalā kusalasaṅkhātā ye dhammā akusalā akusalasaṅkhātā ye dhammā sāvajjā sāvajjasaṅkhātā ye dhammā anavajjā anavajjasaṅkhātā ye dhammā kaṇhā kaṇhasaṅkhātā ye dhammā sukkā sukkasaṅkhātā ye dhammā sevitabbā sevitabbasaṅkhātā ye dhammā asevitabbā asevitabbasaṅkhātā ye dhammā nālamariyā nālamariyasaṅkhātā ye dhammā alamariyā alamariyasaṅkhātā, tyāssa dhammā paññāya vodiṭṭhā honti vocaritā. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, paññābalaṃ.

5. Bhikkhus, there are these four powers. What four? The power of wisdom, the power of energy, the power of faultlessness, and the power of beneficence. And what, bhikkhus, is the power of wisdom? Those states that are wholesome and known as wholesome; those states that are unwholesome and known as unwholesome; those states that are blameworthy and known as blameworthy; those states that are blameless and known as blameless; those states that are dark and known as dark; those states that are bright and known as bright; those states that are to be cultivated and known as to be cultivated; those states that are not to be cultivated and known as not to be cultivated; those states that are unfit for the noble ones and known as unfit for the noble ones; those states that are fit for the noble ones and known as fit for the noble ones—for that person, these states are well seen, well investigated with wisdom. This, bhikkhus, is called the power of wisdom.

“Katamañca[T 4.0377][V 3.0182], bhikkhave, vīriyabalaṃ? Ye dhammā akusalā akusalasaṅkhātā ye dhammā sāvajjā sāvajjasaṅkhātā ye dhammā kaṇhā kaṇhasaṅkhātā ye dhammā asevitabbā asevitabbasaṅkhātā ye dhammā nālamariyā nālamariyasaṅkhātā, tesaṃ dhammānaṃ pahānāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati. Ye dhammā kusalā kusalasaṅkhātā ye dhammā anavajjā anavajjasaṅkhātā ye dhammā sukkā sukkasaṅkhātā ye dhammā sevitabbā sevitabbasaṅkhātā [P 4.0364] ye dhammā alamariyā alamariyasaṅkhātā, tesaṃ dhammānaṃ paṭilābhāya chandaṃ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṃ ārabhati cittaṃ paggaṇhāti padahati. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, vīriyabalaṃ.

And what, bhikkhus, is the power of energy? Regarding those states that are unwholesome and known as unwholesome; those states that are blameworthy and known as blameworthy; those states that are dark and known as dark; those states that are not to be cultivated and known as not to be cultivated; those states that are unfit for the noble ones and known as unfit for the noble ones—for the abandoning of these states, one generates desire, endeavors, arouses energy, exerts the mind, and strives. Regarding those states that are wholesome and known as wholesome; those states that are blameless and known as blameless; those states that are bright and known as bright; those states that are to be cultivated and known as to be cultivated; those states that are fit for the noble ones and known as fit for the noble ones—for the attainment of these states, one generates desire, endeavors, arouses energy, exerts the mind, and strives. This, bhikkhus, is called the power of energy.

“Katamañca, bhikkhave, anavajjabalaṃ? Idha, bhikkhave, ariyasāvako anavajjena kāyakammena samannāgato hoti, anavajjena vacīkammena samannāgato hoti, anavajjena manokammena samannāgato hoti. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, anavajjabalaṃ.

“And what, Bhikkhus, is the power of faultlessness? Here, Bhikkhus, a noble disciple is endowed with faultless bodily action, is endowed with faultless verbal action, and is endowed with faultless mental action. This, Bhikkhus, is called the power of faultlessness.”

“Katamañca[M 3.0174], bhikkhave, saṅgāhabalaṃ? Cattārimāni, bhikkhave, saṅgahavatthūni – dānaṃ, peyyavajjaṃ, atthacariyā, samānattatā. Etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, dānānaṃ yadidaṃ dhammadānaṃ. Etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, peyyavajjānaṃ yadidaṃ atthikassa ohitasotassa punappunaṃ dhammaṃ deseti. Etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, atthacariyānaṃ yadidaṃ assaddhaṃ saddhāsampadāya samādapeti niveseti patiṭṭhāpeti, dussīlaṃ sīlasampadāya… pe… macchariṃ cāgasampadāya…pe… duppaññaṃ paññāsampadāya samādapeti niveseti patiṭṭhāpeti. Etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, samānattatānaṃ yadidaṃ [T 4.0378] sotāpanno sotāpannassa samānatto, sakadāgāmī sakadāgāmissa samānatto, anāgāmī anāgāmissa samānatto, arahā arahato samānatto. Idaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, saṅgāhabalaṃ. Imāni kho, bhikkhave, cattāri balāni.

“And what, Bhikkhus, is the power of beneficence? There are, Bhikkhus, these four grounds for beneficence: giving, endearing speech, beneficial conduct, and impartiality. Of gifts, Bhikkhus, this is the highest: the gift of the Dhamma. Of endearing speeches, Bhikkhus, this is the highest: to teach the Dhamma again and again to one who is willing and lends an ear. Of beneficial conducts, Bhikkhus, this is the highest: to encourage, settle, and establish one who is faithless in the accomplishment of faith; one who is immoral in the accomplishment of virtue; one who is stingy in the accomplishment of generosity; and one who is unwise in the accomplishment of wisdom. Of impartiality, Bhikkhus, this is the highest: that a stream-enterer is an equal to a stream-enterer, a once-returner to a once-returner, a non-returner to a non-returner, and an arahant to an arahant. This, Bhikkhus, is called the power of beneficence. These, indeed, Bhikkhus, are the four powers.”

“Imehi kho, bhikkhave, catūhi balehi samannāgato ariyasāvako pañca bhayāni samatikkanto hoti. Katamāni pañca? Ājīvikabhayaṃ, asilokabhayaṃ, parisasārajjabhayaṃ, maraṇabhayaṃ[P 4.0365], duggatibhayaṃ. Sa kho so, bhikkhave, ariyasāvako iti paṭisañcikkhati – ‘nāhaṃ ājīvikabhayassa bhāyāmi. Kissāhaṃ ājīvikabhayassa bhāyissāmi? Atthi me cattāri balāni – paññābalaṃ, vīriyabalaṃ, anavajjabalaṃ, saṅgāhabalaṃ. Duppañño kho ājīvikabhayassa [V 3.0183] bhāyeyya. Kusīto ājīvikabhayassa bhāyeyya. Sāvajjakāyakammantavacīkammantamanokammanto ājīvikabhayassa bhāyeyya. Asaṅgāhako ājīvikabhayassa bhāyeyya. Nāhaṃ asilokabhayassa bhāyāmi…pe… nāhaṃ parisasārajjabhayassa bhāyāmi…pe… nāhaṃ maraṇabhayassa bhāyāmi…pe… nāhaṃ duggatibhayassa bhāyāmi. Kissāhaṃ duggatibhayassa bhāyissāmi? Atthi me cattāri balāni – paññābalaṃ, vīriyabalaṃ, anavajjabalaṃ, saṅgāhabalaṃ. Duppañño kho duggatibhayassa bhāyeyya. Kusīto duggatibhayassa bhāyeyya. Sāvajjakāyakammantavacīkammantamanokammanto duggatibhayassa bhāyeyya. Asaṅgāhako duggatibhayassa bhāyeyya. Imehi kho, bhikkhave, catūhi balehi samannāgato ariyasāvako imāni pañca bhayāni samatikkanto hotī”ti. Pañcamaṃ.

“Indeed, Bhikkhus, a noble disciple endowed with these four powers has overcome five fears. What five? The fear regarding livelihood, the fear of ill repute, the fear of diffidence in assemblies, the fear of death, and the fear of a woeful destination. That noble disciple, Bhikkhus, reflects thus: ‘I do not fear the fear regarding livelihood. Why should I fear the fear regarding livelihood? I have these four powers: the power of wisdom, the power of energy, the power of faultlessness, and the power of beneficence. One without wisdom might fear the fear regarding livelihood. A lazy person might fear the fear regarding livelihood. One with blameworthy bodily, verbal, and mental actions might fear the fear regarding livelihood. One who is not beneficent might fear the fear regarding livelihood. I do not fear the fear of ill repute… I do not fear the fear of diffidence in assemblies… I do not fear the fear of death… I do not fear the fear of a woeful destination. Why should I fear the fear of a woeful destination? I have these four powers: the power of wisdom, the power of energy, the power of faultlessness, and the power of beneficence. One without wisdom might fear the fear of a woeful destination. A lazy person might fear the fear of a woeful destination. One with blameworthy bodily, verbal, and mental actions might fear the fear of a woeful destination. One who is not beneficent might fear the fear of a woeful destination.’ Indeed, Bhikkhus, a noble disciple endowed with these four powers has overcome these five fears.” The fifth.

6. Sevanāsuttaṃ

6. The Discourse on Association

6. Tatra [T 4.0379] kho āyasmā sāriputto bhikkhū āmantesi…pe… āyasmā sāriputto etadavoca –

6. There, the Venerable Sāriputta addressed the bhikkhus… The Venerable Sāriputta said this:

“Puggalopi[M 3.0175], āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopi. Cīvarampi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbaṃ – sevitabbampi asevitabbampi. Piṇḍapātopi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopi. Senāsanampi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbaṃ – sevitabbampi asevitabbampi. Gāmanigamopi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi [P 4.0366] asevitabbopi. Janapadapadesopi āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopi.

“Friends, a person, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be associated with and as one not to be associated with. Friends, a robe, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be used and as one not to be used. Friends, alms-food, too, should be understood in two ways: as that to be used and as that not to be used. Friends, a lodging, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be used and as one not to be used. Friends, a village or town, too, should be understood in two ways: as a place to be frequented and as a place not to be frequented. Friends, a country or region, too, should be understood in two ways: as a place to be frequented and as a place not to be frequented.”

“‘Puggalopi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Tattha yaṃ jaññā puggalaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me puggalaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti, kusalā dhammā parihāyanti; ye ca kho me pabbajitena jīvitaparikkhārā samudānetabbā cīvarapiṇḍapātasenāsanagilānapaccayabhesajjaparikkhārā te ca kasirena samudāgacchanti; yassa camhi atthāya agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajito so ca me sāmaññattho [V 3.0184] na bhāvanāpāripūriṃ gacchatī’ti, tenāvuso, puggalena so puggalo rattibhāgaṃ vā divasabhāgaṃ vā saṅkhāpi anāpucchā pakkamitabbaṃ nānubandhitabbo.

“‘Friends, a person, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be associated with and as one not to be associated with.’ So it was said. With reference to what was this said? Here, one should know of a person: ‘By associating with this person, my unwholesome qualities increase and my wholesome qualities decline; the life-requisites that are to be obtained by one who has gone forth—robes, alms-food, lodging, and medicinal provisions for the sick—are obtained with difficulty; and that purpose of recluseship for which I went forth from the household life into homelessness does not reach fulfillment through development.’ Therefore, friends, having reflected, one should depart from that person by night or by day without asking leave, and should not continue to associate with them.

“Tattha yaṃ jaññā puggalaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me puggalaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti, kusalā dhammā parihāyanti; ye ca kho me pabbajitena jīvitaparikkhārā samudānetabbā cīvarapiṇḍapātasenāsanagilānapaccayabhesajjaparikkhārā te [T 4.0380] ca appakasirena samudāgacchanti; yassa camhi atthāya agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajito so ca me sāmaññattho na bhāvanāpāripūriṃ gacchatī’ti, tenāvuso, puggalena so puggalo saṅkhāpi anāpucchā pakkamitabbaṃ nānubandhitabbo.

“Here, one should know of a person: ‘By associating with this person, my unwholesome qualities increase and my wholesome qualities decline; the life-requisites that are to be obtained by one who has gone forth—robes, alms-food, lodging, and medicinal provisions for the sick—are obtained without difficulty; yet that purpose of recluseship for which I went forth from the household life into homelessness does not reach fulfillment through development.’ Therefore, friends, having reflected, one should depart from that person without asking leave, and should not continue to associate with them.”

“Tattha yaṃ jaññā puggalaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me puggalaṃ sevato akusalā [P 4.0367] dhammā parihāyanti, kusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti; ye ca kho me pabbajitena jīvitaparikkhārā samudānetabbā cīvarapiṇḍapātasenāsanagilānapaccayabhesajjaparikkhārā te ca kasirena samudāgacchanti; yassa camhi atthāya agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajito so ca me sāmaññattho bhāvanāpāripūriṃ gacchatī’ti, tenāvuso, puggalena so puggalo saṅkhāpi anubandhitabbo na pakkamitabbaṃ.

Here, should one know of a person: ‘By associating with this person, my unwholesome qualities decline and my wholesome qualities increase; the life-requisites that are to be obtained by one who has gone forth—robes, alms-food, lodging, and medicinal provisions for the sick—are obtained with difficulty; but that purpose of recluseship for which I went forth from the household life into homelessness reaches fulfillment through development.’ Therefore, friends, having reflected, one should continue to associate with that person; one should not depart.

“Tattha [M 3.0176] yaṃ jaññā puggalaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me puggalaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā parihāyanti, kusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti; ye ca kho me pabbajitena jīvitaparikkhārā samudānetabbā cīvarapiṇḍapātasenāsanagilānapaccayabhesajjaparikkhārā te ca appakasirena samudāgacchanti; yassa camhi atthāya agārasmā anagāriyaṃ pabbajito so ca me sāmaññattho bhāvanāpāripūriṃ gacchatī’ti, tenāvuso, puggalena so puggalo yāvajīvaṃ anubandhitabbo na pakkamitabbaṃ api panujjamānena paṇujjamānena (?). ‘Puggalopi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

Here, should one know of a person: ‘By associating with this person, my unwholesome qualities decline and my wholesome qualities increase; the life-requisites that are to be obtained by one who has gone forth—robes, alms-food, lodging, and medicinal provisions for the sick—are obtained without difficulty; and that purpose of recluseship for which I went forth from the household life into homelessness reaches fulfillment through development.’ Therefore, friends, one should continue to associate with that person for life and should not depart, even if being driven away. ‘Friends, a person, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be associated with and as one not to be associated with.’ That which was said was said in reference to this.

“‘Cīvarampi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbaṃ – sevitabbampi asevitabbampī’ti, iti [T 4.0381] kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Tattha yaṃ jaññā cīvaraṃ – ‘idaṃ kho me cīvaraṃ sevato akusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti, kusalā dhammā parihāyantī’ti, evarūpaṃ cīvaraṃ na sevitabbaṃ[V 3.0185]. Tattha yaṃ jaññā cīvaraṃ – ‘idaṃ kho me cīvaraṃ sevato akusalā dhammā parihāyanti, kusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhantī’ti, evarūpaṃ cīvaraṃ sevitabbaṃ. ‘Cīvarampi[P 4.0368], āvuso, duvidhena veditabbaṃ – sevitabbampi asevitabbampī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

‘Friends, a robe, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be used and as one not to be used.’ So it was said. With reference to what was this said? Here, should one know of a robe: ‘By using this robe, my unwholesome qualities increase and my wholesome qualities decline,’ such a robe is not to be used. Here, should one know of a robe: ‘By using this robe, my unwholesome qualities decline and my wholesome qualities increase,’ such a robe is to be used. ‘Friends, a robe, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be used and as one not to be used.’ That which was said was said in reference to this.

“‘Piṇḍapātopi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Tattha yaṃ jaññā piṇḍapātaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me piṇḍapātaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti, kusalā dhammā parihāyantī’ti, evarūpo piṇḍapāto na sevitabbo. Tattha yaṃ jaññā piṇḍapātaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me piṇḍapātaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā parihāyanti, kusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhantī’ti, evarūpo piṇḍapāto sevitabbo. ‘Piṇḍapātopi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

‘Friends, alms-food, too, should be understood in two ways: as that to be used and as that not to be used.’ So it was said. With reference to what was this said? Here, should one know of alms-food: ‘By using this alms-food, my unwholesome qualities increase and my wholesome qualities decline,’ such alms-food is not to be used. Here, should one know of alms-food: ‘By using this alms-food, my unwholesome qualities decline and my wholesome qualities increase,’ such alms-food is to be used. ‘Friends, alms-food, too, should be understood in two ways: as that to be used and as that not to be used.’ That which was said was said in reference to this.

“‘Senāsanampi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbaṃ – sevitabbampi asevitabbampī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Tattha yaṃ jaññā senāsanaṃ – “idaṃ kho me senāsanaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti, kusalā dhammā parihāyantī’ti, evarūpaṃ senāsanaṃ na [T 4.0382] sevitabbaṃ. Tattha yaṃ jaññā senāsanaṃ – ‘idaṃ kho me senāsanaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā parihāyanti[M 3.0177], kusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhantī’ti, evarūpaṃ senāsanaṃ sevitabbaṃ. ‘Senāsanampi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbaṃ – sevitabbampi asevitabbampī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

‘Friends, a lodging, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be resorted to and as one not to be resorted to.’ So it was said. With reference to what was this said? Here, should one know of a lodging: ‘By resorting to this lodging, my unwholesome qualities increase and my wholesome qualities decline,’ such a lodging is not to be resorted to. Here, should one know of a lodging: ‘By resorting to this lodging, my unwholesome qualities decline and my wholesome qualities increase,’ such a lodging is to be resorted to. ‘Friends, a lodging, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be resorted to and as one not to be resorted to.’ That which was said was said in reference to this.

“‘Gāmanigamopi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Tattha yaṃ jaññā gāmanigamaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me gāmanigamaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti, kusalā dhammā parihāyantī’ti, evarūpo gāmanigamo na sevitabbo. Tattha yaṃ jaññā gāmanigamaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho, me gāmanigamaṃ [P 4.0369] sevato akusalā dhammā parihāyanti, kusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhantī’ti, evarūpo gāmanigamo sevitabbo. ‘Gāmanigamopi, āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

‘Friends, a village or market town, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be resorted to and as one not to be resorted to.’ So it was said. With reference to what was this said? Here, should one know of a village or market town: ‘By resorting to this village or market town, my unwholesome qualities increase and my wholesome qualities decline,’ such a village or market town is not to be resorted to. Here, should one know of a village or market town: ‘By resorting to this village or market town, my unwholesome qualities decline and my wholesome qualities increase,’ such a village or market town is to be resorted to. ‘Friends, a village or market town, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be resorted to and as one not to be resorted to.’ That which was said was said in reference to this.

“‘Janapadapadesopi[V 3.0186], āvuso, duvidhena veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Tattha yaṃ jaññā janapadapadesaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me janapadapadesaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhanti, kusalā dhammā parihāyantī’ti, evarūpo janapadapadeso na sevitabbo. Tattha yaṃ jaññā janapadapadesaṃ – ‘imaṃ kho me janapadapadesaṃ sevato akusalā dhammā parihāyanti, kusalā dhammā abhivaḍḍhantī’ti, evarūpo janapadapadeso sevitabbo. ‘Janapadapadesopi, āvuso, duvidhena [T 4.0383] veditabbo – sevitabbopi asevitabbopī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vutta”nti. Chaṭṭhaṃ.

‘Friends, a region of the country, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be resorted to and as one not to be resorted to.’ So it was said. With reference to what was this said? Here, should one know of a region of the country: ‘By resorting to this region of the country, my unwholesome qualities increase and my wholesome qualities decline,’ such a region of the country is not to be resorted to. Here, should one know of a region of the country: ‘By resorting to this region of the country, my unwholesome qualities decline and my wholesome qualities increase,’ such a region of the country is to be resorted to. ‘Friends, a region of the country, too, should be understood in two ways: as one to be resorted to and as one not to be resorted to.’ That which was said was said in reference to this. The Sixth.

7. Sutavāsuttaṃ

7. The Sutavā Sutta

7. Ekaṃ samayaṃ bhagavā rājagahe viharati gijjhakūṭe pabbate. Atha kho sutavā paribbājako yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavatā saddhiṃ sammodi. Sammodanīyaṃ kathaṃ sāraṇīyaṃ vītisāretvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinno kho sutavā paribbājako bhagavantaṃ etadavoca –

7. On one occasion the Blessed One was dwelling at Rājagaha, on Vulture Peak Mountain. Then the wanderer Sutavā approached the Blessed One; having approached, he exchanged greetings with the Blessed One. Having exchanged courteous and memorable talk, he sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the wanderer Sutavā said this to the Blessed One:

“Ekamidāhaṃ, bhante, samayaṃ bhagavā idheva rājagahe viharāmi giribbaje. Tatra me, bhante, bhagavato sammukhā sutaṃ sammukhā paṭiggahitaṃ – ‘yo so, sutavā [M 3.0178] sutava (syā.), bhikkhu arahaṃ khīṇāsavo vusitavā katakaraṇīyo ohitabhāro [P 4.0370] anuppattasadattho parikkhīṇabhavasaṃyojano sammadaññāvimutto, abhabbo so pañca ṭhānāni ajjhācarituṃ – abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sañcicca pāṇaṃ jīvitā voropetuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu adinnaṃ theyyasaṅkhātaṃ ādātuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu methunaṃ dhammaṃ paṭisevituṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sampajānamusā sampajānaṃ musā (ka. sī.) bhāsituṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sannidhikārakaṃ kāme paribhuñjituṃ seyyathāpi pubbe agāriyabhūto’ti. Kacci metaṃ, bhante, bhagavato sussutaṃ suggahitaṃ sumanasikataṃ sūpadhārita”nti?

“Venerable sir, on one occasion the Blessed One was dwelling right here in Rājagaha, at Giribbaja. There, venerable sir, I heard and received this in the Blessed One's presence: ‘That monk, Sutavā, who is an arahant, whose taints are destroyed, who has lived the holy life, done what had to be done, laid down the burden, attained his own goal, utterly destroyed the fetters of existence, and is completely liberated through final knowledge, is incapable of transgressing in five instances. A monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of deliberately depriving a living being of life; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of taking what is not given, reckoned as theft; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of engaging in sexual intercourse; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of knowingly speaking falsehood; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of enjoying stored-up sensual pleasures as he did formerly when he was a householder.’ Venerable sir, have I heard this well from the Blessed One, grasped it well, attended to it well, and remembered it well?”

“Taggha te etaṃ, sutavā, sussutaṃ suggahitaṃ sumanasikataṃ sūpadhāritaṃ. Pubbe cāhaṃ, sutavā, etarahi ca evaṃ vadāmi – ‘yo so bhikkhu arahaṃ khīṇāsavo vusitavā katakaraṇīyo ohitabhāro anuppattasadattho parikkhīṇabhavasaṃyojano sammadaññāvimutto, abhabbo [V 3.0187] so nava ṭhānāni ajjhācarituṃ [T 4.0384] – abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sañcicca pāṇaṃ jīvitā voropetuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu adinnaṃ theyyasaṅkhātaṃ ādātuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu methunaṃ dhammaṃ paṭisevituṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sampajānamusā bhāsituṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sannidhikārakaṃ kāme paribhuñjituṃ seyyathāpi pubbe agāriyabhūto, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu chandāgatiṃ gantuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu dosāgatiṃ gantuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu mohāgatiṃ gantuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu bhayāgatiṃ [P 4.0371] gantuṃ’. Pubbe cāhaṃ, sutavā, etarahi ca evaṃ vadāmi – ‘yo so bhikkhu arahaṃ khīṇāsavo vusitavā katakaraṇīyo ohitabhāro anuppattasadattho parikkhīṇabhavasaṃyojano sammadaññāvimutto, abhabbo so imāni nava ṭhānāni ajjhācaritu’”nti. Sattamaṃ.

“Indeed, Sutavā, you have heard this well, grasped it well, attended to it well, and remembered it well. In the past, Sutavā, and now too, I say this: ‘A monk who is an arahant—with taints destroyed, who has lived the holy life, done what had to be done, laid down the burden, attained his own goal, utterly destroyed the fetters of existence, and is completely liberated through final knowledge—is incapable of transgressing in nine instances. A monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of deliberately depriving a living being of life; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of taking what is not given, reckoned as theft; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of engaging in sexual intercourse; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of knowingly speaking falsehood; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of enjoying stored-up sensual pleasures as he did formerly when he was a householder; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of following a wrong course through desire; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of following a wrong course through hatred; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of following a wrong course through delusion; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of following a wrong course through fear.’ In the past, Sutavā, and now too, I say this: ‘A monk who is an arahant—with taints destroyed, who has lived the holy life, done what had to be done, laid down the burden, attained his own goal, utterly destroyed the fetters of existence, and is completely liberated through final knowledge—is incapable of transgressing in these nine instances.’” Seventh.

8. Sajjhasuttaṃ

8. The Discourse concerning Sajjha

8. Ekaṃ samayaṃ bhagavā rājagahe viharati gijjhakūṭe pabbate. Atha kho sajjho paribbājako yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavatā saddhiṃ sammodi. Sammodanīyaṃ kathaṃ sāraṇīyaṃ vītisāretvā ekamantaṃ [M 3.0179] nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinno kho sajjho paribbājako bhagavantaṃ etadavoca –

8. On one occasion the Blessed One was dwelling at Rājagaha, on Vulture Peak Mountain. Then the wanderer Sajjha approached the Blessed One; having approached, he exchanged greetings with the Blessed One. Having concluded a courteous and memorable talk, he sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the wanderer Sajjha said this to the Blessed One:

“Ekamidāhaṃ, bhante, samayaṃ bhagavā idheva rājagahe viharāmi giribbaje. Tatra me, bhante, bhagavato sammukhā sutaṃ sammukhā paṭiggahitaṃ – ‘yo so, sajjha, bhikkhu arahaṃ khīṇāsavo vusitavā katakaraṇīyo ohitabhāro anuppattasadattho parikkhīṇabhavasaṃyojano sammadaññāvimutto, abhabbo so pañca ṭhānāni ajjhācarituṃ – abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sañcicca [T 4.0385] pāṇaṃ jīvitā voropetuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu adinnaṃ theyyasaṅkhātaṃ ādātuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu methunaṃ dhammaṃ paṭisevituṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sampajānamusā bhāsituṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sannidhikārakaṃ kāme paribhuñjituṃ seyyathāpi pubbe agāriyabhūto’ti. Kacci metaṃ, bhante, bhagavato sussutaṃ suggahitaṃ sumanasikataṃ sūpadhārita”nti?

“Venerable sir, on one occasion the Blessed One was dwelling right here in Rājagaha, at Giribbaja. There, venerable sir, I heard and received this in the Blessed One's presence: ‘That monk, Sajjha, who is an arahant, whose taints are destroyed, who has lived the holy life, done what had to be done, laid down the burden, attained his own goal, utterly destroyed the fetters of existence, and is completely liberated through final knowledge, is incapable of transgressing in five instances. A monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of deliberately depriving a living being of life; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of taking what is not given, reckoned as theft; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of engaging in sexual intercourse; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of knowingly speaking falsehood; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of enjoying stored-up sensual pleasures as he did formerly when he was a householder.’ Venerable sir, have I heard this well from the Blessed One, grasped it well, attended to it well, and remembered it well?”

“Taggha [P 4.0372] te etaṃ, sajjha, sussutaṃ suggahitaṃ sumanasikataṃ sūpadhāritaṃ. Pubbe cāhaṃ, sajjha[V 3.0188], etarahi ca evaṃ vadāmi – ‘yo so bhikkhu arahaṃ khīṇāsavo vusitavā katakaraṇīyo ohitabhāro anuppattasadattho parikkhīṇabhavasaṃyojano sammadaññāvimutto, abhabbo so nava ṭhānāni ajjhācarituṃ – abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sañcicca pāṇaṃ jīvitā voropetuṃ…pe… abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sannidhikārakaṃ kāme paribhuñjituṃ seyyathāpi pubbe agāriyabhūto, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu buddhaṃ paccakkhātuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu dhammaṃ paccakkhātuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu saṅghaṃ paccakkhātuṃ, abhabbo khīṇāsavo bhikkhu sikkhaṃ paccakkhātuṃ’. Pubbe cāhaṃ, sajjha, etarahi ca evaṃ vadāmi – ‘yo so bhikkhu arahaṃ khīṇāsavo vusitavā katakaraṇīyo ohitabhāro anuppattasadattho parikkhīṇabhavasaṃyojano sammadaññāvimutto, abhabbo so imāni nava ṭhānāni ajjhācaritu’”nti. Aṭṭhamaṃ.

“Indeed, Sajjha, you have heard this well, grasped it well, attended to it well, and remembered it well. In the past, Sajjha, and now too, I say this: ‘A monk who is an arahant—with taints destroyed, who has lived the holy life, done what had to be done, laid down the burden, attained his own goal, utterly destroyed the fetters of existence, and is completely liberated through final knowledge—is incapable of transgressing in nine instances. A monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of deliberately depriving a living being of life; …pe… a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of enjoying stored-up sensual pleasures as he did formerly when he was a householder; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of rejecting the Buddha; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of rejecting the Dhamma; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of rejecting the Saṅgha; a monk whose taints are destroyed is incapable of rejecting the training.’ In the past, Sajjha, and now too, I say this: ‘A monk who is an arahant—with taints destroyed, who has lived the holy life, done what had to be done, laid down the burden, attained his own goal, utterly destroyed the fetters of existence, and is completely liberated through final knowledge—is incapable of transgressing in these nine instances.’” Eighth.

9. Puggalasuttaṃ

9. The Discourse on Individuals

9. “Navayime, bhikkhave, puggalā santo saṃvijjamānā lokasmiṃ. Katame [T 4.0386] nava? Arahā, arahattāya paṭipanno, anāgāmī, anāgāmiphalasacchikiriyāya paṭipanno, sakadāgāmī, sakadāgāmiphalasacchikiriyāya paṭipanno[M 3.0180], sotāpanno, sotāpattiphalasacchikiriyāya paṭipanno, puthujjano – ime kho, bhikkhave, nava puggalā santo saṃvijjamānā lokasmi”nti. Navamaṃ.

9. “Monks, these nine individuals exist and are found in the world. Which nine? The Arahant, the one practicing for Arahantship, the Non-returner, the one practicing for the realization of the fruit of Non-returning, the Once-returner, the one practicing for the realization of the fruit of Once-returning, the Stream-enterer, the one practicing for the realization of the fruit of Stream-entry, and the worldling. Indeed, monks, these nine individuals exist and are found in the world.” Ninth.

10. Āhuneyyasuttaṃ

10. The Discourse on Those Worthy of Offerings

10. “Navayime[P 4.0373], bhikkhave, puggalā āhuneyyā pāhuneyyā dakkhiṇeyyā añjalikaraṇīyā anuttaraṃ puññakkhettaṃ lokassa. Katame nava? Arahā, arahattāya paṭipanno, anāgāmī, anāgāmiphalasacchikiriyāya paṭipanno, sakadāgāmī, sakadāgāmiphalasacchikiriyāya paṭipanno, sotāpanno, sotāpattiphalasacchikiriyāya paṭipanno, gotrabhū – ime kho, bhikkhave, nava puggalā āhuneyyā…pe… anuttaraṃ puññakkhettaṃ lokassā”ti. Dasamaṃ.

10. “Monks, these nine individuals are worthy of offerings, worthy of hospitality, worthy of donations, worthy of reverential salutation, an unsurpassed field of merit for the world. Which nine? The Arahant, the one practicing for Arahantship, the Non-returner, the one practicing for the realization of the fruit of Non-returning, the Once-returner, the one practicing for the realization of the fruit of Once-returning, the Stream-enterer, the one practicing for the realization of the fruit of Stream-entry, and the change-of-lineage person. Indeed, monks, these nine individuals are worthy of offerings… an unsurpassed field of merit for the world.” Tenth.

Sambodhivaggo paṭhamo.

The First Chapter on Awakening.

Tassuddānaṃ [V 3.0189] –

The summary of that:

Sambodhi nissayo ceva, meghiya nandakaṃ balaṃ;

Sevanā sutavā sajjho, puggalo āhuneyyena cāti.

Awakening, Support, Meghiya, Nandaka, Power, Association, Sutavā, Sajjha, Individuals, and Those Worthy of Offerings.

2. Sīhanādavaggo

2. The Chapter on the Lion's Roar

1. Sīhanādasuttaṃ

1. The Discourse on the Lion's Roar

11. Ekaṃ [V 3.0190] samayaṃ bhagavā sāvatthiyaṃ viharati jetavane anāthapiṇḍikassa ārāme. Atha kho āyasmā sāriputto yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinno kho āyasmā sāriputto bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – “vuttho [T 4.0387] me, bhante, sāvatthiyaṃ vassāvāso. Icchāmahaṃ, bhante, janapadacārikaṃ pakkamitu”nti. “Yassadāni tvaṃ, sāriputta, kālaṃ maññasī”ti. Atha kho āyasmā sāriputto uṭṭhāyāsanā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā padakkhiṇaṃ katvā pakkāmi. Atha [P 4.0374] kho aññataro bhikkhu acirapakkante āyasmante sāriputte bhagavantaṃ etadavoca – “āyasmā maṃ, bhante, sāriputto āsajja appaṭinissajja cārikaṃ pakkanto”ti. Atha kho bhagavā aññataraṃ bhikkhuṃ āmantesi – “ehi [M 3.0181] tvaṃ, bhikkhu, mama vacanena sāriputtaṃ āmantehi – ‘satthā taṃ, āvuso sāriputta, āmantetī’”ti. “Evaṃ, bhante”ti kho so bhikkhu bhagavato paṭissutvā yenāyasmā sāriputto tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā āyasmantaṃ sāriputtaṃ etadavoca – “satthā taṃ, āvuso sāriputta, āmantetī”ti. “Evamāvuso”ti kho āyasmā sāriputto tassa bhikkhuno paccassosi.

11. On one occasion, the Blessed One was dwelling in Sāvatthī at Jeta’s Grove, Anāthapiṇḍika’s Park. Then the Venerable Sāriputta approached the Blessed One, and having approached, paid homage to the Blessed One and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the Venerable Sāriputta said this to the Blessed One: “Venerable Sir, I have dwelt the rains-residence in Sāvatthī. I wish, Venerable Sir, to depart on a tour of the country.” “Sāriputta, you may do so at the time you deem fit.” Then the Venerable Sāriputta rose from his seat, paid homage to the Blessed One, circumambulated him keeping his right side toward him, and departed. Then, not long after the Venerable Sāriputta had departed, a certain monk said this to the Blessed One: “Venerable Sir, the Venerable Sāriputta, having collided with me, has departed on a tour without making amends.” Then the Blessed One addressed a certain monk: “Come, monk, in my name, call Sāriputta, saying: ‘Friend Sāriputta, the Teacher calls you.’” “Yes, Venerable Sir,” that monk replied to the Blessed One, and he approached the Venerable Sāriputta. Having approached, he said this to the Venerable Sāriputta: “Friend Sāriputta, the Teacher calls you.” “Yes, friend,” the Venerable Sāriputta assented to that monk.

Tena kho pana samayena āyasmā ca mahāmoggallāno mahāmoggalāno (ka.) āyasmā ca ānando avāpuraṇaṃ apāpuraṇaṃ (syā. ka.) ādāya vihāre āhiṇḍanti vihārena vihāraṃ anvāhiṇḍanti (sī. pī.), vihāraṃ āhiṇḍanti (syā.) – “abhikkamathāyasmanto, abhikkamathāyasmanto! Idānāyasmā sāriputto bhagavato sammukhā sīhanādaṃ nadissatī”ti. Atha kho āyasmā sāriputto yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinnaṃ kho āyasmantaṃ sāriputtaṃ bhagavā etadavoca – “idha te, sāriputta, aññataro sabrahmacārī khīyanadhammaṃ āpanno – ‘āyasmā maṃ, bhante, sāriputto āsajja [T 4.0388] appaṭinissajjacārikaṃ pakkanto’”ti.

Now at that time, the Venerable Mahāmoggallāna and the Venerable Ānanda, taking a key, were going about the dwellings, saying: “Come forward, venerable sirs, come forward! Presently, the Venerable Sāriputta will roar a lion’s roar in the presence of the Blessed One.” Then the Venerable Sāriputta approached the Blessed One, and having approached, paid homage to the Blessed One and sat down to one side. The Blessed One said this to the Venerable Sāriputta who was seated to one side: “Here, Sāriputta, a certain fellow in the holy life is given to making a complaint, saying: ‘Venerable Sir, the Venerable Sāriputta, having collided with me, has departed on a tour without making amends.’”

“Yassa [V 3.0191] nūna, bhante, kāye kāyagatāsati anupaṭṭhitā assa, so idha aññataraṃ sabrahmacāriṃ āsajja appaṭinissajja cārikaṃ pakkameyya.

“Venerable Sir, it is surely one in whom mindfulness directed to the body is unestablished who might, in this Dispensation, collide with a certain fellow in the holy life and depart on a tour without making amends.

“Seyyathāpi, bhante, pathaviyaṃ sucimpi nikkhipanti asucimpi nikkhipanti gūthagatampi nikkhipanti muttagatampi nikkhipanti kheḷagatampi nikkhipanti pubbagatampi nikkhipanti lohitagatampi nikkhipanti, na ca tena pathavī aṭṭīyati vā harāyati vā jigucchati vā; evamevaṃ [P 4.0375] kho ahaṃ, bhante, pathavīsamena cetasā viharāmi vipulena mahaggatena appamāṇena averena abyāpajjena. Yassa nūna, bhante, kāye kāyagatāsati anupaṭṭhitā assa, so idha aññataraṃ sabrahmacāriṃ āsajja appaṭinissajja cārikaṃ pakkameyya.

“Venerable Sir, just as people cast both clean and unclean things upon the earth—they cast feces, they cast urine, they cast spittle, they cast pus, and they cast blood—and the earth is not thereby distressed, humiliated, or disgusted; even so, Venerable Sir, I dwell with a mind like the earth: vast, exalted, immeasurable, without enmity, and without ill will. Venerable Sir, it is surely one in whom mindfulness directed to the body is unestablished who might, in this Dispensation, collide with a certain fellow in the holy life and depart on a tour without making amends.
