Dependent on desire and lust, attachment comes to be. Dependent on attachment, possessiveness comes to be. Dependent on possessiveness, stinginess comes to be. Dependent on stinginess, guarding comes to be. With guarding as the cause, the taking up of rods, the taking up of weapons, quarrels, brawls, disputes, recrimination, slander, and false speech come to be; numerous other evil, unwholesome states arise. These, Bhikkhus, are the nine phenomena rooted in craving.

4. Sattāvāsasuttaṃ

4. The Discourse on the Abodes of Beings

24. dī. ni. 3.341 “Navayime, bhikkhave, sattāvāsā. Katame nava? Santi, bhikkhave, sattā nānattakāyā nānattasaññino, seyyathāpi manussā, ekacce ca devā, ekacce ca vinipātikā. Ayaṃ paṭhamo sattāvāso.

24. Bhikkhus, there are these nine abodes of beings. What are the nine? There are, Bhikkhus, beings who are diverse in body and diverse in perception, such as human beings, some devas, and some beings in the states of deprivation. This is the first abode of beings.

“Santi[V 3.0211], bhikkhave, sattā nānattakāyā ekattasaññino, seyyathāpi devā brahmakāyikā paṭhamābhinibbattā. Ayaṃ dutiyo sattāvāso.

There are, Bhikkhus, beings who are diverse in body and singular in perception, such as the devas of Brahmā’s retinue who are first reborn there. This is the second abode of beings.

“Santi[T 4.0414], bhikkhave, sattā ekattakāyā nānattasaññino, seyyathāpi devā ābhassarā. Ayaṃ tatiyo sattāvāso.

There are, Bhikkhus, beings who are singular in body and diverse in perception, such as the Ābhassara devas. This is the third abode of beings.

“Santi, bhikkhave, sattā ekattakāyā ekattasaññino, seyyathāpi devā subhakiṇhā. Ayaṃ catuttho sattāvāso.

There are, Bhikkhus, beings who are singular in body and singular in perception, such as the Subhakiṇha devas. This is the fourth abode of beings.

“Santi, bhikkhave, sattā asaññino appaṭisaṃvedino, seyyathāpi devā asaññasattā. Ayaṃ pañcamo sattāvāso.

There are, Bhikkhus, beings who are non-percipient and non-experiencing, such as the non-percipient devas. This is the fifth abode of beings.

“Santi[M 3.0202], bhikkhave, sattā sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ‘ananto ākāso’ti ākāsānañcāyatanūpagā. Ayaṃ chaṭṭho sattāvāso.

There are, Bhikkhus, beings who, by completely transcending perceptions of form, by the passing away of perceptions of resistance, and by not attending to perceptions of diversity, percipient that ‘space is infinite,’ have attained the sphere of the infinity of space. This is the sixth abode of beings.

“Santi, bhikkhave, sattā sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘anantaṃ viññāṇa’nti viññāṇañcāyatanūpagā. Ayaṃ sattamo sattāvāso.

There are, Bhikkhus, beings who, by completely transcending the sphere of the infinity of space, percipient that ‘consciousness is infinite,’ have attained the sphere of the infinity of consciousness. This is the seventh abode of beings.

“Santi, bhikkhave, sattā sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘natthi kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanūpagā. Ayaṃ aṭṭhamo sattāvāso.

There are, Bhikkhus, beings who, by completely transcending the sphere of the infinity of consciousness, percipient that ‘there is nothing,’ have attained the sphere of nothingness. This is the eighth abode of beings.

“Santi, bhikkhave, sattā sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanūpagā. Ayaṃ navamo sattāvāso. Ime kho, bhikkhave, nava sattāvāsā”ti. Catutthaṃ.

There are, Bhikkhus, beings who, by completely transcending the sphere of nothingness, have attained the sphere of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. This is the ninth abode of beings. These, Bhikkhus, are the nine abodes of beings.

5. Paññāsuttaṃ

5. The Discourse on Wisdom

25. “Yato [V 3.0212][P 4.0402] kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno kallaṃ vacanāya – ‘khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’”ti.

25. Bhikkhus, when a bhikkhu’s mind is well cultivated with wisdom, it is then fitting for that bhikkhu to declare: ‘Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.’

“Kathañca[T 4.0415], bhikkhave, bhikkhuno paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti? ‘Vītarāgaṃ me citta’nti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘vītadosaṃ me citta’nti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘vītamohaṃ me citta’nti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘asarāgadhammaṃ me citta’nti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘asadosadhammaṃ me citta’nti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘asamohadhammaṃ me citta’nti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘anāvattidhammaṃ me cittaṃ kāmabhavāyā’ti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘anāvattidhammaṃ me cittaṃ rūpabhavāyā’ti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘anāvattidhammaṃ me cittaṃ arūpabhavāyā’ti paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti. Yato kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno paññāya cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhuno kallaṃ vacanāya – ‘khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’”ti. Pañcamaṃ.

“And how, bhikkhus, is a bhikkhu’s mind well cultivated with wisdom? ‘My mind is without lust,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. ‘My mind is without hatred,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. ‘My mind is without delusion,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. ‘My mind is not of a nature to be lustful,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. ‘My mind is not of a nature to be hateful,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. ‘My mind is not of a nature to be deluded,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. ‘My mind is not of a nature to return to the sensual realm of existence,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. ‘My mind is not of a nature to return to the form realm of existence,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. ‘My mind is not of a nature to return to the formless realm of existence,’ thus his mind is well cultivated with wisdom. Indeed, bhikkhus, when a bhikkhu’s mind is well cultivated with wisdom, it is then fitting for that bhikkhu to declare: ‘Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.’” The Fifth.

6. Silāyūpasuttaṃ

6. The Discourse on the Stone Pillar

26. Ekaṃ [M 3.0203] samayaṃ āyasmā ca sāriputto āyasmā ca candikāputto rājagahe viharanti veḷuvane kalandakanivāpe. Tatra kho āyasmā candikāputto bhikkhū āmantesi ( ) (āvuso…pe… etadavoca) (sī.) – “devadatto, āvuso, bhikkhūnaṃ evaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā citaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya [P 4.0403] – ‘khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’”ti.

26. At one time the Venerable Sāriputta and the Venerable Candikāputta were dwelling at Rājagaha in the Bamboo Grove, the Squirrels’ Sanctuary. There, the Venerable Candikāputta addressed the bhikkhus: “Friends, Devadatta teaches the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’”

Evaṃ vutte āyasmā sāriputto āyasmantaṃ candikāputtaṃ etadavoca – “na kho, āvuso candikāputta, devadatto bhikkhūnaṃ evaṃ dhammaṃ deseti [T 4.0416] – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā citaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya – khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ [V 3.0213] brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’ti. Evañca kho, āvuso, candikāputta, devadatto bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya – khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’”ti.

When this was said, the Venerable Sāriputta said this to the Venerable Candikāputta: “Not so, friend Candikāputta, Devadatta does not teach the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’ Rather, friend Candikāputta, Devadatta teaches the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is well cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’”

Dutiyampi kho āyasmā candikāputto bhikkhū āmantesi – “devadatto, āvuso, bhikkhūnaṃ evaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā citaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya – khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’”ti. Dutiyampi kho āyasmā sāriputto āyasmantaṃ candikāputtaṃ etadavoca – “na kho, āvuso candikāputta, devadatto bhikkhūnaṃ evaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā citaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya – khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’ti. Evañca kho, āvuso candikāputta, devadatto bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya – khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’”ti.

For a second time, the Venerable Candikāputta addressed the bhikkhus: “Friends, Devadatta teaches the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’” For a second time, the Venerable Sāriputta said this to the Venerable Candikāputta: “Not so, friend Candikāputta, Devadatta does not teach the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’ Rather, friend Candikāputta, Devadatta teaches the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is well cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’”

Tatiyampi [T 4.0417][M 3.0204] kho āyasmā candikāputto bhikkhū āmantesi – “devadatto, āvuso, bhikkhūnaṃ evaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā citaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya – khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’”ti. Tatiyampi kho āyasmā sāriputto āyasmantaṃ candikāputtaṃ etadavoca – “na kho, āvuso candikāputta, devadatto bhikkhūnaṃ evaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā citaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya – khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’ti. Evañca kho, āvuso candikāputta, devadatto bhikkhūnaṃ dhammaṃ deseti – ‘yato kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti, tassetaṃ bhikkhuno kallaṃ veyyākaraṇāya – khīṇā jāti, vusitaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, kataṃ karaṇīyaṃ, nāparaṃ itthattāyāti pajānāmī’”ti.

For a third time, the Venerable Candikāputta addressed the bhikkhus: “Friends, Devadatta teaches the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’” For a third time, the Venerable Sāriputta said this to the Venerable Candikāputta: “Not so, friend Candikāputta, Devadatta does not teach the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’ Rather, friend Candikāputta, Devadatta teaches the Dhamma to the bhikkhus thus: ‘Friends, when a bhikkhu’s mind is well cultivated by the mind, it is then proper for that bhikkhu to declare: “Birth is destroyed, the holy life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is nothing further for this state of being, I understand.”’”

“Kathañca, āvuso, bhikkhuno cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti? ‘Vītarāgaṃ [P 4.0404] me citta’nti [V 3.0214] cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘vītadosaṃ me citta’nti cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘vītamohaṃ me citta’nti cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘asarāgadhammaṃ me citta’nti cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘asadosadhammaṃ me citta’nti cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘asamohadhammaṃ me citta’nti cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘anāvattidhammaṃ me [T 4.0418] cittaṃ kāmabhavāyā’ti cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘anāvattidhammaṃ me cittaṃ rūpabhavāyā’ti cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti; ‘anāvattidhammaṃ me cittaṃ arūpabhavāyā’ti cetasā cittaṃ suparicitaṃ hoti. Evaṃ sammā vimuttacittassa kho, āvuso, bhikkhuno bhusā cepi cakkhuviññeyyā rūpā cakkhussa āpāthaṃ āgacchanti, nevassa cittaṃ pariyādiyanti; amissīkatamevassa cittaṃ hoti ṭhitaṃ āneñjappattaṃ, vayaṃ cassānupassati.

“And how, friend, is a bhikkhu’s mind well-acquainted by means of his mind? The mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is free from lust’; the mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is free from hatred’; the mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is free from delusion’; the mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is not of a lustful nature’; the mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is not of a hateful nature’; the mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is not of a deluded nature’; the mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is of a nature not to return to sensual existence’; the mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is of a nature not to return to form existence’; the mind is well-acquainted by means of the mind thus: ‘My mind is of a nature not to return to formless existence.’ For a bhikkhu whose mind is thus well-liberated, friend, even if powerful forms cognizable by the eye come into the range of his eye, they do not overcome his mind. His mind remains unmingled, steadfast, having attained the imperturbable, and he observes their passing away.”

“Seyyathāpi, āvuso, silāyūpo soḷasakukkuko. Tassassu aṭṭha kukkū heṭṭhā nemaṅgamā, aṭṭha kukkū upari nemassa. Atha puratthimāya cepi disāya āgaccheyya bhusā vātavuṭṭhi, neva naṃ saṅkampeyya [P 4.0405] na sampavedheyya; atha pacchimāya… atha uttarāya… atha dakkhiṇāya cepi disāya [M 3.0205] āgaccheyya bhusā vātavuṭṭhi, neva naṃ saṅkampeyya na sampavedheyya. Taṃ kissa hetu? Gambhīrattā, āvuso, nemassa, sunikhātattā silāyūpassa. Evamevaṃ kho, āvuso, sammā vimuttacittassa bhikkhuno bhusā cepi cakkhuviññeyyā rūpā cakkhussa āpāthaṃ āgacchanti, nevassa cittaṃ pariyādiyanti; amissīkatamevassa cittaṃ hoti ṭhitaṃ āneñjappattaṃ, vayaṃ cassānupassati.

“Suppose, friend, there was a stone pillar sixteen cubits long, of which eight cubits were sunk in the foundation pit and eight were above the foundation pit. If a fierce squall of wind and rain were to come from the east, it would not shake it or cause it to tremble. If it were to come from the west… from the north… from the south, it would not shake it or cause it to tremble. For what reason? Because, friend, the foundation pit is deep and the stone pillar is well-planted. So too, friend, for a bhikkhu whose mind is well-liberated, even if powerful forms cognizable by the eye come into the range of his eye, they do not overcome his mind. His mind remains unmingled, steadfast, having attained the imperturbable, and he observes their passing away.”

“Bhusā cepi sotaviññeyyā saddā… ghānaviññeyyā gandhā… jivhāviññeyyā rasā… kāyaviññeyyā [T 4.0419] phoṭṭhabbā… manoviññeyyā dhammā manassa āpāthaṃ āgacchanti, nevassa cittaṃ pariyādiyanti; amissīkatamevassa cittaṃ hoti ṭhitaṃ āneñjappattaṃ, vayaṃ cassānupassatī”ti. Chaṭṭhaṃ.

“Even if powerful sounds cognizable by the ear… smells cognizable by the nose… tastes cognizable by the tongue… tactile objects cognizable by the body… mental phenomena cognizable by the mind come into the range of his mind, they do not overcome his mind. His mind remains unmingled, steadfast, having attained the imperturbable, and he observes their passing away.” The Sixth.

7. Paṭhamaverasuttaṃ

7. The First Discourse on Animosities

27. a. ni. 9.92; saṃ. ni. 5.1024 Atha kho anāthapiṇḍiko gahapati yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ [V 3.0215] abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinnaṃ kho anāthapiṇḍikaṃ gahapatiṃ bhagavā etadavoca –

27. Then the householder Anāthapiṇḍika went to the Blessed One, paid homage to him, and sat down to one side. The Blessed One said this to the householder Anāthapiṇḍika as he was sitting to one side:

“Yato kho, gahapati, ariyasāvakassa pañca bhayāni verāni vūpasantāni honti, catūhi ca sotāpattiyaṅgehi samannāgato hoti, so ākaṅkhamāno attanāva attānaṃ byākareyya – ‘khīṇanirayomhi khīṇatiracchānayoni khīṇapettivisayo khīṇāpāyaduggativinipāto, sotāpannohamasmi avinipātadhammo niyato sambodhiparāyaṇo’”ti.

“Householder, when for a noble disciple the five fears and animosities have subsided, and he is endowed with the four factors of stream-entry, he may, if he wishes, declare of himself: ‘I am finished with hell, finished with the animal realm, finished with the domain of ghosts, finished with the plane of misery, the bad destination, the lower world. I am a stream-enterer, no longer subject to downfall, certain, destined for enlightenment.’”

“Katamāni [P 4.0406] pañca bhayāni verāni vūpasantāni honti? Yaṃ, gahapati, pāṇātipātī pāṇātipātapaccayā diṭṭhadhammikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, samparāyikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, cetasikampi dukkhaṃ domanassaṃ paṭisaṃvedeti, pāṇātipātā paṭivirato neva diṭṭhadhammikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, na samparāyikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, na cetasikampi dukkhaṃ domanassaṃ paṭisaṃvedeti. Pāṇātipātā paṭiviratassa evaṃ taṃ bhayaṃ veraṃ vūpasantaṃ hoti.

“What are the five fears and animosities that have subsided? Householder, one who takes life, on account of taking life, generates fear and animosity in this very life, generates fear and animosity in the life to come, and experiences mental suffering and displeasure. But one who abstains from taking life does not generate fear and animosity in this very life, does not generate fear and animosity in the life to come, and does not experience mental suffering and displeasure. For one who abstains from taking life, that fear and animosity has thus subsided.

“Yaṃ, gahapati, adinnādāyī…pe… kāmesumicchācārī… musāvādī… surāmerayamajjapamādaṭṭhāyī surāmerayamajjapamādaṭṭhānapaccayā diṭṭhadhammikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, samparāyikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, cetasikampi [T 4.0420] dukkhaṃ domanassaṃ [M 3.0206] paṭisaṃvedeti, surāmerayamajjapamādaṭṭhānā paṭivirato neva diṭṭhadhammikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, na samparāyikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, na cetasikampi dukkhaṃ domanassaṃ paṭisaṃvedeti. Surāmerayamajjapamādaṭṭhānā paṭiviratassa evaṃ taṃ bhayaṃ veraṃ vūpasantaṃ hoti. Imāni pañca bhayāni verāni vūpasantāni honti.

“Householder, one who takes what is not given… one who engages in sexual misconduct… one who speaks falsely… one who partakes of liquors, wines, and intoxicants, which are a basis for heedlessness, on account of partaking of them, generates fear and animosity in this very life, generates fear and animosity in the life to come, and experiences mental suffering and displeasure. But one who abstains from liquors, wines, and intoxicants, which are a basis for heedlessness, does not generate fear and animosity in this very life, does not generate fear and animosity in the life to come, and does not experience mental suffering and displeasure. For one who abstains from them, that fear and animosity has thus subsided. These are the five fears and animosities that have subsided.

“Katamehi catūhi sotāpattiyaṅgehi samannāgato hoti? Idha, gahapati, ariyasāvako buddhe aveccappasādena samannāgato hoti – ‘itipi so bhagavā arahaṃ sammāsambuddho vijjācaraṇasampanno sugato lokavidū anuttaro purisadammasārathi satthā devamanussānaṃ buddho bhagavā’”ti.

“With which four factors of stream-entry is he endowed? Here, householder, a noble disciple is endowed with unwavering confidence in the Buddha thus: ‘The Blessed One is an Arahant, a perfectly self-enlightened one, accomplished in knowledge and conduct, the Well-Gone, knower of the world, unsurpassed trainer of persons to be tamed, teacher of gods and humans, the Enlightened One, the Blessed One.’”

Dhamme [V 3.0216] aveccappasādena samannāgato hoti – ‘svākkhāto bhagavatā dhammo sandiṭṭhiko akāliko ehipassiko opaneyyiko paccattaṃ veditabbo viññūhī’ti.

“He is endowed with unwavering confidence in the Dhamma thus: ‘The Dhamma has been well-expounded by the Blessed One, is visible here and now, timeless, inviting inspection, leading onwards, to be realized for themselves by the wise.’”

Saṅghe aveccappasādena samannāgato hoti – ‘suppaṭipanno bhagavato sāvakasaṅgho ujuppaṭipanno bhagavato sāvakasaṅgho ñāyappaṭipanno [P 4.0407] bhagavato sāvakasaṅgho sāmīcippaṭipanno bhagavato sāvakasaṅgho; yadidaṃ cattāri purisayugāni aṭṭha purisapuggalā esa bhagavato sāvakasaṅgho āhuneyyo pāhuneyyo dakkhiṇeyyo añjalikaraṇīyo anuttaraṃ puññakkhettaṃ lokassā’ti.

He is endowed with unwavering confidence in the Sangha thus: ‘The Sangha of the Blessed One’s disciples has practiced well; the Sangha of the Blessed One’s disciples has practiced uprightly; the Sangha of the Blessed One’s disciples has practiced methodically; the Sangha of the Blessed One’s disciples has practiced properly. That is, the four pairs of men, the eight individual persons—this Sangha of the Blessed One’s disciples is worthy of gifts, worthy of hospitality, worthy of offerings, worthy of reverential salutation, the unsurpassed field of merit for the world.’

Ariyakantehi sīlehi samannāgato hoti akhaṇḍehi acchiddehi asabalehi akammāsehi bhujissehi viññuppasatthehi aparāmaṭṭhehi samādhisaṃvattanikehi. Imehi catūhi sotāpattiyaṅgehi samannāgato hoti.

He is endowed with virtues cherished by the noble ones, which are unbroken, unblemished, unspotted, unmarred, liberating, praised by the wise, not misapprehended, and conducive to concentration. He is endowed with these four factors of stream-entry.

“Yato [T 4.0421] kho, gahapati, ariyasāvakassa imāni pañca bhayāni verāni vūpasantāni honti, imehi ca catūhi sotāpattiyaṅgehi samannāgato hoti, so ākaṅkhamāno attanāva attānaṃ byākareyya – ‘khīṇanirayomhi khīṇatiracchānayoni khīṇapettivisayo khīṇāpāyaduggativinipāto; sotāpannohamasmi avinipātadhammo niyato sambodhiparāyaṇo’”ti. Sattamaṃ.

Indeed, householder, when for a noble disciple these five fears and enmities have subsided, and he is endowed with these four factors of stream-entry, he may, if he so desires, declare of himself by himself: ‘Hell is destroyed for me, the animal realm is destroyed, the realm of ghosts is destroyed, the plane of misery, the bad destination, the state of downfall is destroyed. I am a stream-enterer, not subject to falling into states of woe, certain, and destined for enlightenment.’ Seventh.

8. Dutiyaverasuttaṃ

8. The Second Discourse on Fears and Enmities

28. saṃ. ni. 5.1025 “Yato [M 3.0207] kho, bhikkhave, ariyasāvakassa pañca bhayāni verāni vūpasantāni honti, catūhi ca sotāpattiyaṅgehi samannāgato hoti, so ākaṅkhamāno attanāva attānaṃ byākareyya – ‘khīṇanirayomhi khīṇatiracchānayoni khīṇapettivisayo khīṇāpāyaduggativinipāto; sotāpannohamasmi avinipātadhammo niyato sambodhiparāyaṇo’”ti.

28. Indeed, Bhikkhus, when for a noble disciple the five fears and enmities have subsided, and he is endowed with the four factors of stream-entry, he may, if he so desires, declare of himself by himself: ‘Hell is destroyed for me, the animal realm is destroyed, the realm of ghosts is destroyed, the plane of misery, the bad destination, the state of downfall is destroyed. I am a stream-enterer, not subject to falling into states of woe, certain, and destined for enlightenment.’

“Katamāni pañca bhayāni verāni vūpasantāni honti? Yaṃ, bhikkhave, pāṇātipātī pāṇātipātapaccayā diṭṭhadhammikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, samparāyikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, cetasikampi [V 3.0217] dukkhaṃ domanassaṃ paṭisaṃvedeti, pāṇātipātā paṭivirato…pe… evaṃ taṃ bhayaṃ veraṃ vūpasantaṃ hoti.

What are the five fears and enmities that are stilled? Bhikkhus, one who takes life, on account of taking life, generates fear and enmity in the present existence and generates fear and enmity in a future existence, and he experiences mental suffering and displeasure. For one who abstains from taking life… thus that fear and enmity is stilled.

“Yaṃ, bhikkhave, adinnādāyī…pe… surāmerayamajjapamādaṭṭhāyī surāmerayamajjapamādaṭṭhānapaccayā diṭṭhadhammikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, samparāyikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, cetasikampi dukkhaṃ domanassaṃ paṭisaṃvedeti, surāmerayamajjapamādaṭṭhānā paṭivirato neva diṭṭhadhammikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, na samparāyikampi bhayaṃ veraṃ pasavati, na cetasikampi dukkhaṃ domanassaṃ paṭisaṃvedeti. Surāmerayamajjapamādaṭṭhānā [T 4.0422] paṭiviratassa evaṃ taṃ bhayaṃ veraṃ vūpasantaṃ hoti. Imāni pañca bhayāni verāni vūpasantāni honti.

Bhikkhus, one who takes what is not given… one who partakes of fermented and distilled intoxicants which are the basis for negligence, on account of partaking of fermented and distilled intoxicants which are the basis for negligence, generates fear and enmity in the present existence and generates fear and enmity in a future existence, and he experiences mental suffering and displeasure. For one who abstains from fermented and distilled intoxicants which are the basis for negligence, he does not generate fear and enmity in the present existence, nor does he generate fear and enmity in a future existence, nor does he experience mental suffering and displeasure. For one who abstains from fermented and distilled intoxicants which are the basis for negligence, thus that fear and enmity is stilled. These are the five fears and enmities that are stilled.

“Katamehi catūhi sotāpattiyaṅgehi samannāgato hoti? Idha, bhikkhave, ariyasāvako buddhe aveccappasādena samannāgato hoti – ‘itipi so bhagavā…pe… satthā devamanussānaṃ buddho bhagavā’ti. Dhamme…pe… saṅghe… ariyakantehi sīlehi samannāgato hoti akhaṇḍehi acchiddehi asabalehi akammāsehi bhujissehi viññuppasatthehi aparāmaṭṭhehi samādhisaṃvattanikehi. Imehi catūhi sotāpattiyaṅgehi samannāgato hoti.

With which four factors of stream-entry is he endowed? Here, Bhikkhus, a noble disciple is endowed with unwavering confidence in the Buddha thus: ‘For this reason, the Blessed One is… the Teacher of gods and humans, the Buddha, the Blessed One.’ In the Dhamma… In the Sangha… He is endowed with virtues cherished by the noble ones, which are unbroken, unblemished, unspotted, unmarred, liberating, praised by the wise, not misapprehended, and conducive to concentration. He is endowed with these four factors of stream-entry.

“Yato [P 4.0408] kho, bhikkhave, ariyasāvakassa imāni pañca bhayāni verāni vūpasantāni honti, imehi ca catūhi sotāpattiyaṅgehi samannāgato hoti, so ākaṅkhamāno attanāva attānaṃ byākareyya – ‘khīṇanirayomhi khīṇatiracchānayoni khīṇapettivisayo khīṇāpāyaduggativinipāto[M 3.0208]; sotāpannohamasmi avinipātadhammo niyato sambodhiparāyaṇo’”ti. Aṭṭhamaṃ.

Indeed, Bhikkhus, when for a noble disciple these five fears and enmities have subsided, and he is endowed with these four factors of stream-entry, he may, if he so desires, declare of himself by himself: ‘Hell is destroyed for me, the animal realm is destroyed, the realm of ghosts is destroyed, the plane of misery, the bad destination, the state of downfall is destroyed. I am a stream-enterer, not subject to falling into states of woe, certain, and destined for enlightenment.’ Eighth.

9. Āghātavatthusuttaṃ

9. Discourse on the Grounds for Resentment

29. vibha. 960; dī. ni. 3.340; a. ni. 10.79 “Navayimāni, bhikkhave, āghātavatthūni. Katamāni nava? ‘Anatthaṃ me acarī’ti āghātaṃ bandhati; ‘anatthaṃ me caratī’ti āghātaṃ bandhati; ‘anatthaṃ me carissatī’ti āghātaṃ bandhati; ‘piyassa me manāpassa anatthaṃ acarī’ti…pe… ‘anatthaṃ caratī’ti…pe… ‘anatthaṃ carissatī’ti āghātaṃ bandhati; ‘appiyassa me [T 4.0423] amanāpassa atthaṃ acarī’ti [V 3.0218] …pe… ‘atthaṃ caratī’ti…pe… ‘atthaṃ carissatī’ti āghātaṃ bandhati. Imāni kho, bhikkhave, nava āghātavatthūnī”ti. Navamaṃ.

29. Bhikkhus, there are these nine grounds for harboring resentment. What nine? One harbors resentment, thinking: ‘He has done me harm.’ One harbors resentment, thinking: ‘He is doing me harm.’ One harbors resentment, thinking: ‘He will do me harm.’ One harbors resentment, thinking: ‘He has done harm to a person dear and pleasing to me’ … ‘he is doing harm…’ … ‘he will do harm.’ One harbors resentment, thinking: ‘He has benefited a person not dear and not pleasing to me’ … ‘he is benefiting…’ … ‘he will benefit.’ These, bhikkhus, are the nine grounds for harboring resentment. Ninth.

10. Āghātapaṭivinayasuttaṃ

10. Discourse on the Removal of Resentment

30. dī. ni. 3.340, 359 “Navayime, bhikkhave, āghātapaṭivinayā. Katame nava? ‘Anatthaṃ me acari acarīti (syā.), evaṃ “carati, carissati” padesupi, taṃ kutettha labbhā’ti āghātaṃ paṭivineti; ‘anatthaṃ me carati, taṃ kutettha labbhā’ti āghātaṃ paṭivineti; ‘anatthaṃ me carissati, taṃ kutettha labbhā’ti āghātaṃ paṭivineti; piyassa me manāpassa anatthaṃ acari…pe… anatthaṃ carati…pe… ‘anatthaṃ [P 4.0409] carissati, taṃ kutettha labbhā’ti āghātaṃ paṭivineti; appiyassa me amanāpassa atthaṃ acari…pe… atthaṃ carati…pe… ‘atthaṃ carissati, taṃ kutettha labbhā’ti āghātaṃ paṭivineti. Ime kho, bhikkhave, nava āghātapaṭivinayā”ti. Dasamaṃ.

30. Bhikkhus, there are these nine ways of removing resentment. What nine? One removes resentment by reflecting: ‘He has done me harm, but how could it be otherwise in this case?’ One removes resentment by reflecting: ‘He is doing me harm, but how could it be otherwise in this case?’ One removes resentment by reflecting: ‘He will do me harm, but how could it be otherwise in this case?’ One removes resentment by reflecting: ‘He has done harm to a person dear and pleasing to me… is doing harm… will do harm, but how could it be otherwise in this case?’ One removes resentment by reflecting: ‘He has benefited a person not dear and not pleasing to me… is benefiting… will benefit, but how could it be otherwise in this case?’ These, bhikkhus, are the nine ways of removing resentment. Tenth.

11. Anupubbanirodhasuttaṃ

11. Discourse on Gradual Cessation

31. “Navayime, bhikkhave, anupubbanirodhā. Katame nava? Paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa kāmasaññā āmissasaññā (syā.) niruddhā hoti; dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa vitakkavicārā niruddhā honti; tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa pīti niruddhā hoti; catutthaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa assāsapassāsā niruddhā honti; ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samāpannassa rūpasaññā niruddhā hoti; viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samāpannassa ākāsānañcāyatanasaññā niruddhā hoti; ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samāpannassa [M 3.0209] viññāṇañcāyatanasaññā niruddhā hoti[T 4.0424]; nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samāpannassa ākiñcaññāyatanasaññā niruddhā hoti; saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ samāpannassa saññā ca vedanā ca niruddhā honti. Ime kho, bhikkhave, nava anupubbanirodhā”ti dī. ni. 3.344, 349. Ekādasamaṃ.

31. “Bhikkhus, there are these nine gradual cessations. What nine? For one who has entered the first jhāna, perception of sensual pleasures has ceased. For one who has entered the second jhāna, applied and sustained thought have ceased. For one who has entered the third jhāna, rapture has ceased. For one who has entered the fourth jhāna, in-and-out breathing has ceased. For one who has entered the base of infinite space, perception of form has ceased. For one who has entered the base of infinite consciousness, perception of the base of infinite space has ceased. For one who has entered the base of nothingness, perception of the base of infinite consciousness has ceased. For one who has entered the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, perception of the base of nothingness has ceased. For one who has entered the cessation of perception and feeling, perception and feeling have ceased. These, bhikkhus, are the nine gradual cessations.” Eleventh.

Sattāvāsavaggo tatiyo.

The Third Chapter: On the Abodes of Beings.

Tassuddānaṃ –

The summary for this chapter:

Tiṭhānaṃ khaḷuṅko taṇhā, sattapaññā silāyupo;

Dve verā dve āghātāni, anupubbanirodhena cāti.

Grounds, the Horse, Craving, Beings, Wisdom, the Stone Pillar; two on Enmities, two on Resentments, and with Gradual Cessation.

4. Mahāvaggo

4. The Great Chapter

1. Anupubbavihārasuttaṃ

1. The Discourse on Gradual Abidings

32. dī. ni. 3.344, 359 “Navayime[V 3.0219][P 4.0410], bhikkhave, anupubbavihārā. Katame nava? ettha sī. pī. potthakesu “idha bhikkhave bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehī” tiādinā vittharena pāṭho dissati Paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ, dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ, tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ, catutthaṃ jhānaṃ, ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ, viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ, ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ, nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ, saññāvedayitanirodho – ime kho, bhikkhave, nava anupubbavihārā”ti. Paṭhamaṃ.

32. “Bhikkhus, there are these nine gradual abidings. What nine? The first jhāna, the second jhāna, the third jhāna, the fourth jhāna, the base of infinite space, the base of infinite consciousness, the base of nothingness, the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, and the cessation of perception and feeling. These, bhikkhus, are the nine gradual abidings.” First.

2. Anupubbavihārasamāpattisuttaṃ

2. The Discourse on the Attainments of Gradual Abidings

33. “Navayimā, bhikkhave nava bhikkhave (?), anupubbavihārasamāpattiyo desessāmi, taṃ suṇātha…pe… katamā ca, bhikkhave, nava anupubbavihārasamāpattiyo? Yattha kāmā nirujjhanti, ye ca kāme nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā tiṇṇā [P 4.0411] pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha kāmā nirujjhanti, ke ca kāme nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo – ‘idhāvuso[T 4.0425], bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja [M 3.0210] viharati. Ettha kāmā nirujjhanti, te ca kāme nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya anumodeyya; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya.

33. “Bhikkhus, I shall teach the nine attainments of gradual abidings. Listen to that… And what, bhikkhus, are the nine attainments of gradual abidings? Where sensual pleasures cease, and as for those who abide having caused sensual pleasures to cease again and again, I declare of them: ‘Surely, by means of that state, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say: ‘Where do sensual pleasures cease, and who are those who abide having caused sensual pleasures to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, quite secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, enters and abides in the first jhāna, which is accompanied by applied and sustained thought, with rapture and pleasure born of seclusion. Here sensual pleasures cease, and those are the ones who abide having caused sensual pleasures to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, a person who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms.

“Yattha vitakkavicārā nirujjhanti, ye ca vitakkavicāre nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā tiṇṇā pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha vitakkavicārā nirujjhanti, ke ca vitakkavicāre nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo [V 3.0220] – ‘idhāvuso, bhikkhu vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati; ettha vitakkavicārā nirujjhanti, te ca vitakkavicāre nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya anumodeyya; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya.

“Where applied and sustained thought cease, and as for those who abide having caused applied and sustained thought to cease again and again, I declare of them: ‘Surely, by means of that state, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say: ‘Where do applied and sustained thought cease, and who are those who abide having caused applied and sustained thought to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, with the stilling of applied and sustained thought… enters and abides in the second jhāna. Here applied and sustained thought cease, and those are the ones who abide having caused applied and sustained thought to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, a person who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms.

“Yattha pīti nirujjhati, ye ca pītiṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā tiṇṇā pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha pīti nirujjhati, ke ca pītiṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo – ‘idhāvuso, bhikkhu pītiyā ca virāgā…pe… [T 4.0426] tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati; ettha pīti nirujjhati, te ca pītiṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā [P 4.0412] viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya anumodeyya; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya.

“Where rapture ceases, and as for those who abide having caused rapture to cease again and again, I declare of them: ‘Surely, by means of that state, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say: ‘Where does rapture cease, and who are those who abide having caused rapture to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, with the fading away of rapture… enters and abides in the third jhāna. Here rapture ceases, and those are the ones who abide having caused rapture to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, a person who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms.

“Yattha upekkhāsukhaṃ nirujjhati, ye ca upekkhāsukhaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā tiṇṇā pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha upekkhāsukhaṃ nirujjhati, ke ca upekkhāsukhaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo – ‘idhāvuso, bhikkhu sukhassa ca pahānā…pe… catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati; ettha upekkhāsukhaṃ nirujjhati, te ca upekkhāsukhaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya [M 3.0211] anumodeyya; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya.

“Where the pleasure of equanimity ceases, and as for those who abide having caused the pleasure of equanimity to cease again and again, I declare of them: ‘Surely, by means of that state, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say: ‘Where does the pleasure of equanimity cease, and who are those who abide having caused the pleasure of equanimity to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, with the abandoning of pleasure… enters and abides in the fourth jhāna. Here the pleasure of equanimity ceases, and those are the ones who abide having caused the pleasure of equanimity to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, a person who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms.

“Yattha rūpasaññā nirujjhati, ye ca rūpasaññaṃ yattha rūpasaññā nirujjhanti, ye ca rūpasaññā (sī. syā. pī.) nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā tiṇṇā pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha rūpasaññā nirujjhati[V 3.0221], ke ca rūpasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo – ‘idhāvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ananto ākāsoti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ [T 4.0427] upasampajja viharati. Ettha rūpasaññā nirujjhati, te ca rūpasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya anumodeyya; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya.

“Where perception of form ceases, and as for those who abide having caused perception of form to cease again and again, I say: ‘Surely, by that factor, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say: ‘Where does perception of form cease, and who are those who abide having caused perception of form to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending perceptions of form, with the cessation of perceptions of resistance, and by not attending to perceptions of diversity, perceiving “Space is infinite,” enters and abides in the base of infinite space. Here perception of form ceases, and those are the ones who abide having caused perception of form to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, one who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, saying ‘Good!’, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms.

“Yattha [P 4.0413] ākāsānañcāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, ye ca ākāsānañcāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā tiṇṇā pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha ākāsānañcāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, ke ca ākāsānañcāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo – ‘idhāvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma anantaṃ viññāṇanti viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ettha ākāsānañcāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, te ca ākāsānañcāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya anumodeyya; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya.

“Where the perception of the base of infinite space ceases, and as for those who abide having caused the perception of the base of infinite space to cease again and again, I say: ‘Surely, by that factor, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say, ‘Where does the perception of the base of infinite space cease, and who are those who abide having caused the perception of the base of infinite space to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the base of infinite space, perceiving “Consciousness is infinite,” enters and abides in the base of infinite consciousness. Here the perception of the base of infinite space ceases, and those are the ones who abide having caused the perception of the base of infinite space to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, one who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, saying ‘Good!’, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms.

“Yattha viññāṇañcāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, ye ca viññāṇañcāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā [M 3.0212] tiṇṇā pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha viññāṇañcāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, ke ca viññāṇañcāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ [T 4.0428] na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo – ‘idhāvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma natthi kiñcīti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ettha viññāṇañcāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, te ca viññāṇañcāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya anumodeyya[V 3.0222]; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya.

“Where the perception of the base of infinite consciousness ceases, and as for those who abide having caused the perception of the base of infinite consciousness to cease again and again, I say: ‘Surely, by that factor, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say, ‘Where does the perception of the base of infinite consciousness cease, and who are those who abide having caused the perception of the base of infinite consciousness to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the base of infinite consciousness, perceiving “There is nothing,” enters and abides in the base of nothingness. Here the perception of the base of infinite consciousness ceases, and those are the ones who abide having caused the perception of the base of infinite consciousness to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, one who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, saying ‘Good!’, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms.

“Yattha ākiñcaññāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, ye ca ākiñcaññāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā tiṇṇā pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha ākiñcaññāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, ke ca ākiñcaññāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ [P 4.0414] na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo – ‘idhāvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ettha ākiñcaññāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, te ca ākiñcaññāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya anumodeyya; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya.

“Where the perception of the base of nothingness ceases, and as for those who abide having caused the perception of the base of nothingness to cease again and again, I say: ‘Surely, by that factor, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say, ‘Where does the perception of the base of nothingness cease, and who are those who abide having caused the perception of the base of nothingness to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the base of nothingness, enters and abides in the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. Here the perception of the base of nothingness ceases, and those are the ones who abide having caused the perception of the base of nothingness to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, one who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, saying ‘Good!’, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms.

“Yattha nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, ye ca nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti, ‘addhā te [T 4.0429] āyasmanto nicchātā nibbutā tiṇṇā pāraṅgatā tadaṅgenā’ti vadāmi. ‘Kattha nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, ke ca nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā nirodhetvā viharanti – ahametaṃ na jānāmi ahametaṃ na passāmī’ti, iti yo evaṃ vadeyya, so evamassa vacanīyo – ‘idhāvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ettha nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasaññā nirujjhati, te ca nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasaññaṃ nirodhetvā [M 3.0213] nirodhetvā viharantī’ti. Addhā, bhikkhave, asaṭho amāyāvī ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinandeyya anumodeyya; ‘sādhū’ti bhāsitaṃ abhinanditvā anumoditvā namassamāno pañjaliko payirupāseyya. Imā kho, bhikkhave, nava anupubbavihārasamāpattiyo”ti. Dutiyaṃ.

“Where the perception of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception ceases, and as for those who abide having caused the perception of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception to cease again and again, I say: ‘Surely, by that factor, those venerable ones are without craving, are extinguished, have crossed over, and have gone to the other shore.’ If someone were to say, ‘Where does the perception of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception cease, and who are those who abide having caused the perception of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception to cease again and again? I do not know this, I do not see this,’ he should be told thus: ‘Here, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, enters and abides in the cessation of perception and feeling. Here the perception of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception ceases, and those are the ones who abide having caused the perception of the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception to cease again and again.’ Surely, bhikkhus, one who is honest and not deceitful would rejoice in and approve of what was said, saying, ‘Good!’; and having rejoiced in and approved of what was said, saying ‘Good!’, he should attend upon him, paying homage with joined palms. These, bhikkhus, are the nine successive attainments of abiding.” The Second.

3. Nibbānasukhasuttaṃ

3. The Discourse on the Happiness of Nibbāna

34. Ekaṃ samayaṃ āyasmā sāriputto rājagahe viharati veḷuvane kalandakanivāpe. Tatra kho āyasmā sāriputto bhikkhū āmantesi – “sukhamidaṃ, āvuso, nibbānaṃ. Sukhamidaṃ[V 3.0223], āvuso, nibbāna”nti. Evaṃ vutte āyasmā udāyī āyasmantaṃ sāriputtaṃ etadavoca – “kiṃ [P 4.0415] panettha, āvuso sāriputta, sukhaṃ yadettha natthi vedayita”nti? “Etadeva khvettha, āvuso, sukhaṃ yadettha natthi vedayitaṃ. Pañcime, āvuso, kāmaguṇā. Katame pañca? Cakkhuviññeyyā rūpā iṭṭhā kantā manāpā piyarūpā kāmūpasaṃhitā rajanīyā, sotaviññeyyā saddā…pe… ghānaviññeyyā gandhā… jivhāviññeyyā rasā… kāyaviññeyyā phoṭṭhabbā [T 4.0430] iṭṭhā kantā manāpā piyarūpā kāmūpasaṃhitā rajanīyā – ime kho, āvuso, pañca kāmaguṇā. Yaṃ kho, āvuso, ime pañca kāmaguṇe paṭicca uppajjati sukhaṃ somanassaṃ, idaṃ vuccatāvuso, kāmasukhaṃ.

34. At one time the Venerable Sāriputta was dwelling at Rājagaha in the Bamboo Grove, the Squirrels’ Sanctuary. There the Venerable Sāriputta addressed the bhikkhus: “Friends, this Nibbāna is happiness. Friends, this Nibbāna is happiness.” When this was said, the Venerable Udāyī said to the Venerable Sāriputta: “But what happiness is there in this, friend Sāriputta, since there is no feeling here?” “Just this, friend, is the happiness here: that there is no feeling. Friend, there are these five cords of sensual pleasure. What five? Forms cognizable by the eye that are wished for, desired, agreeable, pleasing, connected with sensual desire, and alluring; sounds cognizable by the ear… odors cognizable by the nose… tastes cognizable by the tongue… tactile objects cognizable by the body that are wished for, desired, agreeable, pleasing, connected with sensual desire, and alluring—these, friend, are the five cords of sensual pleasure. Friend, whatever pleasure and joy arises in dependence on these five cords of sensual pleasure, this is called the happiness of sensual pleasure.

“Idhāvuso, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi…pe… paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Tassa ce, āvuso, bhikkhuno iminā vihārena viharato kāmasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti, svassa hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, āvuso, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa te kāmasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa hoti ābādho. Yo kho panāvuso, ābādho dukkhametaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbānaṃ.

Here, friend, a bhikkhu, quite secluded from sensual pleasures… enters and dwells in the first jhāna. If, friend, while that bhikkhu is dwelling thus, perceptions and acts of attention associated with sensual pleasure arise in him, that is an affliction for him. Just as, friend, suffering might arise for a happy person, simply for the sake of affliction; so too those perceptions and acts of attention associated with sensual pleasure that arise in him are an affliction for him. And whatever is an affliction, friend, this has been called suffering by the Blessed One. It is in this way too, friend, that it is to be known how Nibbāna is happiness.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Tassa ce, āvuso, bhikkhuno iminā vihārena viharato vitakkasahagatā saññāmanasikārā [P 4.0416] samudācaranti, svassa hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, āvuso, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa te vitakkasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti[M 3.0214]. Svassa hoti ābādho. Yo kho panāvuso, ābādho dukkhametaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbānaṃ.

Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, with the stilling of initial and sustained thought… enters and dwells in the second jhāna. If, friend, while that bhikkhu is dwelling thus, perceptions and acts of attention associated with initial thought arise in him, that is an affliction for him. Just as, friend, suffering might arise for a happy person, simply for the sake of affliction; so too those perceptions and acts of attention associated with initial thought that arise in him are an affliction for him. And whatever is an affliction, friend, this has been called suffering by the Blessed One. It is in this way too, friend, that it is to be known how Nibbāna is happiness.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu pītiyā ca virāgā…pe… tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Tassa ce, āvuso, bhikkhuno iminā vihārena viharato [T 4.0431] pītisahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti, svassa hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi[V 3.0224], āvuso, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa te pītisahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa hoti ābādho. Yo kho panāvuso, ābādho dukkhametaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbānaṃ.

Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, with the fading away of rapture… enters and dwells in the third jhāna. If, friend, while that bhikkhu is dwelling thus, perceptions and acts of attention associated with rapture arise in him, that is an affliction for him. Just as, friend, suffering might arise for a happy person, simply for the sake of affliction; so too those perceptions and acts of attention associated with rapture that arise in him are an affliction for him. And whatever is an affliction, friend, this has been called suffering by the Blessed One. It is in this way too, friend, that it is to be known how Nibbāna is happiness.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sukhassa ca pahānā…pe… catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Tassa ce, āvuso, bhikkhuno iminā vihārena viharato upekkhāsahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti, svassa hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, āvuso, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa te upekkhāsahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa hoti ābādho. Yo kho panāvuso, ābādho dukkhametaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbānaṃ.

Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, with the abandoning of pleasure… enters and dwells in the fourth jhāna. If, friend, while that bhikkhu is dwelling thus, perceptions and acts of attention associated with equanimity arise in him, that is an affliction for him. Just as, friend, suffering might arise for a happy person, simply for the sake of affliction; so too those perceptions and acts of attention associated with equanimity that arise in him are an affliction for him. And whatever is an affliction, friend, this has been called suffering by the Blessed One. It is in this way too, friend, that it is to be known how Nibbāna is happiness.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ananto ākāsoti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Tassa ce, āvuso, bhikkhuno iminā vihārena viharato rūpasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti, svassa hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, āvuso, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa te [P 4.0417] rūpasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti[T 4.0432]. Svassa hoti ābādho. Yo kho panāvuso, ābādho dukkhametaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbānaṃ.

Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, with the complete transcendence of perceptions of form, with the passing away of perceptions of resistance, with non-attention to perceptions of diversity, aware that ‘space is infinite,’ enters and dwells in the base of the infinity of space. If, friend, while that bhikkhu is dwelling thus, perceptions and acts of attention associated with form arise in him, that is an affliction for him. Just as, friend, suffering might arise for a happy person, simply for the sake of affliction; so too those perceptions and acts of attention associated with form that arise in him are an affliction for him. And whatever is an affliction, friend, this has been called suffering by the Blessed One. It is in this way too, friend, that it is to be known how Nibbāna is happiness.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma anantaṃ viññāṇanti viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Tassa ce[M 3.0215], āvuso, bhikkhuno iminā vihārena viharato ākāsānañcāyatanasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti, svassa hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, āvuso, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa te ākāsānañcāyatanasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa hoti ābādho. Yo kho panāvuso, ābādho dukkhametaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbānaṃ.

Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the base of the infinity of space, aware that ‘consciousness is infinite,’ enters and dwells in the base of the infinity of consciousness. If, friend, while that bhikkhu is dwelling thus, perceptions and acts of attention associated with the base of the infinity of space arise in him, that is an affliction for him. Just as, friend, suffering might arise for a happy person, simply for the sake of affliction; so too those perceptions and acts of attention associated with the base of the infinity of space that arise in him are an affliction for him. And whatever is an affliction, friend, this has been called suffering by the Blessed One. It is in this way too, friend, that it is to be known how Nibbāna is happiness.

“Puna [V 3.0225] caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma, natthi kiñcīti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Tassa ce, āvuso, bhikkhuno iminā vihārena viharato viññāṇañcāyatanasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti, svassa hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, āvuso, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa te viññāṇañcāyatanasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa hoti ābādho. Yo kho panāvuso, ābādho dukkhametaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbānaṃ.

Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the base of the infinity of consciousness and cultivating the thought 'there is nothing,' enters and dwells in the base of nothingness. If, friend, while that bhikkhu is dwelling in this state, perceptions and attentions associated with the base of the infinity of consciousness should arise, that is an affliction for him. Just as, friend, for a happy person pain might arise solely for the sake of affliction, so too, those perceptions and attentions associated with the base of the infinity of consciousness arise for him. That is an affliction for him. Indeed, friend, whatever is an affliction has been declared by the Blessed One to be suffering. It is also by this method, friend, that it should be understood how Nibbāna is happiness.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ [T 4.0433] upasampajja viharati. Tassa ce, āvuso, bhikkhuno iminā vihārena viharato ākiñcaññāyatanasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti, svassa hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, āvuso, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa te ākiñcaññāyatanasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa hoti ābādho. Yo kho panāvuso, ābādho dukkhametaṃ vuttaṃ bhagavatā. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbānaṃ.

Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the base of nothingness, enters and dwells in the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. If, friend, while that bhikkhu is dwelling in this state, perceptions and attentions associated with the base of nothingness should arise, that is an affliction for him. Just as, friend, for a happy person pain might arise solely for the sake of affliction, so too, those perceptions and attentions associated with the base of nothingness arise for him. That is an affliction for him. Indeed, friend, whatever is an affliction has been declared by the Blessed One to be suffering. It is also by this method, friend, that it should be understood how Nibbāna is happiness.

“Puna [P 4.0418] caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ upasampajja viharati, paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti. Imināpi kho etaṃ, āvuso, pariyāyena veditabbaṃ yathā sukhaṃ nibbāna”nti. Tatiyaṃ.

Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, enters and dwells in the cessation of perception and feeling. And for him, having seen with wisdom, the taints are utterly destroyed. It is also by this method, friend, that it should be understood how Nibbāna is happiness.

4. Gāvīupamāsuttaṃ

4. 4. The Discourse with the Simile of the Cow

35. “Seyyathāpi[M 3.0216], bhikkhave, gāvī pabbateyyā bālā abyattā akhettaññū akusalā visame pabbate carituṃ. Tassā evamassa – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ agatapubbañceva disaṃ gaccheyyaṃ, akhāditapubbāni ca tiṇāni khādeyyaṃ, apītapubbāni ca pānīyāni piveyya’nti. Sā purimaṃ pādaṃ na suppatiṭṭhitaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā pacchimaṃ pādaṃ uddhareyya. Sā na ceva agatapubbaṃ disaṃ gaccheyya, na ca akhāditapubbāni tiṇāni khādeyya, na ca apītapubbāni pānīyāni piveyya; yasmiṃ cassā padese ṭhitāya evamassa – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ agatapubbañceva disaṃ gaccheyyaṃ, akhāditapubbāni [V 3.0226] ca tiṇāni khādeyyaṃ, apītapubbāni [T 4.0434] ca pānīyāni piveyya’nti tañca padesaṃ na sotthinā paccāgaccheyya. Taṃ kissa hetu? Tathā hi sā, bhikkhave, gāvī pabbateyyā bālā abyattā akhettaññū akusalā visame pabbate carituṃ. Evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, idhekacco bhikkhu bālo abyatto akhettaññū akusalo vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati; so taṃ nimittaṃ na āsevati na bhāveti na bahulīkaroti na svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

35. Bhikkhus, it is like a mountain cow that is foolish, inept, unfamiliar with its territory, and unskilled in roaming on rugged mountains. It might occur to her: 'What if I were to go to a region I have never gone to before, eat grass I have never eaten before, and drink water I have never drunk before?' Without having firmly planted her front foot, she would lift her back foot. She would not get to go to a region she had never gone to before, nor get to eat grass she had never eaten before, nor get to drink water she had never drunk before. And from the spot where she stood when it occurred to her, 'What if I were to go to a region I have never gone to before, eat grass I have never eaten before, and drink water I have never drunk before?,' she would not return safely to that same spot. For what reason? Because, bhikkhus, that mountain cow is foolish, inept, unfamiliar with its territory, and unskilled in roaming on rugged mountains. In the same way, bhikkhus, some bhikkhu here is foolish, inept, unfamiliar with the territory, and unskilled. Quite secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, he enters and dwells in the first jhāna, which is accompanied by directed thought and evaluation, with rapture and happiness born of seclusion. He does not resort to that sign, does not develop it, does not practice it frequently, and does not establish it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā ajjhattaṃ sampasādanaṃ cetaso ekodibhāvaṃ avitakkaṃ avicāraṃ samādhijaṃ pītisukhaṃ dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja [P 4.0419] vihareyya’nti. So na sakkoti vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharituṃ. Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So na sakkoti vivicceva kāmehi…pe… paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharituṃ. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, ‘bhikkhu ubhato bhaṭṭho ubhato parihīno, seyyathāpi sā gāvī pabbateyyā bālā abyattā akhettaññū akusalā visame pabbate carituṃ’”.

It occurs to him: 'What if, with the stilling of directed thought and evaluation, I were to enter and dwell in the second jhāna, which has internal confidence and unification of mind, is without directed thought and evaluation, and has rapture and happiness born of concentration?' He is not able to, with the stilling of directed thought and evaluation… enter and dwell in the second jhāna. It occurs to him: 'What if, quite secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states… I were to enter and dwell in the first jhāna?' He is not able to, quite secluded from sensual pleasures… enter and dwell in the first jhāna. This bhikkhu, bhikkhus, is said to be one who has failed in both and lost both, just like that mountain cow that is foolish, inept, unfamiliar with its territory, and unskilled in roaming on rugged mountains.

“Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, gāvī pabbateyyā paṇḍitā byattā khettaññū kusalā visame pabbate carituṃ. Tassā evamassa – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ agatapubbañceva disaṃ gaccheyyaṃ, akhāditapubbāni ca tiṇāni khādeyyaṃ, apītapubbāni [T 4.0435] ca pānīyāni piveyya’nti. Sā purimaṃ pādaṃ suppatiṭṭhitaṃ patiṭṭhāpetvā pacchimaṃ pādaṃ uddhareyya. Sā agatapubbañceva disaṃ gaccheyya, akhāditapubbāni ca tiṇāni [M 3.0217] khādeyya, apītapubbāni ca pānīyāni piveyya. Yasmiṃ cassā padese ṭhitāya evamassa – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ agatapubbañceva disaṃ gaccheyyaṃ, akhāditapubbāni ca tiṇāni khādeyyaṃ, apītapubbāni ca pānīyāni piveyya’nti tañca padesaṃ sotthinā paccāgaccheyya. Taṃ kissa hetu? Tathā hi sā, bhikkhave, gāvī pabbateyyā paṇḍitā byattā khettaññū kusalā visame pabbate carituṃ. Evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, idhekacco bhikkhu paṇḍito byatto khettaññū kusalo vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ [V 3.0227] savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So taṃ nimittaṃ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

Bhikkhus, it is like a mountain cow that is wise, competent, familiar with its territory, and skilled in roaming on rugged mountains. It might occur to her: 'What if I were to go to a region I have never gone to before, eat grass I have never eaten before, and drink water I have never drunk before?' Having firmly planted her front foot, she would lift her back foot. She would get to go to a region she had never gone to before, get to eat grass she had never eaten before, and get to drink water she had never drunk before. And from the spot where she stood when it occurred to her, 'What if I were to go to a region I have never gone to before, eat grass I have never eaten before, and drink water I have never drunk before?,' she would return safely to that same spot. For what reason? Because, bhikkhus, that mountain cow is wise, competent, familiar with its territory, and skilled in roaming on rugged mountains. In the same way, bhikkhus, some bhikkhu here is wise, competent, familiar with the territory, and skilled. Quite secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, he enters and dwells in the first jhāna, which is accompanied by directed thought and evaluation, with rapture and happiness born of seclusion. He resorts to that sign, develops it, practices it frequently, and establishes it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā ajjhattaṃ sampasādanaṃ cetaso ekodibhāvaṃ avitakkaṃ avicāraṃ samādhijaṃ pītisukhaṃ dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ anabhihiṃsamāno vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So taṃ nimittaṃ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

To him, this thought occurs: 'It would be good if, with the stilling of applied and sustained thought, I might attain and dwell in the second jhāna, which brings internal confidence and unification of mind, is without applied and sustained thought, and has rapture and happiness born of concentration.' Not disparaging the second jhāna, with the stilling of applied and sustained thought… he attains and dwells in the second jhāna. He frequents that sign, develops it, practices it repeatedly, and establishes it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ pītiyā ca virāgā upekkhako ca vihareyyaṃ sato ca sampajāno, sukhañca kāyena paṭisaṃvedeyyaṃ yaṃ taṃ ariyā ācikkhanti – upekkhako satimā sukhavihārīti tatiyaṃ [P 4.0420] jhānaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ anabhihiṃsamāno pītiyā ca virāgā…pe… [T 4.0436] tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So taṃ nimittaṃ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

To him, this thought occurs: 'It would be good if, with the fading away of rapture, I might dwell equanimous, mindful, and clearly comprehending, and experience happiness with the body, that of which the Noble Ones declare: “Equanimous and mindful, one dwells in happiness.” It would be good if I might attain and dwell in the third jhāna.' Not disparaging the third jhāna, and with the fading away of rapture… he attains and dwells in the third jhāna. He frequents that sign, develops it, practices it repeatedly, and establishes it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ sukhassa ca pahānā dukkhassa ca pahānā pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So catutthaṃ jhānaṃ anabhihiṃsamāno sukhassa ca pahānā…pe… catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So taṃ nimittaṃ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

To him, this thought occurs: 'It would be good if, with the abandoning of pleasure and pain, and with the previous disappearance of joy and displeasure, I might attain and dwell in the fourth jhāna, which is neither-painful-nor-pleasant and has purity of mindfulness due to equanimity.' Not disparaging the fourth jhāna, with the abandoning of pleasure and pain… he attains and dwells in the fourth jhāna. He frequents that sign, develops it, practices it repeatedly, and establishes it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ananto ākāsoti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ [M 3.0218] anabhihiṃsamāno sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā [V 3.0228] …pe… ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So taṃ nimittaṃ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

To him, this thought occurs: 'It would be good if, by completely transcending perceptions of form, with the disappearance of perceptions of resistance, and by not attending to perceptions of diversity, perceiving “Space is infinite,” I might attain and dwell in the sphere of infinite space.' Not disparaging the sphere of infinite space, by completely transcending perceptions of form… he attains and dwells in the sphere of infinite space. He frequents that sign, develops it, practices it repeatedly, and establishes it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma anantaṃ viññāṇanti viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ anabhihiṃsamāno sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘anantaṃ viññāṇa’nti viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So taṃ nimittaṃ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

To him, this thought occurs: 'It would be good if, by completely transcending the sphere of infinite space, perceiving “Consciousness is infinite,” I might attain and dwell in the sphere of infinite consciousness.' Not disparaging the sphere of infinite consciousness, by completely transcending the sphere of infinite space and perceiving “Consciousness is infinite,” he attains and dwells in the sphere of infinite consciousness. He frequents that sign, develops it, practices it repeatedly, and establishes it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma natthi kiñcīti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ anabhihiṃsamāno sabbaso [T 4.0437] viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘natthi kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So taṃ nimittaṃ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

To him, this thought occurs: 'It would be good if, by completely transcending the sphere of infinite consciousness, perceiving “There is nothing,” I might attain and dwell in the sphere of nothingness.' Not disparaging the sphere of nothingness, by completely transcending the sphere of infinite consciousness and perceiving “There is nothing,” he attains and dwells in the sphere of nothingness. He frequents that sign, develops it, practices it repeatedly, and establishes it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ anabhihiṃsamāno sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So taṃ nimittaṃ āsevati bhāveti bahulīkaroti [P 4.0421] svādhiṭṭhitaṃ adhiṭṭhāti.

To him, this thought occurs: 'It would be good if, by completely transcending the sphere of nothingness, I might attain and dwell in the sphere of neither-perception-nor-non-perception.' Not disparaging the sphere of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, by completely transcending the sphere of nothingness, he attains and dwells in the sphere of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. He frequents that sign, develops it, practices it repeatedly, and establishes it well.

“Tassa evaṃ hoti – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. So saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ anabhihiṃsamāno sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ upasampajja viharati.

To him, this thought occurs: 'It would be good if, by completely transcending the sphere of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, I might attain and dwell in the cessation of perception and feeling.' Not disparaging the cessation of perception and feeling, by completely transcending the sphere of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, he attains and dwells in the cessation of perception and feeling.

“Yato kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu taṃ tadeva samāpattiṃ samāpajjatipi vuṭṭhātipi, tassa mudu cittaṃ hoti kammaññaṃ. Mudunā kammaññena cittena appamāṇo samādhi hoti subhāvito. So appamāṇena samādhinā subhāvitena yassa yassa abhiññāsacchikaraṇīyassa dhammassa cittaṃ [M 3.0219] abhininnāmeti [V 3.0229] abhiññāsacchikiriyāya tatra tatreva sakkhibhabbataṃ pāpuṇāti sati sati āyatane.

Bhikkhus, when a monk both enters into and emerges from each of those attainments, his mind becomes soft and workable. With a soft and workable mind, his immeasurable concentration becomes well-developed. With that immeasurable, well-developed concentration, whatever phenomenon is to be realized by direct knowledge, he inclines his mind toward realizing it by direct knowledge, and he becomes capable of witnessing it in every case, there being a basis.

“So sace ākaṅkhati – ‘anekavihitaṃ iddhividhaṃ paccanubhaveyyaṃ, ekopi hutvā bahudhā assaṃ, bahudhāpi hutvā eko assaṃ…pe… yāva brahmalokāpi kāyena vasaṃ vatteyya’nti, tatra tatreva sakkhibhabbataṃ pāpuṇāti sati sati āyatane.

If he wishes: 'I would experience the various kinds of psychic power: having been one, I would become many; having been many, I would become one… I would exercise mastery with the body even up to the Brahmā world,' he becomes capable of witnessing it in every case, there being a basis.

“So sace ākaṅkhati – dibbāya [T 4.0438] sotadhātuyā…pe… sati sati āyatane.

If he wishes: 'With the divine ear element…,' he becomes capable of witnessing it in every case, there being a basis.

“So sace ākaṅkhati – ‘parasattānaṃ parapuggalānaṃ cetasā ceto paricca pajāneyyaṃ, sarāgaṃ vā cittaṃ sarāgaṃ cittanti pajāneyyaṃ, vītarāgaṃ vā cittaṃ vītarāgaṃ cittanti pajāneyyaṃ, sadosaṃ vā cittaṃ sadosaṃ cittanti pajāneyyaṃ, vītadosaṃ vā cittaṃ vītadosaṃ cittanti pajāneyyaṃ, samohaṃ vā cittaṃ samohaṃ cittanti pajāneyyaṃ, vītamohaṃ vā cittaṃ… saṃkhittaṃ vā cittaṃ… vikkhittaṃ vā cittaṃ… mahaggataṃ vā cittaṃ… amahaggataṃ vā cittaṃ… sauttaraṃ vā cittaṃ… anuttaraṃ vā cittaṃ… samāhitaṃ vā cittaṃ… asamāhitaṃ vā cittaṃ… vimuttaṃ vā cittaṃ… avimuttaṃ vā cittaṃ avimuttaṃ cittanti pajāneyya’nti, tatra tatreva sakkhibhabbataṃ pāpuṇāti sati sati āyatane.

If he wishes: ‘May I know the minds of other beings, of other individuals, having encompassed them with my own mind—may I know a mind with lust as “a mind with lust,” or a mind without lust as “a mind without lust”; a mind with hate as “a mind with hate,” or a mind without hate as “a mind without hate”; a mind with delusion as “a mind with delusion,” or a mind without delusion… a constricted mind… a scattered mind… an exalted mind… an unexalted mind… a surpassable mind… an unsurpassable mind… a concentrated mind… an unconcentrated mind… a liberated mind… or an unliberated mind as “an unliberated mind”’—he attains the capability to witness it directly in each and every case, when the basis for it exists.
