“So sace ākaṅkhati – ‘anekavihitaṃ pubbenivāsaṃ anussareyyaṃ, seyyathidaṃ – ekampi jātiṃ dvepi jātiyo…pe… iti sākāraṃ sauddesaṃ anekavihitaṃ pubbenivāsaṃ anussareyya’nti, tatra tatreva sakkhibhabbataṃ pāpuṇāti sati sati āyatane.

If he wishes: ‘May I recollect my manifold past lives, that is, one birth, two births… and so on… thus with their aspects and details may I recollect my manifold past lives’—he attains the capability to witness it directly in each and every case, when the basis for it exists.

“So [P 4.0422] sace ākaṅkhati – ‘dibbena cakkhunā visuddhena atikkantamānusakena…pe… yathākammūpage satte pajāneyya’nti, tatra tatreva sakkhibhabbataṃ pāpuṇāti sati sati āyatane.

If he wishes: ‘With the divine eye, purified and surpassing the human… and so on… may I know beings faring according to their kamma’—he attains the capability to witness it directly in each and every case, when the basis for it exists.

“So sace ākaṅkhati – ‘āsavānaṃ khayā anāsavaṃ cetovimuttiṃ paññāvimuttiṃ diṭṭheva dhamme sayaṃ abhiññā sacchikatvā upasampajja vihareyya’nti, tatra tatreva sakkhibhabbataṃ pāpuṇāti sati sati āyatane”ti. Catutthaṃ.

If he wishes: ‘By the destruction of the taints, having realized for myself with direct knowledge, may I enter and dwell in this very life in the taintless liberation of mind, liberation by wisdom’—he attains the capability to witness it directly in each and every case, when the basis for it exists.” Fourth.

5. Jhānasuttaṃ

5. The Discourse on Jhāna

36. “Paṭhamampāhaṃ[V 3.0230][M 3.0220], bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi[T 4.0439]; dutiyampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi; tatiyampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi; catutthampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi; ākāsānañcāyatanampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi; viññāṇañcāyatanampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi; ākiñcaññāyatanampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi; nevasaññānāsaññāyatanampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi; saññāvedayitanirodhampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmi.

36. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the first jhāna. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the second jhāna. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the third jhāna. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the fourth jhāna. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the base of infinite space. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the base of infinite consciousness. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the base of nothingness. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the cessation of perception and feeling.

“‘Paṭhamampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi…pe… paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So yadeva tattha hoti rūpagataṃ vedanāgataṃ saññāgataṃ saṅkhāragataṃ viññāṇagataṃ, te dhamme aniccato dukkhato rogato gaṇḍato sallato aghato ābādhato parato palokato suññato [P 4.0423] anattato samanupassati. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpeti patiṭṭhāpeti (syā.), paṭipādeti (ka.) ma. ni. 2.133 passitabbaṃ. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpetvā patiṭṭhāpetvā (syā.), paṭipādetvā (ka.) amatāya dhātuyā cittaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇītaṃ yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbāna’nti. So tattha ṭhito āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti. No ce āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti, teneva dhammarāgena tāya dhammanandiyā pañcannaṃ orambhāgiyānaṃ saṃyojanānaṃ parikkhayā opapātiko hoti tattha parinibbāyī anāvattidhammo tasmā lokā.

‘Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the first jhāna.’ This was said. In reference to what was it said? Here, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu, quite secluded from sensual pleasures… and so on… enters and dwells in the first jhāna. He regards whatever phenomena are found there connected with form, feeling, perception, volitional formations, and consciousness as impermanent, as suffering, as a disease, as a boil, as a dart, as misery, as an affliction, as alien, as disintegrating, as empty, as non-self. He turns his mind away from those phenomena. Having turned his mind away from them, he directs it to the deathless element: ‘This is the peaceful, this is the sublime, that is, the stilling of all formations, the relinquishing of all acquisitions, the destruction of craving, dispassion, cessation, Nibbāna.’ Established in that, he attains the destruction of the taints. If he does not attain the destruction of the taints, then with the utter destruction of the five lower fetters, through that passion for the Dhamma, that delight in the Dhamma, he becomes one of spontaneous birth, due to attain final Nibbāna there, without returning from that world.

“Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, issāso vā issāsantevāsī vā tiṇapurisarūpake vā mattikāpuñje vā yoggaṃ karitvā, so aparena samayena dūrepātī ca hoti akkhaṇavedhī ca mahato ca kāyassa padāletā padālitā (ka.) a. ni. 3.134; 4.181; evamevaṃ kho[T 4.0440], bhikkhave, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi…pe… paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja [M 3.0221] viharati. So yadeva tattha hoti rūpagataṃ vedanāgataṃ saññāgataṃ saṅkhāragataṃ viññāṇagataṃ, te dhamme aniccato dukkhato rogato gaṇḍato sallato aghato ābādhato parato palokato suññato anattato samanupassati. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpeti[V 3.0231]. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpetvā amatāya dhātuyā cittaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇītaṃ yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbāna’nti. So tattha ṭhito āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti. No ce āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti, teneva dhammarāgena tāya dhammanandiyā pañcannaṃ orambhāgiyānaṃ saṃyojanānaṃ parikkhayā opapātiko hoti tattha parinibbāyī anāvattidhammo tasmā lokā. ‘Paṭhamampāhaṃ[P 4.0424], bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

Suppose, bhikkhus, an archer or an archer’s apprentice has practiced on a straw man or a clay mound. After a time, he becomes a long-range shooter, a rapid-fire shooter, and a piercer of great masses. So too, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu, quite secluded from sensual pleasures… and so on… enters and dwells in the first jhāna. He regards whatever phenomena are found there connected with form, feeling, perception, volitional formations, and consciousness as impermanent, as suffering, as a disease, as a boil, as a dart, as misery, as an affliction, as alien, as disintegrating, as empty, as non-self. He turns his mind away from those phenomena. Having turned his mind away from them, he directs it to the deathless element: ‘This is the peaceful, this is the sublime, that is, the stilling of all formations, the relinquishing of all acquisitions, the destruction of craving, dispassion, cessation, Nibbāna.’ Established in that, he attains the destruction of the taints. If he does not attain the destruction of the taints, then with the utter destruction of the five lower fetters, through that passion for the Dhamma, that delight in the Dhamma, he becomes one of spontaneous birth, due to attain final Nibbāna there, without returning from that world. ‘Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the first jhāna’: it is in reference to this that this was said.

“Dutiyampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya…pe… tatiyampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya… ‘catutthampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sukhassa ca pahānā dukkhassa ca pahānā pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So yadeva tattha hoti rūpagataṃ vedanāgataṃ saññāgataṃ saṅkhāragataṃ viññāṇagataṃ, te dhamme aniccato dukkhato [T 4.0441] rogato gaṇḍato sallato aghato ābādhato parato palokato suññato anattato samanupassati. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpeti. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpetvā amatāya dhātuyā cittaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇītaṃ yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbāna’nti. So tattha ṭhito āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti. No ce āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti, teneva dhammarāgena tāya dhammanandiyā pañcannaṃ orambhāgiyānaṃ saṃyojanānaṃ parikkhayā opapātiko hoti tattha parinibbāyī anāvattidhammo tasmā lokā.

‘Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the second jhāna… and so on… by relying on the third jhāna… ‘Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the fourth jhāna.’ Indeed, this was said. And in reference to what was it said? Here, bhikkhus, with the abandoning of pleasure and pain, and with the previous disappearance of joy and displeasure, a bhikkhu enters and abides in the fourth jhāna, which is neither-painful-nor-pleasant and has purity of mindfulness due to equanimity. He thoroughly contemplates whatever phenomena are comprised therein—included in form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness—as impermanent, as suffering, as a disease, as a boil, as a dart, as misery, as an affliction, as alien, as disintegrating, as empty, as not-self. He turns his mind away from those phenomena. Having turned his mind away from them, he directs his mind to the deathless element: ‘This is peaceful, this is sublime, namely, the stilling of all formations, the relinquishing of all substrates of existence, the destruction of craving, dispassion, cessation, Nibbāna.’ Established therein, he attains the destruction of the taints. If he does not attain the destruction of the taints, then through that very passion for the Dhamma and that delight in the Dhamma, with the utter destruction of the five lower fetters, he is one of spontaneous birth, who will attain final Nibbāna there, not subject to returning from that world.

“Seyyathāpi[V 3.0232], bhikkhave, issāso vā issāsantevāsī vā tiṇapurisarūpake vā mattikāpuñje vā yoggaṃ karitvā, so aparena samayena dūrepātī ca hoti akkhaṇavedhī ca mahato ca kāyassa padāletā[M 3.0222]; evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sukhassa ca pahānā, dukkhassa ca pahānā, pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So yadeva tattha hoti rūpagataṃ vedanāgataṃ…pe… anāvattidhammo tasmā lokā. ‘Catutthampāhaṃ[P 4.0425], bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

Suppose, bhikkhus, an archer or an archer’s apprentice, having practiced on a straw man or a clay mound, at a later time becomes a long-range shooter, a swift piercer, and a splitter of a great body. So too, bhikkhus, with the abandoning of pleasure and pain, and with the previous disappearance of joy and displeasure, a bhikkhu enters and abides in the fourth jhāna, which is neither-painful-nor-pleasant and has purity of mindfulness due to equanimity. He thoroughly contemplates whatever phenomena are comprised therein—included in form, feeling… and so on… not subject to returning from that world. ‘Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the fourth jhāna.’ That which was spoken thus, was spoken in reference to this.

“‘Ākāsānañcāyatanampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, jhānaṃ nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ‘ananto ākāso’ti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So yadeva tattha hoti vedanāgataṃ saññāgataṃ saṅkhāragataṃ [T 4.0442] viññāṇagataṃ, te dhamme aniccato dukkhato rogato gaṇḍato sallato aghato ābādhato parato palokato suññato anattato samanupassati. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpeti. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpetvā amatāya dhātuyā cittaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇītaṃ yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbāna’nti. So tattha ṭhito āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti. No ce āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti, teneva dhammarāgena tāya dhammanandiyā pañcannaṃ orambhāgiyānaṃ saṃyojanānaṃ parikkhayā opapātiko hoti tattha parinibbāyī anāvattidhammo tasmā lokā.

‘Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the jhāna of the base of the infinity of space.’ Indeed, this was said. And in reference to what was it said? Here, bhikkhus, by completely transcending perceptions of form, with the disappearance of perceptions of resistance, and by not attending to perceptions of diversity, contemplating ‘space is infinite,’ a bhikkhu enters and abides in the base of the infinity of space. He thoroughly contemplates whatever phenomena are comprised therein—included in feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness—as impermanent, as suffering, as a disease, as a boil, as a dart, as misery, as an affliction, as alien, as disintegrating, as empty, as not-self. He turns his mind away from those phenomena. Having turned his mind away from them, he directs his mind to the deathless element: ‘This is peaceful, this is sublime, namely, the stilling of all formations, the relinquishing of all substrates of existence, the destruction of craving, dispassion, cessation, Nibbāna.’ Established therein, he attains the destruction of the taints. If he does not attain the destruction of the taints, then through that very passion for the Dhamma and that delight in the Dhamma, with the utter destruction of the five lower fetters, he is one of spontaneous birth, who will attain final Nibbāna there, not subject to returning from that world.

“Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, issāso vā issāsantevāsī vā tiṇapurisarūpake vā mattikāpuñje vā yoggaṃ karitvā, so aparena samayena dūrepātī ca hoti akkhaṇavedhī ca mahato ca kāyassa padāletā; evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ‘ananto ākāso’ti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So yadeva tattha hoti vedanāgataṃ saññāgataṃ…pe… anāvattidhammo tasmā lokā. ‘Ākāsānañcāyatanampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

Suppose, bhikkhus, an archer or an archer’s apprentice, having practiced on a straw man or a clay mound, at a later time becomes a long-range shooter, a swift piercer, and a splitter of a great body. So too, bhikkhus, by completely transcending perceptions of form, with the disappearance of perceptions of resistance, and by not attending to perceptions of diversity, contemplating ‘space is infinite,’ a bhikkhu enters and abides in the base of the infinity of space. He thoroughly contemplates whatever phenomena are comprised therein—included in feeling, perception… and so on… not subject to returning from that world. ‘Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the base of the infinity of space.’ That which was spoken thus, was spoken in reference to this.

“‘Viññāṇañcāyatanampāhaṃ[V 3.0233], bhikkhave, nissāya…pe… ākiñcaññāyatanampāhaṃ, bhikkhave, nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmī’ti, iti kho panetaṃ vuttaṃ[T 4.0443]. Kiñcetaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ? Idha[P 4.0426], bhikkhave, bhikkhu sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma [M 3.0223] ‘natthi kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So yadeva tattha hoti vedanāgataṃ saññāgataṃ saṅkhāragataṃ viññāṇagataṃ, te dhamme aniccato dukkhato rogato gaṇḍato sallato aghato ābādhato parato palokato suññato anattato samanupassati. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpeti. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpetvā amatāya dhātuyā cittaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇītaṃ yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbāna’nti. So tattha ṭhito āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti. No ce āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti, teneva dhammarāgena tāya dhammanandiyā pañcannaṃ orambhāgiyānaṃ saṃyojanānaṃ parikkhayā opapātiko hoti tattha parinibbāyī anāvattidhammo tasmā lokā.

‘Bhikkhus, I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the sphere of the infinity of consciousness... and by relying on the sphere of nothingness.’ Now, this was said, and in reference to what was it said? Here, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the sphere of the infinity of consciousness, developing the thought ‘There is nothing whatsoever,’ enters and abides in the sphere of nothingness. Whatever feeling, perception, volitional formations, or consciousness is associated with it, he thoroughly contemplates those phenomena as impermanent, as suffering, as a disease, as a tumor, as a dart, as misery, as an affliction, as other, as perishable, as empty, as not-self. He turns his mind away from those phenomena. Having turned his mind away from them, he directs it toward the deathless element: ‘This is the peaceful, this is the sublime—that is, the stilling of all formations, the relinquishing of all attachments, the destruction of craving, dispassion, cessation, Nibbāna.’ Established therein, he attains the destruction of the taints. If he does not attain the destruction of the taints, then through that very passion for the Dhamma and that delight in the Dhamma, with the utter destruction of the five lower fetters, he is of spontaneous birth, destined to attain final Nibbāna there, not subject to return from that world.

“Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, issāso vā issāsantevāsī vā tiṇapurisarūpake vā mattikāpuñje vā yoggaṃ karitvā, so aparena samayena dūrepātī ca hoti akkhaṇavedhī ca mahato ca kāyassa padāletā; evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘natthi kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So yadeva tattha hoti vedanāgataṃ saññāgataṃ saṅkhāragataṃ viññāṇagataṃ, te dhamme aniccato dukkhato rogato gaṇḍato sallato aghato ābādhato parato palokato suññato anattato samanupassati. So tehi dhammehi [T 4.0444] cittaṃ paṭivāpeti. So tehi dhammehi cittaṃ paṭivāpetvā amatāya dhātuyā cittaṃ upasaṃharati – ‘etaṃ santaṃ etaṃ paṇītaṃ yadidaṃ sabbasaṅkhārasamatho sabbūpadhipaṭinissaggo taṇhākkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbāna’nti. So tattha ṭhito āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti. No ce āsavānaṃ khayaṃ pāpuṇāti, teneva dhammarāgena tāya dhammanandiyā pañcannaṃ orambhāgiyānaṃ saṃyojanānaṃ parikkhayā opapātiko hoti tattha parinibbāyī anāvattidhammo tasmā lokā. ‘Ākiñcaññāyatanampāhaṃ, nissāya āsavānaṃ khayaṃ vadāmī’ti, iti yaṃ taṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ.

Suppose, bhikkhus, an archer or an archer’s apprentice, having practiced on a straw man or a clay mound, would in time become one who shoots from a distance, one who can pierce in an instant, and a splitter of large bodies. So too, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the sphere of the infinity of consciousness, developing the thought ‘There is nothing whatsoever,’ enters and abides in the sphere of nothingness. Whatever feeling, perception, volitional formations, or consciousness is associated with it, he thoroughly contemplates those phenomena as impermanent, as suffering, as a disease, as a tumor, as a dart, as misery, as an affliction, as other, as perishable, as empty, as not-self. He turns his mind away from those phenomena. Having turned his mind away from them, he directs it toward the deathless element: ‘This is the peaceful, this is the sublime—that is, the stilling of all formations, the relinquishing of all attachments, the destruction of craving, dispassion, cessation, Nibbāna.’ Established therein, he attains the destruction of the taints. If he does not attain the destruction of the taints, then through that very passion for the Dhamma and that delight in the Dhamma, with the utter destruction of the five lower fetters, he is of spontaneous birth, destined to attain final Nibbāna there, not subject to return from that world. ‘I declare the destruction of the taints by relying on the sphere of nothingness.’ It was in reference to this that this was said.

“Iti kho, bhikkhave, yāvatā saññāsamāpatti tāvatā aññāpaṭivedho. Yāni ca kho imāni, bhikkhave, nissāya dve āyatanāni – nevasaññānāsaññāyatanasamāpatti ca saññāvedayitanirodho [V 3.0234] ca, jhāyīhete[M 3.0224], bhikkhave, samāpattikusalehi samāpattivuṭṭhānakusalehi samāpajjitvā vuṭṭhahitvā sammā akkhātabbānīti vadāmī”ti. Pañcamaṃ.

Thus, bhikkhus, as far as the attainments with perception extend, so far extends the penetration of final knowledge. As for these two bases—the attainment of the sphere of neither-perception-nor-non-perception and the cessation of perception and feeling—I say they are to be properly explained by those who delight in jhāna, who are skilled in the attainment and in emerging from it, having entered and emerged from them. The fifth.

6. Ānandasuttaṃ

6. The Discourse with Ānanda

37. Ekaṃ samayaṃ āyasmā ānando kosambiyaṃ viharati ghositārāme. Tatra kho āyasmā ānando bhikkhū āmantesi – “āvuso bhikkhave”ti. “Āvuso”ti kho te bhikkhū āyasmato ānandassa paccassosuṃ. Āyasmā ānando etadavoca –

37. At one time the Venerable Ānanda was dwelling at Kosambī in Ghosita’s Park. There the Venerable Ānanda addressed the bhikkhus: “Friends, bhikkhus!” “Friend,” those bhikkhus replied to the Venerable Ānanda. The Venerable Ānanda said this:

“Acchariyaṃ, āvuso, abbhutaṃ, āvuso! Yāvañcidaṃ tena bhagavatā jānatā passatā arahatā sammāsambuddhena sambādhe okāsādhigamo [T 4.0445] anubuddho sattānaṃ visuddhiyā sokaparidevānaṃ samatikkamāya dukkhadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamāya ñāyassa adhigamāya nibbānassa sacchikiriyāya. Tadeva nāma cakkhuṃ bhavissati te rūpā tañcāyatanaṃ [P 4.0427] no paṭisaṃvedissati paṭisaṃvedayati (ka.). Tadeva nāma sotaṃ bhavissati te saddā tañcāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedissati. Tadeva nāma ghānaṃ bhavissati te gandhā tañcāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedissati. Sāva nāma jivhā bhavissati te rasā tañcāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedissati. Sova nāma kāyo bhavissati te phoṭṭhabbā tañcāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedissatī”ti.

“It is wonderful, friends, it is marvelous, how that Blessed One who knows and sees, the Arahant, the Perfectly Enlightened One, has fittingly understood how an opportunity can be found in confinement for the purification of beings, for the overcoming of sorrow and lamentation, for the passing away of pain and displeasure, for the attainment of the way, for the realization of Nibbāna. That very eye will exist and those forms will come into range, yet one will not experience that sense-sphere. That very ear will exist and those sounds will come into range, yet one will not experience that sense-sphere. That very nose will exist and those odors will come into range, yet one will not experience that sense-sphere. That very tongue will exist and those tastes will come into range, yet one will not experience that sense-sphere. That very body will exist and those tactile objects will come into range, yet one will not experience that sense-sphere.”

Evaṃ vutte āyasmā udāyī āyasmantaṃ ānandaṃ etadavoca – “saññīmeva nu kho, āvuso ānanda, tadāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedeti udāhu asaññī”ti? “Saññīmeva kho, āvuso, tadāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedeti, no asaññī”ti.

When this was said, the Venerable Udāyī said to the Venerable Ānanda: “But, friend Ānanda, is it a percipient person who does not experience that sense-sphere, or is it a non-percipient one?” “Friend, it is a percipient person who does not experience that sense-sphere, not a non-percipient one.”

“Kiṃsaññī panāvuso, tadāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedetī”ti? “Idhāvuso, bhikkhu, sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ‘ananto ākāso’ti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Evaṃsaññīpi kho, āvuso, tadāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedeti.

“Being percipient of what, friend, does one not experience that sense-sphere?” “Here, friend, a bhikkhu, with the complete transcending of perceptions of form, with the cessation of perceptions of resistance, not attending to perceptions of diversity, developing the thought, ‘Space is infinite,’ enters and abides in the sphere of the infinity of space. Being percipient in this way also, friend, one does not experience that sense-sphere.

“Puna [M 3.0225] caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘anantaṃ viññāṇa’nti [V 3.0235] viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Evaṃsaññīpi kho, āvuso, tadāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedeti.

“Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the sphere of the infinity of space, developing the thought ‘Consciousness is infinite,’ enters and abides in the sphere of the infinity of consciousness. Being percipient in this way also, friend, one does not experience that sense-sphere.

“Puna caparaṃ, āvuso, bhikkhu sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘natthi kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Evaṃsaññīpi kho[T 4.0446], āvuso, tadāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedetī”ti.

“Furthermore, friend, a bhikkhu, by completely transcending the sphere of the infinity of consciousness, developing the thought ‘There is nothing whatsoever,’ enters and abides in the sphere of nothingness. Being percipient in this way also, friend, one does not experience that sense-sphere.”

“Ekamidāhaṃ, āvuso, samayaṃ sākete viharāmi añjanavane migadāye. Atha kho, āvuso, jaṭilavāsikā jaṭilagāhiyā (sī. pī.), jaḍilabhāgikā (syā.) bhikkhunī [P 4.0428] yenāhaṃ tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā maṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ aṭṭhāsi. Ekamantaṃ ṭhitā kho, āvuso, jaṭilavāsikā bhikkhunī maṃ etadavoca – ‘yāyaṃ, bhante ānanda, samādhi na cābhinato na cāpanato na ca sasaṅkhāraniggayhavāritagato sasaṅkhāraniggayhavāritavato (sī. syā. kaṃ. pī.), sasaṅkhāraniggayhavārivāvaṭo (ka.) a. ni. 3.102; 5.27; dī. ni. 3.355, vimuttattā ṭhito, ṭhitattā santusito, santusitattā no paritassati. Ayaṃ, bhante ānanda, samādhi kiṃphalo vutto bhagavatā’”ti?

“On one occasion, friend, I was dwelling at Sāketa in the Añjana Wood, a sanctuary for deer. Then, friend, the bhikkhunī who was a resident of Jaṭila approached where I was; having approached, she paid homage to me and stood to one side. Having stood to one side, friend, the bhikkhunī who was a resident of Jaṭila said this to me: ‘Venerable Ānanda, this concentration which is not inclined towards, nor drawn away, and is not established by suppressing hindrances with exertion; which is steady because of being liberated, content because of being steady, and because of being content, one does not crave—for what fruit has this concentration been declared by the Blessed One?’”

“Evaṃ vutte, sohaṃ, āvuso, jaṭilavāsikaṃ bhikkhuniṃ etadavocaṃ – ‘yāyaṃ, bhagini, samādhi na cābhinato na cāpanato na ca sasaṅkhāraniggayhavāritagato, vimuttattā ṭhito, ṭhitattā santusito, santusitattā no paritassati. Ayaṃ, bhagini, samādhi aññāphalo vutto bhagavatā’ti. Evaṃsaññīpi kho, āvuso, tadāyatanaṃ no paṭisaṃvedetī”ti. Chaṭṭhaṃ.

“When this was said, friend, I said this to the bhikkhunī who was a resident of Jaṭila: ‘Sister, this concentration which is not inclined towards, nor drawn away, and is not established by suppressing hindrances with exertion; which is steady because of being liberated, content because of being steady, and because of being content, one does not crave—this concentration, sister, has been declared by the Blessed One to have final knowledge as its fruit.’ Even one who perceives thus, friend, does not experience that sense base.” Sixth.

7. Lokāyatikasuttaṃ

7. The Sutta on the Lokāyatikas

38. Atha kho dve lokāyatikā brāhmaṇā yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṃsu; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavatā saddhiṃ sammodiṃsu. Sammodanīyaṃ kathaṃ sāraṇīyaṃ vītisāretvā ekamantaṃ nisīdiṃsu. Ekamantaṃ nisinnā kho te brāhmaṇā bhagavantaṃ etadavocuṃ –

38. Then two brahmins who were Lokāyatikas approached the Blessed One and exchanged joyful greetings with him. When they had concluded their joyful and memorable talk, they sat down to one side. Having sat down to one side, those brahmins said this to the Blessed One:

“Pūraṇo, bho gotama, kassapo sabbaññū sabbadassāvī aparisesaṃ ñāṇadassanaṃ paṭijānāti – ‘carato ca me tiṭṭhato ca suttassa ca jāgarassa [M 3.0226] ca satataṃ samitaṃ ñāṇadassanaṃ [T 4.0447] paccupaṭṭhita’nti. So evamāha – ‘ahaṃ anantena ñāṇena anantaṃ lokaṃ jānaṃ passaṃ [V 3.0236] viharāmī’ti. Ayampi [P 4.0429] ayampi hi (syā. ka.), bho gotama, nigaṇṭho nāṭaputto sabbaññū sabbadassāvī aparisesaṃ ñāṇadassanaṃ paṭijānāti – ‘carato ca me tiṭṭhato ca suttassa ca jāgarassa ca satataṃ samitaṃ ñāṇadassanaṃ paccupaṭṭhita’nti. So sopi (?) evamāha – ‘ahaṃ anantena ñāṇena anantaṃ lokaṃ jānaṃ passaṃ viharāmī’ti. Imesaṃ, bho gotama, ubhinnaṃ ñāṇavādānaṃ ubhinnaṃ aññamaññaṃ vipaccanīkavādānaṃ ko saccaṃ āha ko musā”ti?

“Master Gotama, Pūraṇa Kassapa, who is all-knowing and all-seeing, claims complete knowledge and vision thus: ‘Whether I am walking or standing, sleeping or awake, knowledge and vision are constantly and continuously established in me.’ He says thus: ‘I dwell knowing and seeing the infinite world with infinite knowledge.’ This Nigaṇṭha Nāṭaputta also, Master Gotama, who is all-knowing and all-seeing, claims complete knowledge and vision thus: ‘Whether I am walking or standing, sleeping or awake, knowledge and vision are constantly and continuously established in me.’ He says thus: ‘I dwell knowing and seeing the infinite world with infinite knowledge.’ Of these two proponents of knowledge, these two who make mutually contradictory assertions, who speaks truth and who speaks falsehood?”

“Alaṃ, brāhmaṇā! Tiṭṭhatetaṃ – ‘imesaṃ ubhinnaṃ ñāṇavādānaṃ ubhinnaṃ aññamaññaṃ vipaccanīkavādānaṃ ko saccaṃ āha ko musā’ti. Dhammaṃ vo, brāhmaṇā, desessāmi, taṃ suṇātha, sādhukaṃ manasi karotha; bhāsissāmī”ti. “Evaṃ, bho”ti kho te brāhmaṇā bhagavato paccassosuṃ. Bhagavā etadavoca –

“Enough, brahmins! Let this be: ‘Of these two proponents of knowledge, these two who make mutually contradictory assertions, who speaks truth and who speaks falsehood?’ I will teach you the Dhamma, brahmins. Listen to it, attend carefully, and I will speak.” “So be it, Master,” those brahmins replied to the Blessed One. The Blessed One said this:

“Seyyathāpi, brāhmaṇā, cattāro purisā catuddisā ṭhitā paramena javena ca samannāgatā paramena ca padavītihārena. Te evarūpena javena samannāgatā assu, seyyathāpi nāma daḷhadhammā daḷhadhammo (sabbattha) a. ni. 4.45; ma. ni. 1.116 ca, taṃsaṃvaṇṇanāṭīkāyo ca moggallānabyākaraṇañca oloketabbā dhanuggaho sikkhito katahattho katūpāsano lahukena asanena appakasirena tiriyaṃ tālacchāyaṃ tālacchātiṃ (sī. syā. pī.), tālacchādiṃ (ka.) a. ni. 4.45; ma. ni. 1.161 passitabbaṃ atipāteyya; evarūpena ca padavītihārena, seyyathāpi nāma puratthimā samuddā pacchimo samuddo atha puratthimāya disāya ṭhito puriso evaṃ vadeyya – ‘ahaṃ gamanena lokassa antaṃ pāpuṇissāmī’ti. So aññatreva asitapītakhāyitasāyitā aññatra uccārapassāvakammā aññatra niddākilamathapaṭivinodanā [T 4.0448] vassasatāyuko vassasatajīvī vassasataṃ gantvā appatvāva lokassa antaṃ antarā kālaṃ [P 4.0430] kareyya. Atha pacchimāya disāya…pe… atha uttarāya disāya… atha dakkhiṇāya disāya ṭhito puriso evaṃ vadeyya – ‘ahaṃ gamanena lokassa antaṃ pāpuṇissāmī’ti. So aññatreva asitapītakhāyitasāyitā aññatra uccārapassāvakammā aññatra [M 3.0227] niddākilamathapaṭivinodanā vassasatāyuko vassasatajīvī vassasataṃ gantvā appatvāva lokassa antaṃ antarā kālaṃ kareyya. Taṃ kissa hetu? Nāhaṃ, brāhmaṇā, evarūpāya sandhāvanikāya lokassa antaṃ ñāteyyaṃ daṭṭheyyaṃ patteyyanti vadāmi. Na cāhaṃ, brāhmaṇā, appatvāva lokassa antaṃ dukkhassa antakiriyaṃ vadāmi.

“Suppose, brahmins, four men were standing in the four directions, endowed with supreme speed and the utmost stride. They might be endowed with such speed as that of a strong-bowed archer—skilled, deft, and practiced—who with a light arrow could easily shoot across the shadow of a palm tree; and with a stride such as from the eastern ocean to the western ocean. Then the man standing in the eastern direction might say: ‘I will reach the end of the world by traveling.’ Apart from eating, drinking, chewing, and tasting, apart from urination and defecation, and apart from dispelling sleep and fatigue, with a lifespan of a hundred years, living for a hundred years, having traveled for a hundred years, without having reached the end of the world, he would die along the way. Then the man in the western direction… and so on… then the man in the northern direction… then the man standing in the southern direction might say: ‘I will reach the end of the world by traveling.’ Apart from eating, drinking, chewing, and tasting, apart from urination and defecation, and apart from dispelling sleep and fatigue, with a lifespan of a hundred years, living for a hundred years, having traveled for a hundred years, without having reached the end of the world, he would die along the way. For what reason? I do not say, brahmins, that by such running the end of the world can be known, seen, or reached. And yet, brahmins, I do not say that one can make an end of suffering without having reached the end of the world.”

“Pañcime, brāhmaṇā, kāmaguṇā ariyassa vinaye lokoti vuccati. Katame pañca? Cakkhuviññeyyā [V 3.0237] rūpā iṭṭhā kantā manāpā piyarūpā kāmūpasaṃhitā rajanīyā; sotaviññeyyā saddā…pe… ghānaviññeyyā gandhā… jivhāviññeyyā rasā… kāyaviññeyyā phoṭṭhabbā iṭṭhā kantā manāpā piyarūpā kāmūpasaṃhitā rajanīyā; ime kho, brāhmaṇā, pañca kāmaguṇā ariyassa vinaye lokoti vuccati.

“These five, brahmins, are the cords of sensual pleasure that in the discipline of the Noble One are called ‘the world.’ What five? Forms cognizable by the eye that are wished for, desired, agreeable, of a likable nature, connected with sensual desire, and enticing. Sounds cognizable by the ear… and so on… Odors cognizable by the nose… Tastes cognizable by the tongue… Tactile objects cognizable by the body that are wished for, desired, agreeable, of a likable nature, connected with sensual desire, and enticing. These, brahmins, are the five cords of sensual pleasure that in the discipline of the Noble One are called ‘the world.’”

“Idha, brāhmaṇā, bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ayaṃ vuccati, brāhmaṇā, ‘bhikkhu lokassa antamāgamma, lokassa ante viharati’. Tamaññe evamāhaṃsu – ‘ayampi [T 4.0449] lokapariyāpanno, ayampi anissaṭo lokamhā’ti. Ahampi hi ahampi (sī. pī.), brāhmaṇā, evaṃ vadāmi – ‘ayampi lokapariyāpanno, ayampi anissaṭo lokamhā’”ti.

“Here, brahmins, secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, a bhikkhu enters and dwells in the first jhāna, which is accompanied by applied thought and sustained thought, with rapture and pleasure born of seclusion. This, brahmins, is called ‘a bhikkhu who has come to the end of the world and dwells at the end of the world.’ Others might say of him: ‘This one too is included in the world; this one too has not escaped from the world.’ I too, brahmins, say this: ‘This one too is included in the world; this one too has not escaped from the world.’”

“Puna [P 4.0431] caparaṃ, brāhmaṇā, bhikkhu vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ… tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ… catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ayaṃ vuccati, brāhmaṇā, ‘bhikkhu lokassa antamāgamma lokassa ante viharati’. Tamaññe evamāhaṃsu – ‘ayampi lokapariyāpanno, ayampi anissaṭo lokamhā’ti. Ahampi hi, brāhmaṇā, evaṃ vadāmi – ‘ayampi lokapariyāpanno, ayampi anissaṭo lokamhā’”ti.

Furthermore, brahmins, with the stilling of applied and sustained thought … a bhikkhu enters and dwells in the second jhāna … the third jhāna … the fourth jhāna. This, brahmins, is called 'a bhikkhu who has come to the end of the world and dwells at the end of the world.' Others say of him: ‘This one too is included in the world; this one too has not escaped from the world.’ I too, brahmins, say thus: ‘This one too is included in the world; this one too has not escaped from the world.’”

“Puna caparaṃ, brāhmaṇā, bhikkhu sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ‘ananto ākāso’ti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ayaṃ vuccati, brāhmaṇā, ‘bhikkhu lokassa antamāgamma lokassa ante viharati’. Tamaññe evamāhaṃsu – ‘ayampi lokapariyāpanno, ayampi anissaṭo lokamhā’ti[M 3.0228]. Ahampi hi, brāhmaṇā, evaṃ vadāmi – ‘ayampi lokapariyāpanno, ayampi anissaṭo lokamhā’”ti.

Furthermore, brahmins, a bhikkhu, by completely surmounting perceptions of form, with the passing away of perceptions of resistance, and not attending to perceptions of diversity, aware that ‘space is infinite,’ enters and dwells in the base of infinite space. This, brahmins, is called ‘a bhikkhu who has come to the end of the world and dwells at the end of the world.’ Others say of him: ‘This one too is included in the world; this one too has not escaped from the world.’ I too, brahmins, say thus: ‘This one too is included in the world; this one too has not escaped from the world.’”

“Puna caparaṃ, brāhmaṇā, bhikkhu sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘anantaṃ viññāṇa’nti [V 3.0238] viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati…pe… sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘natthi kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati…pe… sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ayaṃ vuccati, brāhmaṇā[T 4.0450], ‘bhikkhu lokassa antamāgamma lokassa ante viharati’. Tamaññe evamāhaṃsu – ‘ayampi lokapariyāpanno, ayampi anissaṭo lokamhā’ti. Ahampi hi, brāhmaṇā, evaṃ vadāmi – ‘ayampi lokapariyāpanno, ayampi anissaṭo lokamhā’”ti.

Furthermore, brahmins, a bhikkhu, by completely surmounting the base of infinite space, aware that ‘consciousness is infinite,’ enters and dwells in the base of infinite consciousness… and by completely surmounting the base of infinite consciousness, aware that ‘there is nothing,’ enters and dwells in the base of nothingness… and by completely surmounting the base of nothingness, enters and dwells in the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception. This, brahmins, is called ‘a bhikkhu who has come to the end of the world and dwells at the end of the world.’ Others say of him: ‘This one too is included in the world; this one too has not escaped from the world.’ I too, brahmins, say thus: ‘This one too is included in the world; this one too has not escaped from the world.’”

“Puna caparaṃ, brāhmaṇā, bhikkhu sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ upasampajja viharati, paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti. Ayaṃ vuccati, brāhmaṇā, ‘bhikkhu lokassa [P 4.0432] antamāgamma lokassa ante viharati tiṇṇo loke visattika’”nti. Sattamaṃ.

Furthermore, brahmins, a bhikkhu, by completely surmounting the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, enters and dwells in the cessation of perception and feeling. And having seen with wisdom, his taints are destroyed. This one, brahmins, is called: ‘A bhikkhu who has come to the end of the world and dwells at the end of the world, one who has crossed over attachment in the world.’ The seventh.

8. Devāsurasaṅgāmasuttaṃ

8. The Discourse on the Battle Between Devas and Asuras

39. “Bhūtapubbaṃ, bhikkhave, devāsurasaṅgāmo samupabyūḷho samupabbūḷho (sī. pī.) ahosi. Tasmiṃ kho pana, bhikkhave, saṅgāme asurā jiniṃsu, devā parājayiṃsu parājiyiṃsu (sī. syā. ka.). Parājitā ca, bhikkhave, devā devā bhītā (pī.) apayiṃsuyeva apayaṃsveva (sī.) uttarenābhimukhā, abhiyiṃsu abhiyaṃsu (sī.) asurā. Atha kho, bhikkhave, devānaṃ etadahosi – ‘abhiyanteva kho asurā. Yaṃnūna mayaṃ dutiyampi asurehi saṅgāmeyyāmā’ti. Dutiyampi kho, bhikkhave, devā asurehi saṅgāmesuṃ. Dutiyampi kho, bhikkhave, asurāva jiniṃsu, devā parājayiṃsu. Parājitā ca, bhikkhave, devā apayiṃsuyeva uttarenābhimukhā, abhiyiṃsu asurā”.

39. Once in the past, bhikkhus, a battle was drawn up between the devas and the asuras. In that battle, bhikkhus, the asuras were victorious and the devas were defeated. The defeated devas, bhikkhus, fled, facing northwards, and the asuras pursued them. Then, bhikkhus, it occurred to the devas: ‘The asuras are pursuing us. What if we were to engage the asuras in battle a second time?’ So a second time, bhikkhus, the devas engaged the asuras in battle. And a second time, bhikkhus, the asuras were victorious and the devas were defeated. The defeated devas, bhikkhus, fled, facing northwards, and the asuras pursued them.

Atha kho, bhikkhave, devānaṃ etadahosi – ‘abhiyanteva kho asurā. Yaṃnūna mayaṃ tatiyampi asurehi saṅgāmeyyāmā’ti. Tatiyampi kho, bhikkhave, [M 3.0229] devā asurehi saṅgāmesuṃ. Tatiyampi kho[T 4.0451], bhikkhave, asurāva jiniṃsu, devā parājayiṃsu. Parājitā ca, bhikkhave, devā bhītā devapuraṃyeva pavisiṃsu. Devapuragatānañca pana puna (ka.), bhikkhave, devānaṃ etadahosi – ‘bhīruttānagatena kho dāni mayaṃ etarahi [P 4.0433] attanā viharāma akaraṇīyā asurehī’ti. Asurānampi, bhikkhave, etadahosi – ‘bhīruttānagatena kho dāni devā etarahi attanā viharanti akaraṇīyā amhehī’ti.

Then, bhikkhus, it occurred to the devas: ‘The asuras are pursuing us. What if we were to engage the asuras in battle a third time?’ So a third time, bhikkhus, the devas engaged the asuras in battle. And a third time, bhikkhus, the asuras were victorious and the devas were defeated. The defeated devas, bhikkhus, being frightened, entered their own city of the devas. Having entered the city of the devas, bhikkhus, it occurred to them: ‘Now we dwell, having attained a refuge from fear, unassailable by the asuras.’ And it occurred to the asuras, bhikkhus: ‘Now the devas dwell, having attained a refuge from fear, unassailable by us.’”

“Bhūtapubbaṃ[V 3.0239], bhikkhave, devāsurasaṅgāmo samupabyūḷho ahosi. Tasmiṃ kho pana, bhikkhave, saṅgāme devā jiniṃsu, asurā parājayiṃsu. Parājitā ca, bhikkhave, asurā apayiṃsuyeva dakkhiṇenābhimukhā, abhiyiṃsu devā. Atha kho, bhikkhave, asurānaṃ etadahosi – ‘abhiyanteva kho devā. Yaṃnūna mayaṃ dutiyampi devehi saṅgāmeyyāmā’ti. Dutiyampi kho, bhikkhave, asurā devehi saṅgāmesuṃ. Dutiyampi kho, bhikkhave, devā jiniṃsu, asurā parājayiṃsu. Parājitā ca, bhikkhave, asurā apayiṃsuyeva dakkhiṇenābhimukhā, abhiyiṃsu devā”.

Once in the past, bhikkhus, a battle was drawn up between the devas and the asuras. In that battle, bhikkhus, the devas were victorious and the asuras were defeated. The defeated asuras, bhikkhus, fled, facing southwards, and the devas pursued them. Then, bhikkhus, it occurred to the asuras: ‘The devas are pursuing us. What if we were to engage the devas in battle a second time?’ So a second time, bhikkhus, the asuras engaged the devas in battle. And a second time, bhikkhus, the devas were victorious and the asuras were defeated. The defeated asuras, bhikkhus, fled, facing southwards, and the devas pursued them.

Atha kho, bhikkhave, asurānaṃ etadahosi – ‘abhiyanteva kho devā. Yaṃnūna mayaṃ tatiyampi devehi saṅgāmeyyāmā’ti. Tatiyampi kho, bhikkhave, asurā devehi saṅgāmesuṃ. Tatiyampi kho, bhikkhave, devā jiniṃsu, asurā parājayiṃsu. Parājitā ca, bhikkhave, asurā bhītā asurapuraṃyeva pavisiṃsu. Asurapuragatānañca pana, bhikkhave, asurānaṃ etadahosi – ‘bhīruttānagatena kho dāni mayaṃ etarahi attanā viharāma akaraṇīyā [T 4.0452] devehī’ti. Devānampi, bhikkhave, etadahosi – ‘bhīruttānagatena kho dāni asurā etarahi attanā viharanti akaraṇīyā amhehī’ti.

Then, bhikkhus, it occurred to the asuras: ‘The devas are pursuing us. What if we were to engage the devas in battle a third time?’ So a third time, bhikkhus, the asuras engaged the devas in battle. And a third time, bhikkhus, the devas were victorious and the asuras were defeated. The defeated asuras, bhikkhus, being frightened, entered their own city of the asuras. Having entered the city of the asuras, bhikkhus, it occurred to them: ‘Now we dwell, having attained a refuge from fear, unassailable by the devas.’ And it occurred to the devas, bhikkhus: ‘Now the asuras dwell, having attained a refuge from fear, unassailable by us.’”

“Evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, yasmiṃ samaye bhikkhu vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati, tasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye bhikkhussa evaṃ hoti – ‘bhīruttānagatena kho dānāhaṃ etarahi attanā viharāmi [P 4.0434] akaraṇīyo mārassā’ti[M 3.0230]. Mārassāpi, bhikkhave, pāpimato evaṃ hoti – ‘bhīruttānagatena kho dāni bhikkhu etarahi attanā viharati akaraṇīyo mayha’”nti.

So too, bhikkhus, at a time when a bhikkhu—quite secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states—enters and dwells in the first jhāna, which is accompanied by applied thought and sustained thought, with rapture and pleasure born of seclusion, on that occasion, bhikkhus, it occurs to the bhikkhu: ‘Now I dwell, having attained a refuge from fear, where nothing can be done to me by Māra.’ And to Māra the Evil One, bhikkhus, it also occurs: ‘Now this bhikkhu dwells, having attained a refuge from fear, where nothing can be done to him by me.’

“Yasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye bhikkhu vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ… tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ… catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati, tasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye [V 3.0240] bhikkhussa evaṃ hoti – ‘bhīruttānagatena kho dānāhaṃ etarahi attanā viharāmi akaraṇīyo mārassā’ti. Mārassāpi, bhikkhave, pāpimato evaṃ hoti – ‘bhīruttānagatena kho dāni bhikkhu etarahi attanā viharati, akaraṇīyo mayha’”nti.

Bhikkhus, at a time when a bhikkhu, with the stilling of applied thought and sustained thought… enters and dwells in the second jhāna… the third jhāna… the fourth jhāna, on that occasion, bhikkhus, it occurs to the bhikkhu: ‘Now I dwell, having attained a refuge from fear, where nothing can be done to me by Māra.’ And to Māra the Evil One, bhikkhus, it also occurs: ‘Now this bhikkhu dwells, having attained a refuge from fear, where nothing can be done to him by me.’

“Yasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye bhikkhu sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ‘ananto ākāso’ti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, ‘bhikkhu antamakāsi māraṃ, apadaṃ vadhitvā māracakkhuṃ adassanaṃ gato pāpimato tiṇṇo loke visattika’”nti.

Bhikkhus, at a time when a bhikkhu, by completely surmounting perceptions of form, with the passing away of perceptions of sensory impact, and by not attending to perceptions of diversity, aware that ‘space is infinite,’ enters and dwells in the base of infinite space—this bhikkhu, bhikkhus, is called: ‘One who has blinded Māra; having destroyed Māra’s eye without a footing, he has gone beyond the sight of the Evil One and has crossed over attachment in the world.’

“Yasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye bhikkhu sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘anantaṃ viññāṇa’nti viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati… sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘natthi [T 4.0453] kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati… sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati… sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ upasampajja viharati, paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti. Ayaṃ vuccati, bhikkhave, ‘bhikkhu antamakāsi māraṃ, apadaṃ vadhitvā māracakkhuṃ adassanaṃ gato pāpimato tiṇṇo loke visattika’”nti. Aṭṭhamaṃ.

Bhikkhus, at a time when a bhikkhu, by completely surmounting the base of infinite space, aware that ‘consciousness is infinite,’ enters and dwells in the base of infinite consciousness… by completely surmounting the base of infinite consciousness, aware that ‘there is nothing,’ enters and dwells in the base of nothingness… by completely surmounting the base of nothingness, enters and dwells in the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception… by completely surmounting the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, he enters and dwells in the cessation of perception and feeling, and having seen with wisdom, his taints are destroyed. This bhikkhu, bhikkhus, is called: ‘One who has blinded Māra; having destroyed Māra’s eye without a footing, he has gone beyond the sight of the Evil One and has crossed over attachment in the world.’ The Eighth Sutta.

9. Nāgasuttaṃ

9. The Discourse on the Elephant

40. “Yasmiṃ[P 4.0435], bhikkhave, samaye āraññikassa nāgassa gocarapasutassa hatthīpi hatthiniyopi hatthikalabhāpi hatthicchāpāpi purato purato gantvā tiṇaggāni chindanti, tena, bhikkhave, āraññiko nāgo aṭṭīyati harāyati jigucchati. Yasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye āraññikassa nāgassa gocarapasutassa hatthīpi hatthiniyopi hatthikalabhāpi hatthicchāpāpi obhaggobhaggaṃ sākhābhaṅgaṃ khādanti, tena, bhikkhave, āraññiko nāgo aṭṭīyati harāyati jigucchati. Yasmiṃ[M 3.0231], bhikkhave, samaye āraññikassa nāgassa ogāhaṃ otiṇṇassa hatthīpi hatthiniyopi hatthikalabhāpi hatthicchāpāpi purato purato gantvā soṇḍāya udakaṃ āloḷenti, tena, bhikkhave, āraññiko nāgo aṭṭīyati harāyati jigucchati. Yasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye āraññikassa [V 3.0241] nāgassa ogāhā uttiṇṇassa hatthiniyo kāyaṃ upanighaṃsantiyo gacchanti, tena, bhikkhave, āraññiko nāgo aṭṭīyati harāyati jigucchati.

40. Bhikkhus, at a time when a forest-dwelling bull elephant has gone out to graze, male elephants, female elephants, young elephants, and elephant calves go before him and cut off the tips of the grass. Because of this, bhikkhus, the forest-dwelling bull elephant is troubled, ashamed, and disgusted. At a time when a forest-dwelling bull elephant has gone out to graze, male elephants, female elephants, young elephants, and elephant calves eat the tangled, broken-off branches. Because of this, bhikkhus, the forest-dwelling bull elephant is troubled, ashamed, and disgusted. At a time when a forest-dwelling bull elephant has descended to a watering place, male elephants, female elephants, young elephants, and elephant calves go before him and stir up the water with their trunks. Because of this, bhikkhus, the forest-dwelling bull elephant is troubled, ashamed, and disgusted. At a time when a forest-dwelling bull elephant has come up from the watering place, female elephants go along rubbing against his body. Because of this, bhikkhus, the forest-dwelling bull elephant is troubled, ashamed, and disgusted.

“Tasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye āraññikassa nāgassa evaṃ hoti – ‘ahaṃ kho etarahi ākiṇṇo viharāmi hatthīhi hatthinīhi hatthikalabhehi hatthicchāpehi[T 4.0454]. Chinnaggāni ceva tiṇāni khādāmi, obhaggobhaggañca me sākhābhaṅgaṃ khādanti khāditaṃ (syā. ka.) mahāva. 467 passitabbaṃ, āvilāni ca pānīyāni pivāmi, ogāhā ca ogāhāpi ca (syā. ka.) mahāva. 467 passitabbaṃ me uttiṇṇassa hatthiniyo kāyaṃ upanighaṃsantiyo gacchanti. Yaṃnūnāhaṃ eko gaṇasmā vūpakaṭṭho vihareyya’nti. So aparena samayena eko gaṇasmā vūpakaṭṭho viharati, acchinnaggāni ceva tiṇāni khādati, obhaggobhaggañcassa sākhābhaṅgaṃ [P 4.0436] na khādanti na obhaggobhaggañca sākhābhaṅga khādati (syā. ka.), anāvilāni ca pānīyāni pivati, ogāhā cassa uttiṇṇassa na hatthiniyo kāyaṃ upanighaṃsantiyo gacchanti.

At that time, bhikkhus, this thought occurs to the forest-dwelling bull elephant: ‘I now live crowded by male elephants, female elephants, young elephants, and elephant calves. I eat grass with its tips cut off, and they eat my tangled, broken-off branches. I drink turbid water, and when I have come up from the watering place, female elephants go along rubbing against my body. It would be good if I were to live alone, withdrawn from the herd.’ Then, at a later time, he lives alone, withdrawn from the herd. He eats grass with its tips uncut, and they do not eat his tangled, broken-off branches. He drinks water that is not turbid, and when he has come up from the watering place, female elephants do not go along rubbing against his body.

“Tasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye āraññikassa nāgassa evaṃ hoti – ‘ahaṃ kho pubbe ākiṇṇo vihāsiṃ hatthīhi hatthinīhi hatthikalabhehi hatthicchāpehi, chinnaggāni ceva tiṇāni khādiṃ, obhaggobhaggañca me sākhābhaṅgaṃ khādiṃsu, āvilāni ca pānīyāni apāyiṃ, ogāhā ettha pisaddo sabbatthapi natthi ca me uttiṇṇassa hatthiniyo kāyaṃ upanighaṃsantiyo agamaṃsu. Sohaṃ etarahi eko gaṇasmā vūpakaṭṭho viharāmi, acchinnaggāni ceva tiṇāni khādāmi, obhaggobhaggañca me sākhābhaṅgaṃ na khādanti, anāvilāni ca pānīyāni pivāmi, ogāhā ca me uttiṇṇassa na hatthiniyo kāyaṃ upanighaṃsantiyo gacchantī’ti. So soṇḍāya sākhābhaṅgaṃ bhañjitvā sākhābhaṅgena kāyaṃ parimajjitvā attamano soṇḍaṃ saṃharati kaṇḍuṃ saṃhanti (sī. pī.) kaṇḍuṃ saṃhanati (syā.), ettha kaṇḍuvanadukkhaṃ vinetīti attho,.

At that time, bhikkhus, this thought occurs to the forest-dwelling bull elephant: ‘Formerly, I lived crowded by male elephants, female elephants, young elephants, and elephant calves; I ate grass with its tips cut off, and they ate my tangled, broken-off branches; I drank turbid water, and when I had come up from the watering place, female elephants went along rubbing against my body. But now I live alone, withdrawn from the herd; I eat grass with its tips uncut, and they do not eat my tangled, broken-off branches; I drink water that is not turbid, and when I have come up from the watering place, female elephants do not go along rubbing against my body.’ He breaks off a branch with his trunk, rubs his body with the branch, and being pleased, holds his trunk well.

“Evamevaṃ kho, bhikkhave, yasmiṃ samaye bhikkhu ākiṇṇo viharati bhikkhūhi bhikkhunīhi upāsakehi upāsikāhi raññā rājamahāmattehi titthiyehi [M 3.0232] titthiyasāvakehi, tasmiṃ, bhikkhave, samaye bhikkhussa evaṃ hoti – ‘ahaṃ kho etarahi ākiṇṇo viharāmi bhikkhūhi bhikkhunīhi upāsakehi [T 4.0455] upāsikāhi raññā rājamahāmattehi titthiyehi titthiyasāvakehi. Yaṃnūnāhaṃ eko gaṇasmā vūpakaṭṭho vihareyya’nti. So vivittaṃ senāsanaṃ bhajati araññaṃ rukkhamūlaṃ [P 4.0437] pabbataṃ kandaraṃ giriguhaṃ susānaṃ vanapatthaṃ abbhokāsaṃ palālapuñjaṃ[V 3.0242]. So araññagato vā rukkhamūlagato vā suññāgāragato vā nisīdati pallaṅkaṃ ābhujitvā ujuṃ kāyaṃ paṇidhāya parimukhaṃ satiṃ upaṭṭhapetvā.

“In the very same way, bhikkhus, at a time when a bhikkhu lives crowded by bhikkhus, bhikkhunīs, male lay followers, female lay followers, the king, the king's chief ministers, heretics, and the disciples of heretics, at that time, bhikkhus, this thought occurs to the bhikkhu: ‘I now live crowded by bhikkhus, bhikkhunīs, male lay followers, female lay followers, the king, the king's chief ministers, heretics, and the disciples of heretics. It would be good if I were to live alone, withdrawn from the group.’ He resorts to a secluded lodging: a forest, the root of a tree, a mountain, a ravine, a mountain cave, a charnel ground, a forest grove, an open space, or a heap of straw. Having gone to the forest, or to the root of a tree, or to a secluded place, he sits down, having folded his legs crosswise, set his body erect, and established mindfulness before him.”

“So abhijjhaṃ loke pahāya vigatābhijjhena cetasā viharati, abhijjhāya cittaṃ parisodheti; byāpādapadosaṃ pahāya abyāpannacitto viharati sabbapāṇabhūtahitānukampī, byāpādapadosā cittaṃ parisodheti; thinamiddhaṃ pahāya vigatathinamiddho viharati ālokasaññī sato sampajāno, thinamiddhā cittaṃ parisodheti; uddhaccakukkuccaṃ pahāya anuddhato viharati ajjhattaṃ vūpasantacitto, uddhaccakukkuccā cittaṃ parisodheti; vicikicchaṃ pahāya tiṇṇavicikiccho viharati akathaṃkathī kusalesu dhammesu, vicikicchāya cittaṃ parisodheti. So ime pañca nīvaraṇe pahāya cetaso upakkilese paññāya dubbalīkaraṇe vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So attamano soṇḍaṃ saṃharati kaṇḍuṃ saṃhanti (sī. pī.), kaṇḍuṃ saṃhanati (syā.), ettha kaṇḍuvanasadisaṃ jhānapaṭipakkhaṃ kilesadukkhaṃ vinetīti attho. Vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ… tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ… catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharati. So attamano soṇḍaṃ saṃharati.

“He, having abandoned covetousness in the world, dwells with a mind free from covetousness; he cleanses his mind from covetousness. Having abandoned ill will and malevolence, he dwells with a mind free from ill will, compassionate for the welfare of all living beings; he cleanses his mind from ill will and malevolence. Having abandoned sloth and torpor, he dwells free from sloth and torpor, perceiving light, mindful, and clearly comprehending; he cleanses his mind from sloth and torpor. Having abandoned restlessness and worry, he dwells unagitated, with a mind internally pacified; he cleanses his mind from restlessness and worry. Having abandoned doubt, he dwells having crossed over doubt, free from perplexity about wholesome states; he cleanses his mind from doubt. He, having abandoned these five hindrances—defilements of the mind that weaken wisdom—secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, enters and dwells in the first jhāna, which is accompanied by applied and sustained thought, with rapture and happiness born of seclusion. He, being gladdened, holds his trunk-like mind well. With the stilling of applied and sustained thought… he enters and dwells in the second jhāna… the third jhāna… the fourth jhāna. He, being gladdened, holds his trunk-like mind well.”

“Sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā ‘ananto ākāso’ti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati. So attamano soṇḍaṃ [T 4.0456] saṃharati. Sabbaso ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘anantaṃ viññāṇa’nti viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati… sabbaso viññāṇañcāyatanaṃ samatikkamma ‘natthi kiñcī’ti ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati… sabbaso ākiñcaññāyatanaṃ samatikkamma nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharati… sabbaso nevasaññānāsaññāyatanaṃ [M 3.0233][P 4.0438] samatikkamma saññāvedayitanirodhaṃ upasampajja viharati, paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti. So attamano soṇḍaṃ saṃharatī”ti. Navamaṃ.

“By completely transcending perceptions of form, by the cessation of perceptions of resistance, by not attending to perceptions of diversity, aware that ‘space is infinite,’ he attains and abides in the base of infinite space. He, being gladdened, holds his trunk-like mind well. By completely transcending the base of infinite space, aware that ‘consciousness is infinite,’ he attains and abides in the base of infinite consciousness… by completely transcending the base of infinite consciousness, aware that ‘there is nothing,’ he attains and abides in the base of nothingness… by completely transcending the base of nothingness, he attains and abides in the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception… by completely transcending the base of neither-perception-nor-non-perception, he attains and abides in the cessation of perception and feeling. And for him, having seen with wisdom, the taints are destroyed. He, being gladdened, holds his trunk-like mind well.” The Ninth Sutta is finished.

10. Tapussasuttaṃ

10. The Sutta on Tapussa

41. Ekaṃ [V 3.0243] samayaṃ bhagavā mallesu viharati uruvelakappaṃ nāma mallānaṃ nigamo. Atha kho bhagavā pubbaṇhasamayaṃ nivāsetvā pattacīvaramādāya uruvelakappaṃ piṇḍāya pāvisi. Uruvelakappe piṇḍāya caritvā pacchābhattaṃ piṇḍapātapaṭikkanto āyasmantaṃ ānandaṃ āmantesi – “idheva tāva tvaṃ, ānanda, hohi, yāvāhaṃ mahāvanaṃ ajjhogāhāmi divāvihārāyā”ti. “Evaṃ, bhante”ti kho āyasmā ānando bhagavato paccassosi. Atha kho bhagavā mahāvanaṃ ajjhogāhetvā aññatarasmiṃ rukkhamūle divāvihāraṃ nisīdi.

41. At one time the Blessed One was dwelling among the Mallas, in a market town of the Mallas named Uruvelakappa. Then the Blessed One, having dressed in the morning, took his bowl and outer robe and entered Uruvelakappa for alms-food. Having gone for alms-food in Uruvelakappa, after the meal, upon returning from the alms-round, he addressed the Venerable Ānanda: ‘Ānanda, you should remain right here for now, while I enter the Great Wood for the day's abiding.’ ‘So be it, venerable sir,’ the Venerable Ānanda replied to the Blessed One. Then the Blessed One, having entered the Great Wood, sat down at the foot of a certain tree for the day's abiding.

Atha kho tapusso gahapati yenāyasmā ānando tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā āyasmantaṃ ānandaṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinno kho tapusso gahapati āyasmantaṃ ānandaṃ etadavoca –

Then the householder Tapussa approached the Venerable Ānanda. Having approached, he paid homage to the Venerable Ānanda and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the householder Tapussa said this to the Venerable Ānanda:

“Mayaṃ, bhante ānanda, gihī kāmabhogino kāmārāmā kāmaratā kāmasammuditā. Tesaṃ no, bhante, amhākaṃ gihīnaṃ kāmabhogīnaṃ kāmārāmānaṃ kāmaratānaṃ kāmasammuditānaṃ [T 4.0457] papāto viya khāyati, yadidaṃ nekkhammaṃ. Sutaṃ metaṃ, bhante, ‘imasmiṃ dhammavinaye daharānaṃ daharānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ nekkhamme cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’ passataṃ (?). Tayidaṃ, bhante, imasmiṃ dhammavinaye bhikkhūnaṃ bahunā janena visabhāgo, yadidaṃ nekkhamma”nti.

“Venerable Ānanda, we are laypeople, enjoyers of sensual pleasures, delighting in sensual pleasures, fond of sensual pleasures, rejoicing in sensual pleasures. For us, venerable sir—for us laypeople who are enjoyers of sensual pleasures, delighting in sensual pleasures, fond of sensual pleasures, rejoicing in sensual pleasures—this renunciation appears like a precipice. I have heard this, venerable sir: ‘In this Dhamma and Vinaya, the minds of very young monks leap into renunciation, become confident, are well-established, and are liberated, seeing it as peace.’ Thus, venerable sir, in this Dhamma and Vinaya, this renunciation of the monks is dissimilar to the great mass of people.”

“Atthi [P 4.0439] kho etaṃ, gahapati, kathāpābhataṃ bhagavantaṃ dassanāya. Āyāma, gahapati, yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamissāma; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavato etamatthaṃ ārocessāma. Yathā no bhagavā byākarissati tathā naṃ dhāressāmā”ti.

“Indeed, householder, this is a topic of conversation to be brought as a gift for an audience with the Blessed One. Come, householder, let us approach the Blessed One. Having approached, we shall report this matter to the Blessed One. As the Blessed One explains it to us, so shall we bear it in mind.”

“Evaṃ, bhante”ti kho tapusso gahapati āyasmato ānandassa paccassosi. Atha kho āyasmā ānando tapussena gahapatinā saddhiṃ [M 3.0234] yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ nisīdi. Ekamantaṃ nisinno kho āyasmā ānando bhagavantaṃ etadavoca –

“So be it, venerable sir,” the householder Tapussa replied to the Venerable Ānanda. Then the Venerable Ānanda, together with the householder Tapussa, approached the Blessed One. Having approached, he paid homage to the Blessed One and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, the Venerable Ānanda said this to the Blessed One:

“Ayaṃ[V 3.0244], bhante, tapusso gahapati evamāha – ‘mayaṃ, bhante ānanda, gihī kāmabhogino kāmārāmā kāmaratā kāmasammuditā, tesaṃ no bhante, amhākaṃ gihīnaṃ kāmabhogīnaṃ kāmārāmānaṃ kāmaratānaṃ kāmasammuditānaṃ papāto viya khāyati, yadidaṃ nekkhammaṃ’. Sutaṃ metaṃ, bhante, ‘imasmiṃ dhammavinaye daharānaṃ daharānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ nekkhamme cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. Tayidaṃ, bhante, imasmiṃ dhammavinaye bhikkhūnaṃ bahunā janena visabhāgo yadidaṃ nekkhamma’”nti.

“Venerable sir, this householder Tapussa says: ‘Venerable Ānanda, we are laypeople, enjoyers of sensual pleasures, delighting in sensual pleasures, fond of sensual pleasures, rejoicing in sensual pleasures. For us, venerable sir—for us laypeople who are enjoyers of sensual pleasures, delighting in sensual pleasures, fond of sensual pleasures, rejoicing in sensual pleasures—this renunciation appears like a precipice. I have heard this, venerable sir: “In this Dhamma and Vinaya, the minds of young monks leap into renunciation, find confidence, are well-established, and are liberated, seeing it as peace.” This renunciation of the monks, venerable sir, in this Dhamma and Vinaya, is dissimilar to the many folk.’”

“Evametaṃ, ānanda, evametaṃ, ānanda! Mayhampi kho, ānanda, pubbeva sambodhā anabhisambuddhassa bodhisattasseva sato etadahosi – ‘sādhu nekkhammaṃ[T 4.0458], sādhu paviveko’ti. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, nekkhamme cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘ko nu kho hetu ko paccayo, yena me nekkhamme cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’? Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘kāmesu kho me ādīnavo adiṭṭho, so ca me abahulīkato, nekkhamme ca ānisaṃso anadhigato, so ca me anāsevito[P 4.0440]. Tasmā me nekkhamme cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘sace kho ahaṃ kāmesu ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ bahulaṃ kareyyaṃ bahulīkareyyaṃ (sī. syā. pī.), nekkhamme ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseveyyaṃ, ṭhānaṃ kho panetaṃ vijjati yaṃ me nekkhamme cittaṃ pakkhandeyya pasīdeyya santiṭṭheyya vimucceyya etaṃ santanti passato’. So kho ahaṃ, ānanda, aparena samayena kāmesu ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ bahulamakāsiṃ, nekkhamme ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseviṃ. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, nekkhamme cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. So kho ahaṃ, ānanda, vivicceva kāmehi vivicca akusalehi dhammehi savitakkaṃ savicāraṃ vivekajaṃ pītisukhaṃ paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharāmi. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, iminā vihārena viharato kāmasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa me hoti ābādho[M 3.0235]. Seyyathāpi, ānanda, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya [T 4.0459] yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa me kāmasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa me hoti ābādho.

“So it is, Ānanda! So it is, Ānanda! Indeed, Ānanda, for me too, before my enlightenment, while I was still an unenlightened Bodhisatta, this thought occurred: ‘Renunciation is good; seclusion is good.’ But my mind did not leap into renunciation, did not become confident, did not settle upon it, and was not liberated, though I saw it as ‘peace.’ Then it occurred to me, Ānanda: ‘What is the cause, what is the reason, why my mind does not leap into renunciation, does not become confident, does not settle upon it, and is not liberated, though I see it as “peace”?’ Then it occurred to me, Ānanda: ‘I have not seen the danger in sensual pleasures, and I have not made much of it; and I have not attained the benefit of renunciation, and I have not cultivated it. That is why my mind does not leap into renunciation, does not become confident, does not settle upon it, and is not liberated, though I see it as “peace.”’ Then it occurred to me, Ānanda: ‘If I were to see the danger in sensual pleasures and make much of it, and if I were to attain the benefit of renunciation and cultivate it, it is possible that my mind would leap into renunciation, become confident, settle upon it, and be liberated, seeing it as “peace.”’ Then, Ānanda, at a later time, having seen the danger in sensual pleasures, I made much of it; having attained the benefit of renunciation, I cultivated it. Then my mind leaped into renunciation, became confident, settled upon it, and was liberated, seeing it as ‘peace.’ Then, Ānanda, secluded from sensual pleasures, secluded from unwholesome states, I entered and dwelled in the first jhāna, which is accompanied by applied and sustained thought, with rapture and pleasure born of seclusion. But while I was dwelling thus, perceptions and attentions connected with sensual pleasures arose in me. That was an affliction for me. Just as pain might arise for one who is happy, solely for his affliction, so too those perceptions and attentions connected with sensual pleasures arose in me. That was an affliction for me.”

“Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ [V 3.0245] jhānaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, avitakke cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘ko nu kho hetu ko paccayo, yena me avitakke cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’? Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘vitakkesu kho me ādīnavo adiṭṭho, so ca me abahulīkato, avitakke ca ānisaṃso anadhigato, so ca [P 4.0441] me anāsevito. Tasmā me avitakke cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘sace kho ahaṃ vitakkesu ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ bahulaṃ kareyyaṃ, avitakke ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseveyyaṃ, ṭhānaṃ kho panetaṃ vijjati yaṃ me avitakke cittaṃ pakkhandeyya pasīdeyya santiṭṭheyya vimucceyya etaṃ santanti passato’. So kho ahaṃ, ānanda, aparena samayena vitakkesu ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ bahulamakāsiṃ, avitakke ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseviṃ. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, avitakke cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. So kho ahaṃ, ānanda, vitakkavicārānaṃ vūpasamā…pe… dutiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja [T 4.0460] viharāmi. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, iminā vihārena viharato vitakkasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa me hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, ānanda, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa me vitakkasahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa me hoti ābādho.

“Then it occurred to me, Ānanda: ‘What if, with the stilling of applied and sustained thought… I were to enter and dwell in the second jhāna?’ But my mind did not leap into the state without applied thought, did not become confident, did not settle upon it, and was not liberated, though I saw it as ‘peace.’ Then it occurred to me, Ānanda: ‘What is the cause, what is the reason, why my mind does not leap into the state without applied thought, does not become confident, does not settle upon it, and is not liberated, though I see it as “peace”?’ Then it occurred to me, Ānanda: ‘I have not seen the danger in applied thoughts, and I have not made much of it; and I have not attained the benefit of the state without applied thought, and I have not cultivated it. That is why my mind does not leap into the state without applied thought, does not become confident, does not settle upon it, and is not liberated, though I see it as “peace.”’ Then it occurred to me, Ānanda: ‘If I were to see the danger in applied thoughts and make much of it, and if I were to attain the benefit of the state without applied thought and cultivate it, it is possible that my mind would leap into the state without applied thought, become confident, settle upon it, and be liberated, seeing it as “peace.”’ Then, Ānanda, at a later time, having seen the danger in applied thoughts, I made much of it; having attained the benefit of the state without applied thought, I cultivated it. Then my mind leaped into the state without applied thought, became confident, settled upon it, and was liberated, seeing it as ‘peace.’ Then, Ānanda, with the stilling of applied and sustained thought… I entered and dwelled in the second jhāna. But while I was dwelling thus, perceptions and attentions connected with applied thought arose in me. That was an affliction for me. Just as pain might arise for one who is happy, solely for his affliction, so too those perceptions and attentions connected with applied thought arose in me. That was an affliction for me.”

“Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ pītiyā ca virāgā upekkhako ca vihareyyaṃ sato ca sampajāno sukhañca kāyena paṭisaṃvedeyyaṃ yaṃ taṃ ariyā ācikkhanti – upekkhako satimā sukhavihārīti tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, nippītike cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘ko nu kho hetu ko paccayo[M 3.0236], yena me nippītike cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’? Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘pītiyā kho me ādīnavo adiṭṭho, so ca me abahulīkato, nippītike ca ānisaṃso anadhigato, so ca me anāsevito. Tasmā me nippītike cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati [P 4.0442] na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘sace kho ahaṃ pītiyā ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ [V 3.0246] bahulaṃ kareyyaṃ, nippītike ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseveyyaṃ, ṭhānaṃ kho panetaṃ vijjati yaṃ me nippītike cittaṃ pakkhandeyya pasīdeyya santiṭṭheyya vimucceyya etaṃ santanti passato’. So kho ahaṃ, ānanda, aparena samayena pītiyā ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ bahulamakāsiṃ, nippītike ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseviṃ. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, nippītike cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. So [T 4.0461] kho ahaṃ, ānanda, pītiyā ca virāgā…pe… tatiyaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharāmi. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, iminā vihārena viharato pītisahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa me hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, ānanda, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa me pītisahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa me hoti ābādho.

Then, Ānanda, this thought occurred to me: ‘It would be good if, with the fading away of rapture, I were to dwell equanimous, mindful, and clearly comprehending, and experience happiness with the body, entering and dwelling in the third jhāna, of which the noble ones declare: “Equanimous and mindful, one dwells happily.”’ But, Ānanda, though I saw it as peaceful, my mind did not leap toward the state without rapture, did not become confident in it, did not settle upon it, and was not liberated in it. Then, Ānanda, this thought occurred to me: ‘What is the cause, what is the reason, why my mind does not leap toward the state without rapture, does not become confident in it, does not settle upon it, and is not liberated in it, though I see it as peaceful?’ Then, Ānanda, this thought occurred to me: ‘I have not seen the danger in rapture, nor have I made much of it; I have not attained the benefit in the state without rapture, nor have I cultivated it. That is why my mind does not leap toward the state without rapture, does not become confident in it, does not settle upon it, and is not liberated in it, though I see it as peaceful.’ Then, Ānanda, this thought occurred to me: ‘If I were to see the danger in rapture and make much of it, and if I were to attain the benefit in the state without rapture and cultivate it, it is possible that my mind would leap toward the state without rapture, would become confident in it, would settle upon it, and would be liberated in it, seeing it as peaceful.’ So, Ānanda, at a later time, having seen the danger in rapture, I made much of it; having attained the benefit in the state without rapture, I cultivated it. Then, Ānanda, my mind leaps toward the state without rapture, becomes confident in it, settles upon it, and is liberated in it, seeing it as peaceful. So, Ānanda, with the fading away of rapture… I enter and dwell in the third jhāna. While I am dwelling in this state, Ānanda, perceptions and attentions accompanied by rapture arise in me. That is an affliction for me. Just as, Ānanda, pain might arise for a happy person, solely for the purpose of affliction, even so, for me, perceptions and attentions accompanied by rapture arise. That is an affliction for me.

“Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ sukhassa ca pahānā dukkhassa ca pahānā pubbeva somanassadomanassānaṃ atthaṅgamā adukkhamasukhaṃ upekkhāsatipārisuddhiṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, adukkhamasukhe cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘ko nu kho hetu ko paccayo, yena me adukkhamasukhe cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’? Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘upekkhāsukhe kho me ādīnavo adiṭṭho, so ca me abahulīkato, adukkhamasukhe ca ānisaṃso anadhigato, so ca me anāsevito. Tasmā me adukkhamasukhe cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ [T 4.0462] santanti passato’. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘sace kho ahaṃ upekkhāsukhe ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ [P 4.0443] bahulaṃ kareyyaṃ, adukkhamasukhe ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseveyyaṃ, ṭhānaṃ [M 3.0237] kho panetaṃ vijjati yaṃ me adukkhamasukhe cittaṃ pakkhandeyya pasīdeyya santiṭṭheyya vimucceyya etaṃ santanti passato’. So kho ahaṃ, ānanda, aparena samayena upekkhāsukhe ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ bahulamakāsiṃ adukkhamasukhe ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseviṃ. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, adukkhamasukhe cittaṃ pakkhandati pasīdati santiṭṭhati vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. So kho ahaṃ, ānanda, sukhassa ca pahānā…pe… [V 3.0247] catutthaṃ jhānaṃ upasampajja viharāmi. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, iminā vihārena viharato upekkhāsahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa me hoti ābādho. Seyyathāpi, ānanda, sukhino dukkhaṃ uppajjeyya yāvadeva ābādhāya; evamevassa me upekkhāsahagatā saññāmanasikārā samudācaranti. Svassa me hoti ābādho.

Then, Ānanda, this thought occurred to me: ‘It would be good if, with the abandoning of pleasure and pain, and with the previous passing away of joy and displeasure, I were to enter and dwell in the fourth jhāna, which is neither-painful-nor-pleasant and has purity of mindfulness due to equanimity.’ But, Ānanda, though I saw it as peaceful, my mind did not leap toward the neither-painful-nor-pleasant state, did not become confident in it, did not settle upon it, and was not liberated in it. Then, Ānanda, this thought occurred to me: ‘What is the cause, what is the reason, why my mind does not leap toward the neither-painful-nor-pleasant state, does not become confident in it, does not settle upon it, and is not liberated in it, though I see it as peaceful?’ Then, Ānanda, this thought occurred to me: ‘I have not seen the danger in the happiness of equanimity, nor have I made much of it; I have not attained the benefit in the neither-painful-nor-pleasant state, nor have I cultivated it. That is why my mind does not leap toward the neither-painful-nor-pleasant state, does not become confident in it, does not settle upon it, and is not liberated in it, though I see it as peaceful.’ Then, Ānanda, this thought occurred to me: ‘If I were to see the danger in the happiness of equanimity and make much of it, and if I were to attain the benefit in the neither-painful-nor-pleasant state and cultivate it, it is possible that my mind would leap toward the neither-painful-nor-pleasant state, would become confident in it, would settle upon it, and would be liberated in it, seeing it as peaceful.’ So, Ānanda, at a later time, having seen the danger in the happiness of equanimity, I made much of it; having attained the benefit in the neither-painful-nor-pleasant state, I cultivated it. Then, Ānanda, my mind leaps toward the neither-painful-nor-pleasant state, becomes confident in it, settles upon it, and is liberated in it, seeing it as peaceful. So, Ānanda, with the abandoning of pleasure and pain… I enter and dwell in the fourth jhāna. While I am dwelling in this state, Ānanda, perceptions and attentions accompanied by equanimity arise in me. That is an affliction for me. Just as, Ānanda, pain might arise for a happy person, solely for the purpose of affliction, even so, for me, perceptions and attentions accompanied by equanimity arise. That is an affliction for me.

“Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘yaṃnūnāhaṃ sabbaso rūpasaññānaṃ samatikkamā paṭighasaññānaṃ atthaṅgamā nānattasaññānaṃ amanasikārā “ananto ākāso”ti ākāsānañcāyatanaṃ upasampajja vihareyya’nti. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, ākāsānañcāyatane cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘ko nu kho hetu ko paccayo, yena [T 4.0463] me ākāsānañcāyatane cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti passato’? Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘rūpesu kho me ādīnavo adiṭṭho, so ca abahulīkato, ākāsānañcāyatane ca ānisaṃso anadhigato, so ca me anāsevito. Tasmā me ākāsānañcāyatane cittaṃ na pakkhandati nappasīdati na santiṭṭhati na vimuccati etaṃ santanti [P 4.0444] passato’. Tassa mayhaṃ, ānanda, etadahosi – ‘sace kho ahaṃ rūpesu ādīnavaṃ disvā taṃ bahulaṃ kareyyaṃ, ākāsānañcāyatane ānisaṃsaṃ adhigamma tamāseveyyaṃ, ṭhānaṃ kho panetaṃ vijjati yaṃ me ākāsānañcāyatane cittaṃ pakkhandeyya pasīdeyya santiṭṭheyya vimucceyya etaṃ santanti passato’.
